Hawaii cannabis & hemp research — AUDITED

Last audited: 1 November 2026. Cut-off: 1 November 2026. No 3 November 2026 election result is in this file. This edition contains (1) the independent audit table below and (2) the complete rewritten report with every correction applied in place. Every section of the original Parts 0–12 is kept; nothing was summarised, shortened or dropped. Only what the audit changed was changed. The federal baseline in the brief was taken as given and was not re-audited; the audit checked only that the report states it correctly and that Hawai'i's responses to it are accurate.

Audit result — read this first

152 claims audited: Confirmed 73, Corrected 19, Removed 2, Unverified 58. The counts sum exactly to the number of audit rows. 'Confirmed' means the passage was opened in a primary source in this audit or in the original's opened primary source and re-corroborated here. 'Corrected' means the claim was wrong or incomplete and the rewrite uses the corrected wording. 'Removed' means the claim was wrong and no correct version as stated could be published (BioTrack as official vendor; CannaMLS home-delivery claim). 'Unverified' means only a secondary source exists, the primary source could not be opened, or the primary text was not re-opened in this audit — the flag is kept in the rewrite, not upgraded.

Material corrections applied in the rewrite: Act 241, SLH 2015 was HB 321, not HB 302; the interisland 1-gram lab exception is deleted (patient interisland transport to a laboratory is expressly excluded); hemp cultivation setbacks are 300/100 feet, not 500 feet; Maui's surcharge ordinance is Ordinance No. 5551 (the '5511' variant is a typo); the $50,000 figure is the pre-2023 renewal figure, not the current payable amount (HAR §11-850-28 formula governs from 2023); the production-centre amending Act is Act 309, SLH 2022 (HB 2260), effective 1 July 2022; the 329V fee is $47.41 on the current DOH page ($49.50 superseded); the dispensary-licence transfer ban is qualified by HAR §11-850-8(b)/§329D-5.5; the certified-lab count for 2026 is Unavailable (not '1 lab'); and §329-43.5 fine procedure is §§706-660/706-641/706-640, not §706-640 alone.

What the audit did NOT settle: the November 2025 HAR 11-850 compilation full text; the certified-laboratory list; licence transfer/succession scope under §329D-5.5; §328G-9(m) GRAS/industrial-hemp explanation; the 1 July 2027 hemp sunset extension; HAR §11-850-28(e) market-share percentages; whether DOH 2024–2025 GET collections include the Maui surcharge; Alyas v. Lopez outcome; Hawai'i's response to the federal 12 November / 11 December 2026 hemp dates (re-check after 11 December 2026); DHHL's current position; post-Schedule III §922(g)(3)/Form 4473 treatment and DoD position; and live USDA producer, DOH processor, and DOH registrant counts. See the still-unverified list after the table.

Method limits, stated plainly: several HRS quotations in the original rest on Justia/LawServer/Cornell/PocketLaw reproductions and several DOH PDFs on search-indexed excerpts; where this audit could not re-open the capitol current text, the row is Unverified and the original flag is retained. Rate-limit and form-gated failures are disclosed, not retried into a confirmation. No personal phone number or email of an individual licence holder is reproduced.

Research / access date: 1 November 2026. Cut-off: 1 November 2026. Last audited: 1 November 2026 (this audited edition). No 3 November 2026 election result is in this file. How to read this file: Confirmed means the passage was opened in an official source. Verify means the fact could not be directly opened, rests on a search extract or secondary summary, or conflicts with another source — do not treat it as settled. Unavailable means no official source was found for it after the routes described. PROPOSED means a bill (or a resolution, where stated), not law. Every section gives its source link; the access date for all links is 1 November 2026 unless a different publication/effective date is stated in the fact itself. Part 0Part 1Part 2Part 3Part 4Part 5Part 6Part 7Part 8Part 9Part 10Part 11Part 12Research notes

Federal baseline — given, not re-researched

Split scheduling: state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714). All adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I. The broader 2024 rescheduling proposal is not final. IRS §280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity; adult-use remains fully subject to §280E. Hemp: PL 119-37 §781 takes effect 12 November 2026, but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026. After 11 December 2026 the full total-THC hemp definition and the 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply. USDA enforcement of DEA-registered lab testing for hemp is delayed to 31 December 2026. The federal medical-marijuana appropriations rider runs through 11 December 2026. There is no federal cannabis banking law.

Hawai'i application: Hawai'i's medical programme is state-licensed (HRS chapters 329 and 329D, Department of Health licences), so on the baseline as given its licensed dispensaries fall on the medical / Schedule III side, and §280E is generally lifted federally for that medical activity. Any non-medical possession in Hawai'i remains Schedule I federally, including the decriminalised ≤3-gram violation. For Hawai'i state income tax, Hawai'i had already decoupled before Schedule III — HRS §235-2.4 (subsection (v) in the 2025 text; audit note: Act 035, SLH 2026 (HB 2329) conformity may have renumbered the carve-out to (u) — cite §235-2.4 with year/Act and verify current lettering) provides §280E "shall not be operative with respect to the production and sale of medical cannabis and manufactured cannabis products by dispensaries licensed under chapter 329D and their subcontractors" — so Schedule III changes nothing for Hawai'i state income tax for medical dispensaries; GET is unaffected by §280E/Schedule III. No DOTAX Schedule III guidance was located — Verify. No Hawai'i source found in either run contradicts the federal baseline. This file does not give tax advice for any individual licensee.

Read this before quoting any sales figure. The Department of Health (DOH) Annual Report does not publish a "dispensary sales" table. Its Table 3 is General Excise Tax (GET) collected from dispensaries. The series $99,871 (2017) … $2,937,877 (2024) … $2,828,019 (2025), cumulative $17,247,813, is tax, not sales. Any secondary source quoting those figures as "sales" is wrong. BioTrack sales are a separate series, published in full only via the state-commissioned CPPC analysis (Part 6), and CPPC figures are hypothetical adult-use projections, NOT current sales — always cite the CPPC version.

Jurisdiction note

Hawai'i has no municipal governments below county level; the City and County of Honolulu covers all of O'ahu; Kalawao County is administered by the state. Distinguish same-name places: Kailua, O'ahu vs Kailua-Kona, Hawai'i Island; Waimea, Kaua'i vs Waimea, Hawai'i Island (and likewise Pearl City, Kapolei, Kahului, Wailuku, Lahaina, Hilo, Līhu'e, Kāne'ohe as given in the commission — do not merge same-name locations in different counties).

Headline findings — complete Parts 0–12

  • Medical-only. No adult-use law enacted in 2025 or 2026; no new cannabis authority enacted; regulator remains DOH OMCCR. 8 dispensary licences (3 Honolulu / 2 Hawai'i / 2 Maui / 1 Kaua'i / 0 Kalawao), no new licences since 29 April 2016; 10 production centres + 24 retail locations = 34 facilities at 23 December 2025. Patients 29,315 at 31 August 2026; 329V visitors 823 current + 298 pending at the same date.
  • Act 241, SLH 2025 (HB 302) is medical expansion, not adult-use and not a new authority; its Part IV future cultivator licence is corroborated at 1 January 2028 (enrolled-line Verify retained) and is NOT in effect on 1 November 2026. Always cite the year: Act 241 SLH 2015 = dispensary system; Act 241 SLH 2025 = HB 302. Likewise Act 228 SLH 2000 = medical use; Act 228 SLH 2016 = hemp pilot.
  • No cannabis-specific excise tax. Medical cannabis is taxed under GET (HRS ch 237) on the business's gross receipts, not as a sales tax: retail 4% + 0.5% county surcharge = 4.5% in every county as of 2026, including Maui from 1 January 2024.
  • Hemp: strict, enforced, and sunsetting on paper. No USDA-approved state plan — growers licence directly through USDA. DOH processor permit $500 + online fee, 1-year term (HAR §11-37-44(a), confirmed). Distributor/retailer Certificate of Registration $50 for 5 years from 1 January 2026, enforcement from 1 July 2026 (Act 269, SLH 2025). HRS chapter 328G and chapter 141, Part III are noted as repealed 1 July 2027 (L 2023, c 263, §15) — extension Verify. Product caps: total THC ≤0.3%; edibles 1 mg/serving, 5 mg/package; beverages 0.5 mg/container; tinctures 3 mg/serving, max 1 oz container (85 mg/bottle is DOH guidance only, derivation Verify). Smokable hemp banned; artificially derived cannabinoids banned.
  • Part 8 headline: ZERO properties meet the strict verified standard. Only two marketed-but-unverified candidates: 248 Piliwale Rd, Kula, Maui (MLS $2,900,000 Active, refreshed 31 October 2026, vs $5,999,000 search-indexed figure — conflict flagged) and a Honolulu hemp/CBD retail chain (DealStream $5,000,000, no street addresses). Neither is presented as verified cannabis-zoned. A dispensary licence itself is not freely transferable: §329D-3 states it shall not be sold or otherwise transferred, but HAR §11-850-8(b) qualifies this — except with prior written DOH approval following notice under §329D-5.5 (scope of §329D-5.5 remains Verify).
  • Part 9 headline: no new authority; transfer gap resolved; no initiative; no qualifying general poll. HB 668 = Act 177, SLH 2013, transfer effective 1 January 2015. Statewide citizen initiative CONFIRMED ABSENT (Haw. Const. art. XVII, §1). Only county cannabis ballot question located: Hawai'i County November 2008 LLEP, invalidated in Ruggles v. Yagong (2015). No general-population poll meets the pollster/date/sample standard (PRP Hawaii Perspectives 58%, late/December 2023, lacks sample/methodology; a conflicting 52%/31% figure lacks pollster/date/sample entirely; the Dual Use rapid survey is a self-selected patient/provider sample, not the general public).
  • Part 10 headline: no federally recognised tribes in Hawai'i (BIA 91 FR 4102, 30 January 2026, 575 entities + EPA Region 9) — do not soften. SB 444 (2024) NOT enacted. Patient interisland transport prohibited by any means for any purpose (§329-122(f)), with no patient 1-gram lab-testing exception for interisland carriage (audit-corrected; the 1-gram route is intra-island only). TSA page reported exactly; cruise-line policies are secondary/dated only. Hemp-shipment federal 0.4 mg/container tension after 11 December 2026 is unresolved.
  • Part 11 headline: testing, fees, and security are rule-heavy; several lists are still missing. Full contaminant limits, batch-size and reserve-sample rules, remediation and recall rules are stated from HAR 11-850 (December 2024 compilation). Dispensary renewal fees are reconciled (former $50,000 renewal figure — pre-2023 text, struck/replaced in the 2022–2023 fee changes — vs HAR §11-850-28 per-site formula; the $50,000 figure is NOT the current payable amount). But the certified-laboratory list, licence transfer/succession process, insurance/bonding minimum, indoor/outdoor/greenhouse method, video-retention period, and sample/discount/loyalty rules remain Unavailable/Verify, and November 2025 HAR 11-850 changes are DOH-summary only. BioTrack/BT Government remains SECONDARY ONLY — never published here as the official seed-to-sale vendor.
  • Part 12 headline: paraphernalia, driving, and expungement clarified on statute text. §329-43.5: simple paraphernalia possession/use and delivery = violation, fine ≤$500 (Act 72, SLH 2017); delivery to an under-18 at least 3 years younger = class B felony; paraphernalia advertising-related violation of §329-43.5 = class C felony (fine/imprisonment procedure §§706-660/706-641/706-640 — audit-corrected; the bare §706-640 citation in the original is incomplete). NO per se THC driving limit (§291E-61(a) impairment-based). Act 273 expungement is PETITION/MOTION-based, not automatic (§706-622.5(5)). §329-125.5(a) landlord/school wording, §521-39, firearms, pet/veterinary, and on-campus school rules remain Verify/Unavailable beyond the anchors; no legal advice is given on firearms.

Key remaining Verify items (named on the cover, full list in the combined research notes): November 2025 HAR 11-850 compilation full text; certified-laboratory list; licence transfer/succession route; 329V fee resolved in audit: $47.41 (DOH travel page application fee); §328G-9(m) statute-vs-FAQ exception conflict; 1 July 2027 hemp sunset extension; production-centre amending Act: Act 309, SLH 2022 (HB 2260), effective 1 July 2022 (audit-resolved); Pono Life Maui retail drop (closure vs omission); HAR §11-850-28(e) market-share discount percentages; Maui GET surcharge ordinance number (resolved in audit: Ordinance No. 5551) and whether DOH 2024–2025 collections include it; Alyas v. Lopez outcome; Hawaii response to federal 12 November / 11 December 2026 hemp dates (re-check after 11 December 2026); DHHL current position; post-Schedule III §922(g)(3)/Form 4473 treatment and DoD position; and live USDA producer, DOH processor, and DOH registrant counts.

Method limits — stated plainly. (1) Several DOH PDF-derived facts in Parts 2, 3, and 6 rest on search-indexed excerpts because direct PDF opens failed transiently in Run 1 (the Parts 4–5 pass DID open the DOH January 2026 annual report PDF and confirms the GET and facility totals) — re-open those PDFs and re-verify HRS quotes against capitol.hawaii.gov before publication-grade use. (2) HAR 11-850 quotations in Part 11 are from the Cornell LII 6 December 2024 compilation; the November 2025 compilation effective 17 November 2025 was confirmed to exist on the DOH page but its PDF fetch returned an empty response and was not repeated, so November 2025 changes are DOH news-release summary only. (3) Many Run 1 HRS quotes rest on Justia/LawServer/Cornell/PocketLaw reproductions, and Part 12's §329-43.5 quote rests on a 2021 Justia compilation — re-verify against capitol.hawaii.gov. (4) Rate-limit failures are disclosed, not retried: Cornell §§11-850-52, 53, 32, 145, 27 (429) in Part 11–12, and two HAR compilation re-opens (429) in Part 7–8. (5) PROPOSED law is labelled PROPOSED everywhere it appears and is never presented as current law.

PART 0 — HEADLINES

0.1 Bottom line

Hawai‘i is medical-only. Adult-use cannabis has not been legalised. Possession of 3 grams or less by a non-patient is a non-criminal violation with a $130 fine; above that, criminal “promoting a detrimental drug” penalties apply (Part 1.3). Only registered in-state patients, their primary caregivers, and registered qualifying out-of-state patients may buy from the eight licensed medical cannabis dispensary companies. Source: DOH, Laws and Rules — Medical Cannabis Registry Program, health.hawaii.gov — Laws and Rules (accessed 1 Nov 2026); Act 273, SLH 2019 (HB 1383), became law without Governor’s signature 10 July 2019, Gov. Msg. No. 1393, effective 11 January 2020 — secondary summary LegiScan HB 1383 (accessed 1 Nov 2026). Verify the exact current HRS §712-1249 subsection lettering against capitol.hawaii.gov; the Act 273 session-law PDF itself was not opened in the Part 0–1 pass.

0.2 The biggest recent change: Act 241, SLH 2025 (HB 302) — medical, not adult-use, not a new authority

Signed 27 June 2025, effective 1 July 2025 (Part IV effective 1 January 2028 — see below). Official source opened: Act 241, SLH 2025 (HB 302 CD1) session law PDF; signing message GM 1343 (accessed 1 Nov 2026). Governor’s final veto decisions release: Governor — final veto decisions (accessed 1 Nov 2026).

  • Qualification widened: a person may now qualify if (a) certified for a debilitating medical condition, or (b) diagnosed with a condition other than a debilitating condition by their primary treating medical provider who certifies benefit likely outweighs risk, or (c) receiving hospice care certified by a hospice provider. Verbatim text in Part 1.4. Route (b) is not “any provider may certify any condition on a first visit” — the certifier must be primarily responsible for treatment and ongoing care.
  • Telehealth: the provider–patient relationship may be established by telehealth for written certifications; provider certification fee capped at three times the DOH registration-certificate fee.
  • DOH record inspection: DOH may inspect a qualifying patient’s medical records held by the certifying physician, APRN or hospice provider, with suspension/revocation of certification authority for refusal or non-compliance — the provision Governor Green had put on his intent-to-veto list as “a grave violation of privacy” before signing.
  • Retail sales widened: retail dispensing locations may sell hemp products and accessories for medical use (rolling papers, trays, grinders, vaporizers, etc.), except in the waiting room.
  • Dispensary-to-dispensary transport clarified: 800 ounces per 30 days, or other amount with prior DOH approval; manifest to DOH before sale, after which transfer may proceed immediately.
  • New criminal penalties: class C felony for operating a dispensary without a licence; misdemeanour for operating a search platform / web-hosting / social-media service that posts advertising for an unlicensed seller, subject to a prior DOH cease-and-desist notice and a reasonable-reliance affirmative defence. Hemp processors/retailers with a valid chapter 328G permit and registered primary caregivers acting within registration are excluded.
  • Nuisance enforcement funded: $750,000 in FY 2025–2026 and the same in FY 2026–2027 from the Medical Cannabis Registry and Regulation Special Fund to the Attorney General’s Drug Nuisance Abatement Unit (4.0 FTE investigator V, 1.0 FTE investigator IV, 1.0 FTE analyst).
  • Future cultivator licence — NOT in effect 1 Nov 2026: Part IV, effective 1 January 2028, makes it unlawful to cultivate cannabis without a DOH cannabis cultivator licence, authorising acquisition/cultivation of plants, seeds, cuttings or clones and distribution of plants and flower to a medical dispensary, with no more than one cultivator licence per person. Do not present this as current law.

Do not confuse the two Act 241s: Act 241, SLH 2015 created the dispensary system (HRS chapter 329D). Act 241, SLH 2025 is HB 302 above. Always cite the year.

0.3 No new cannabis authority; no programme transfer in 2025–2026

No Hawai‘i Cannabis Authority, Cannabis and Hemp Office, or Cannabis and Hemp Control Board has been enacted. The regulator remains the DOH Office of Medical Cannabis Control and Regulation (OMCCR), for both the registry (HRS 329 Part IX) and dispensaries (HRS 329D), with hemp processing / manufactured hemp products under HRS chapter 328G and HAR chapter 11-37, also administered through OMCCR. Proposals HB 1246 / SB 1613 (2025), SB 2421 / HB 1625 (2026) and SB 3335 (2024) were not enacted (Part 1.10). DOH registry home page: health.hawaii.gov — Medical Cannabis Registry Program (accessed 1 Nov 2026), which states OMCCR “will roll out a new patient registry portal” on 30 September 2026.

0.4 Adult-use status in 2026: Senate moved, House blocked, nothing enacted

  • No adult-use bill was enacted in 2025 or 2026.
  • 2026 Senate low-dose bill SB 3275 SD1 (no more than 5 mg THC per serving; 5 mg per 12 ounces in liquid form) passed Senate Health and Human Services (18 Feb 2026) and Commerce and Consumer Protection (19 Feb 2026), passed Second Reading 20 February 2026 and was referred to Ways and Means and Judiciary. No later capitol action was found in sources reviewed; secondary reporting (Marijuana Moment, February 2026; Cannabis Business Times, February 2026) describes it as stalled / dead for the year. Status: NOT ENACTED. Final capitol disposition — Verify.
  • Broad contingent bill SB 2421 (companion HB 1625) — Hawai‘i Cannabis and Hemp Office in DCCA, adult use only upon specified federal change or a state constitutional amendment — was deferred 18 February 2026 by Senate CPN and HHS. Official measure page: capitol.hawaii.gov — SB 2421 (accessed 1 Nov 2026).
  • Constitutional-amendment route HB 1624 / SB 2420 (21+ personal use from 1 July 2027, Legislature to enact governing laws) was referred in January 2026 (HB 1624 to JHA/CPC, FIN, 26 Jan 2026) with no hearing/action shown. Official measure page: capitol.hawaii.gov — HB 1624 (accessed 1 Nov 2026). No adult-use question was placed on the November 2026 ballot by these measures in sources reviewed.
  • Hawai‘i has no statewide citizen initiative, so there is no ballot route around the Legislature. Verify against Hawai‘i Constitution, art. XVII, in Run 2 if a formal citation is required.
  • Historical pattern only: the Senate passed broad adult-use bills in 2023 (SB 669) and 2024; the House did not pass them. Governor Green has said he would sign a well-regulated adult-use bill, but that is not law.

0.5 Patient numbers — latest official month-end figures

Source opened directly: DOH Medical Cannabis Registry Program statistics page, health.hawaii.gov — Program Statistics (accessed 1 Nov 2026). Counts are valid registrations at month-end, not cumulative ever-registered. DOH states the programme issues roughly 1,500 329 Cards each month and month-end counts are posted around the 15th of the following month.

August 2026 detail (DOH August 2026 report, via its PDF linked from that page; PDF detail summarised in search results in the Part 6 pass, so treat detail as secondary-reported DOH data — Verify against the PDF): 484 new and 1,151 renewal applications issued 1–31 Aug 2026; 2,342 caregivers; 248 physicians/APRNs had certified one or more currently registered patients as of 31 Aug 2026; most common condition severe pain 24,262 (82.76%), PTSD 5,523 (18.84%) — percentages do not sum to 100% because patients may report more than one condition. Context from Act 241 itself: registrations peaked at 35,444 in August 2021, falling to 30,035 by November 2024 (over 15% decline), cited by the Legislature as evidence patients were shifting to illicit sources. Conflict flagged: a secondary summary of the DOH July 2025 report calls 34,037 in May 2023 “the program’s high” — that cannot be the all-time high if 35,444 in August 2021 stands; treat the May 2023 “high” claim as Verify / likely erroneous. A secondary compilation also gives 29,820 for February 2026; the DOH statistics page figure 28,825 above is adopted as the more authoritative, directly opened source — the 29,820 figure coincides with February 2025 and is Verify, not adopted.

Out-of-state patients, August 2026 (secondary reporting of the DOH August 2026 report, Marijuana Herald, 29 Sep 2026, Marijuana Herald — out-of-state patients 2026, accessed 1 Nov 2026 — Verify against the DOH PDF): 3,792 out-of-state patients registered 1 Jan–31 Aug 2026 (vs 3,305 same period 2025, +~14.7%); 823 currently registered as of 31 Aug 2026, from 38 states/territories/DC, plus 298 approved registrations scheduled to become active after August. Florida was the most common home state (135, 16.4%). Do not confuse the separate “out-of-state address” row (99) in the in-state county table (Part 6.2) with the 329V visitor count.

0.6 Registry modernisation: new portal 30 September 2026; cards electronic only

OMCCR launched a new patient registry portal on 30 September 2026, replacing medmj.ehawaii.gov. Existing credentials carried over; patients must log in to download a new 329 card. The old system was largely unavailable 23–29 September 2026 (existing-card viewing only; no new applications, renewals or updates; applications submitted by 22 September continued to be processed; dispensary access unaffected). Source: DOH registry home page (accessed 1 Nov 2026; states 3–5 business-day processing aim); secondary confirmation 29 Sep 2026, Marijuana Herald — new portal (accessed 1 Nov 2026). No physical card is mailed — cards are electronic/downloadable. DOH in-state application page: health.hawaii.gov — application information (accessed 1 Nov 2026 via search result).

0.7 Dispensary licences: no new licences added by Act 241 (2025)

Statutory allocation in HRS §329D-2 remains eight dispensary licences statewide: 3 City and County of Honolulu, 2 County of Hawai‘i, 2 County of Maui, 1 County of Kaua‘i, 0 Kalawao. Each licence allows up to two retail dispensing locations as of right, plus up to two additional locations if DOH authorises them for a rural/underserved area (Part 4). Production-centre cap: the current HAR / DOH 2026 report position adopted in Part 4 is up to three production centres per licence, 5,000 plants base plus up to 2,500 additional at DOH’s determination, no more than 7,500 at a single centre and 15,000 per licensee — Verify the amending Act Act 309, SLH 2022 (HB 2260), effective 1 July 2022 (audit-resolved), because older reproductions still read “two centres / 3,000 plants” (2015 Act 241 figures, pre-dating Act 309, SLH 2022). DOH January 2025 annual report: DOH Annual Report Jan 2025 (PDF) (accessed 1 Nov 2026), confirming the 3/2/2/1 allocation and recording 75 licensing violations 1 Jan–1 Dec 2024, most commonly HAR §11-850-143 (labelling for retail sale). No official source opened in this run shows DOH opening a new dispensary-licence window; secondary summaries state applications are not currently accepted (last window January 2016) — treat “no open window” as Verify against OMCCR.

0.8 Interisland transport: patients and caregivers — NO, by any means

Unchanged by Act 241 (2025). Verbatim HRS §329-122(f), final paragraph (reproduction: LawServer — HRS §329-122, accessed 1 Nov 2026; verify against capitol.hawaii.gov):

“For purposes of interisland transportation, ‘transport’ of cannabis, usable cannabis, or any manufactured cannabis product, by any means is allowable only between dispensaries to the extent authorized by section 329D-6(r) and between a production center or retail dispensing location and a certified laboratory for the sole purpose of laboratory testing pursuant to section 329D-8, as permitted under section 329D-6(m) and subject to section 329D-6(j), and with the understanding that state law and its protections do not apply outside of the jurisdictional limits of the State. Allowable transport pursuant to this section does not include interisland transportation by any means or for any purpose between a qualifying patient, primary caregiver, qualifying out-of-state patient, or caregiver of a qualifying out-of-state patient and any other entity or individual, including an individual who is a qualifying patient, primary caregiver, qualifying out-of-state patient, or caregiver of a qualifying out-of-state patient.”

Practical consequence: a patient travelling interisland must buy on the island where they are present, from a licensed dispensary there; if the destination island has no dispensary, the only lawful in-state route is home cultivation on-island by the patient or registered caregiver. Air travel also crosses federal jurisdiction (airports, federal airspace), where federal law applies. Full detail in Part 1.5.

0.9 Hemp and intoxicating hemp: strict product regime, now enforced — and sued over

Hemp processing law: HRS chapter 328G (originally chapter 328H in Act 14, SLH 2020; renumbered — see Part 1.2), plus HAR chapter 11-37 (Interim Rules), amended and compiled 28 January 2026 (PDF opened directly: HAR 11-37 compilation, 28 Jan 2026 (PDF), accessed 1 Nov 2026).

  • Only “manufactured hemp products” may be sold to consumers, in oral or topical form only: tablets, capsules, powders (sealed single-serving packets), softgels, gelcaps, oil-based tinctures, gummies, beverages (6–12 fl oz), and topicals. Smokable hemp is banned: hemp flower/leaf, pre-rolls, viable seeds (except to a USDA-licensed producer or permitted processor), vape cartridges/concentrates purporting to contain cannabinoids, inhalers/vape pens for hemp cannabinoids, and foods purporting to contain cannabinoids (chocolate, honey, baked goods, candies, etc.) are prohibited. The smokable ban predates Act 269 — Act 14 (2020) already prohibited hemp leaf/floral material intended to be smoked/inhaled and cannabinoid products for aerosolized delivery; Act 269 created registration and expanded inspection/seizure. Do not date the ban to 2025/2026.
  • Artificially derived / synthetic cannabinoids are banned, including delta-8 THC, delta-10 THC, THCO, HHC, THC-P, THC-H, THCjd, HHCO and CBN created from another hemp-derived cannabinoid such as CBD (or via yeast/algae). Naturally occurring cannabinoids separated by extraction, and cannabinoids produced by decarboxylation without a chemical catalyst, are excluded. A business bears the burden of substantiating a natural route.
  • THC caps (HAR §§11-37-4/5, Jan 2026 compilation): total THC ≤0.3% by weight in all cases; plus mg caps — edibles other than tinctures/beverages: 1 mg total THC per serving, 5 mg per package; beverages: 0.5 mg per container; tinctures: max 1 oz per container, 3 mg per serving, and DOH consumer guidance states up to 85 mg per 1-oz bottle (3 mg × ~28 servings — the 85 mg figure is DOH guidance, not the rule text excerpted; Verify the derivation in the full rule). No CBD mg limit has been set by Hawai‘i.
  • Registration from 1 January 2026 (Act 269, SLH 2025, HB 1482, signed 2 July 2025, Gov. Msg. No. 1372): every manufactured-hemp-product distributor and retailer selling to Hawai‘i customers — including online and out-of-state sellers — must hold a DOH Certificate of Registration, $50 for five years, displayed at the business location and on the website if applicable. Tinctures, topicals, GRAS products and industrial hemp are excepted from registration in DOH consumer guidance, but tincture sales are restricted to persons 21+ with ID check (HRS §328G-13; HAR §11-37-15). DOH outreach release, 4 Nov 2025: DOH — hemp retail registration outreach (4 Nov 2025) (accessed 1 Nov 2026).
  • Enforcement from 1 July 2026: DOH/AG grace period ended 30 June 2026. Penalties up to $10,000 per separate offence (HRS §328G-6), plus embargo, seizure and destruction, and nuisance abatement. Secondary reporting (Marijuana Moment, 1 July 2026; Reason, 25 June 2026; High Times, 2026) states fewer than / about 50 businesses had registered at enforcement opening — secondary, not a DOH count.
  • Federal lawsuit (secondary, outcome Verify): Alyas et al. v. Lopez et al., No. 1:26-cv-00035-JAO-WRP (D. Hawai‘i), retailers challenging registration and product rules under the Supremacy, Dormant Commerce and Due Process Clauses; preliminary-injunction and State motion-to-dismiss heard 2 July 2026 and taken under advisement. No ruling was located in opened sources — outcome is Verify / Unavailable. Audit note: a secondary report of a 4 September 2026 dismissal was found by the auditor, but no court order or docket was opened, so it is NOT adopted here. Do not state that enforcement has been enjoined.
  • THCA: counted in Hawai‘i’s “total THC” (THCA × 0.877 + delta-9 THC + delta-8 THC, HAR §11-37-2). THCA flower sold as smokable hemp is prohibited as hemp flower/leaf regardless of labelled THCA, and any manufactured product above 0.3% total THC is prohibited. There is no separate lawful “THCA flower” retail category in Hawai‘i.

0.10 Federal baseline applied — and what Hawai‘i has NOT done about December 2026

No Hawai‘i statute, rule or DOH guidance responding to PL 119-37 §781 / PL 119-103 §2019 was located as of 1 November 2026. That matters because Hawai‘i’s existing mg caps do not align with the forthcoming federal cap as described in the baseline (0.4 mg total THC per container after 11 December 2026): Hawai‘i currently allows 5 mg per edible package, 0.5 mg per beverage container, and up to 85 mg per tincture bottle. Whether Hawai‘i will conform, seek an exemption, or leave its patchwork in place is Unavailable as of the cut-off. Do not present Hawai‘i as already compliant with the December 2026 federal definition. No Hawai‘i state tax-conformity change for §280E was located in the Part 0–1 pass (Part 2 shows Hawai‘i already decoupled for state income tax — see Part 2.7 — but whether Hawai‘i’s law automatically follows the federal Schedule III change or requires separate action is Verify against DOTAX guidance).

0.11 Enforcement headlines beyond hemp

Act 241 (2025) Part II (unlicensed dispensary felony / platform misdemeanour) and Part III ($750,000/year AG nuisance funding) — see §0.2. Act 46, SLH 2025 (SB 1429, signed 14 May 2025, Gov. Msg. No. 1146): DOH may impose administrative penalties and take enforcement action for medical-cannabis violations; caregiver cap raised from 1 to 5 patients; caregiver cultivation prohibition repealed retroactive to 1 January 2025 (Part 1.4); interim-rule sunset extended two years and civil-service exemption extended four years. Source: DOH Laws and Rules page summary; BillTrack summary BillTrack — SB 1429 (accessed 1 Nov 2026). The $5,000-per-day penalty figure in the BillTrack summary was not verified against the Act 46 session-law text — Verify. HAR chapter 11-850 (Interim Rules) for dispensaries was amended 17 November 2025 (DOH release opened directly: DOH — HAR 11-850 update, 17 Nov 2025, accessed 1 Nov 2026): permitted sale of vaporizers, grinders, storage containers, rolling papers, filters and cones; inhalable oil extracts/concentrates without nicotine, hemp/hemp-derived cannabinoids, tobacco or other non-cannabis substances; caregiver may accompany patient in waiting room (no storage/display/sale in waiting room); advertising ban on depictions of use, children, celebrities/influencers, child-appealing cartoons, or food/candy-resembling products; no provider certification visits (in-person or telehealth) at dispensary facilities; dispensary-to-dispensary sales — 30-day DOH review removed (Act 172, 2024), manifests before sale and completed manifests within one week, direct transport of retail-ready product to the purchasing dispensary’s retail location, and clarification of the 800-ounce/30-day limit and petition process.

Run 2 reconciliation — corrections and updates to Parts 0–6 (read with the verbatim text above; the original Run 1 wording is preserved, these are the Run 2 positions)

  • Hemp cultivation setbacks — CORRECTION to Run 1. Run 1 stated 500 feet from the 2020 Act 14 text. Current HRS §141-42 text as opened in Part 7 gives 300 feet from pre-existing real property comprising a playground, childcare facility, or school, and 100 feet from any pre-existing house, dwelling unit, residential apartment, or other residential structure not owned or controlled by the licence holder (with pilot-programme grandfathering before 27 August 2020). Present 300/100 as current. Whether 500 feet was ever enacted text or a secondary error is Verify against the Act 14 enrolled text. Keep setbacks distinct: medical 750-foot school/playground buffer (HRS §329D-22); hemp processor 500-foot rule (HAR §11-37-51(a)); hemp cultivation 300/100 feet (§141-42).
  • §328G-9(m) exception list — CONFLICT, statute controls. The statute lists only (1) topically applied products and (2) tinctures (21+). The DOH FAQ (current as of 16 December 2025) lists tinctures, topicals, GRAS products, and industrial hemp. Do not merge the lists. The possible explanation — GRAS/industrial products fall outside §328G-8 applicability or the manufactured-hemp-product definition — is Verify against §§328G-1 and 328G-8. Run 1 passages repeating the broader DOH FAQ list are therefore superseded on this point by the statute text in Part 7.
  • HRS chapter 328G and chapter 141, Part III repeal 1 July 2027 — RESOLVED as a current statutory note (L 2023, c 263, §15; Act 263 = HB 1359, H.D. 2, S.D. 1, became law without the Governor's signature 11 July 2023). This supersedes Run 1's 30 June 2022 repeal note for §141-42. Extension beyond 1 July 2027 or permanence was not located — Verify. HAR 11-37 interim-rule sunset under §328G-4(b) runs to 1 July 2027 or earlier permanent-rule adoption — substantially resolved; §328G-4(a) "shall" vs "may" wording is Verify.
  • Processor fee $500 — CONFIRMED. HAR §11-37-44(a): $500 plus an online application processing fee; 1-year term, non-transferable (§11-37-46); renewal application at least 60 days prior to expiration (§11-37-43(a)).
  • Tincture 85 mg/bottle — NOT resolved as a rule figure. HAR rule is max 1 oz container, 3 mg per serving. 85 mg is DOH guidance only; the derivation (3 mg × ~28 servings) is Verify because no servings-per-bottle definition was located in rule text.
  • Universal 21+ vs tincture-only sale ban — SUBSTANTIALLY RESOLVED in favour of tincture-only as a statutory sale ban (HRS §328G-13, Act 269). "Not for sale to persons under 21" on manufactured hemp products generally is a labelling statement, not a universal statutory sale ban on evidence located. HB 1109 HD1 (2025) and HB 2613 (2026) are PROPOSED only.
  • Act 241, SLH 2025 Part IV future cultivator licence — corroborated date 1 January 2028, enrolled-line Verify retained. Run 1 Part 1 said 1 January 2028; Run 1 Parts 4–5 summaries carried a 1 January 2027 variant. Part 9 corroborates 1 January 2028 from the CD1 summary, but the enrolled Act text line was not opened — present 1 January 2028 as the corroborated date with the Verify flag retained. It is NOT in effect on 1 November 2026.
  • Act 228 collision — always cite the year. Act 228, SLH 2000 = medical use; Act 228, SLH 2016 = hemp pilot. Act 241 collision likewise: Act 241, SLH 2015 = dispensary system; Act 241, SLH 2025 (HB 302) = medical amendments.
  • Programme transfer gap — RESOLVED (Part 9). HB 668 = Act 177, SLH 2013 (enacted June 2013), transfer effective 1 January 2015. Correct any "transferred in 2013" shorthand. DOH administration from 1 January 2015 stands.
  • Statewide citizen initiative — CONFIRMED ABSENT (Part 9). Hawai'i Constitution art. XVII, §1: amendments "may be proposed by constitutional convention or by the legislature". Run 1's "Verify against art. XVII" is now resolved. The only county cannabis ballot question located is Hawai'i County November 2008 LLEP, invalidated in Ruggles v. Yagong, 135 Haw. 411 (2015) — do NOT present LLEP as current law; vote margin Unavailable.
  • 329V fee — RESOLVED in audit. Current DOH travel page states $47.41 non-refundable application fee; the earlier secondary figure $47.41 [audit-corrected application fee] is superseded and is retired as the stated fee (re-check the live portal checkout total before republication). All other 329V anchors stay: 823 current + 298 pending at 31 August 2026; 3,792 January–August 2026; max two 60-day terms per calendar year; no cultivation; 4 oz/15 days, 8 oz/30 days statewide; out-of-state-address row 99 is NOT the 329V count; visitor sales dollars Unavailable (never derive a share from 823 ÷ 29,315).
  • Dispensary renewal fees — reconciled (Part 11). The $50,000 figure was the pre-2023 renewal figure (2022 §329D-4 text); the payable amount from 2023 is the HAR §11-850-28 per-site formula. Whether a $50,000 cap survives in current §329D-7 as amended by HB 1082 HD2 (2023) is Verify against current capitol text — do not quote $50,000 as the current payable amount. Payable amount is the HAR §11-850-28 per-site formula (production-centre base fees by plant cap/manufacturing type; $20,000 per retail; county discounts O'ahu 0% / Hawai'i 5% / Maui 5% / Kaua'i 10%; market-share discount percentages truncated — Verify). Totals collected: 2016 $600,000; 2017–2022 $400,000/yr; 2023 $885,250; 2024 $961,250; 2025 $915,250; cumulative $5,761,750.
  • Production centres — current figures confirmed (Part 11). 3 centres / 5,000 + 2,500 discretionary = 7,500 per centre / 15,000 per licensee (HAR §11-850-6 + current §329D-2(f)). Amending Act resolved in audit: Act 309, SLH 2022 (HB 2260), effective 1 July 2022. Older 2-centre / 3,000-plant figures are 2015 figures — never present as current.
  • Facilities, patients, GET, CPPC — Run 1 figures stand, with flags carried forward. Facilities 10 production + 24 retail = 34 at 23 December 2025 (was 13+25=38 December 2024); Pono Life Maui retail drop (4 sites in 2024 incl. Kahului/Pā'ia → 2 in December 2025, Kīhei + Lahaina) is Verify — closure vs table omission unresolved; theoretical maxima (24 production / 32 retail) are never operating counts. Patients 29,315 at 31 August 2026; September 2026 29,142 index figure only; February 2026 adopted 28,825 vs secondary 29,820 (the February 2025 figure, Verify); peak adopted 35,444 August 2021. GET is tax, never sales (series and cumulative $17,247,813 in Part 2/Part 6); retail GET 4% + 0.5% surcharge = 4.5% all counties incl. Maui from 1 January 2024; Maui ordinance number is Ordinance No. 5551 (Bill 49, 2023) — resolved in audit; whether DOH 2024–2025 collections include Maui surcharge is Verify. CPPC figures are hypothetical adult-use projections, NOT current sales — always cite the CPPC version (December 2025 original vs February/March 2026 update tourist figures differ); the $73m 2025 figure is an MJBiz Factbook projection, not actual sales.

PART 1 — LAWS

Method note: “Last amended” is the last amendment date stated in the source opened or, where the source is a reproduction, the amendment history printed in that reproduction. Where the Legislature’s current consolidated text was not directly opened, that is stated. Do not treat a bill draft as current law. Most HRS section text in this Part was verified through DOH pages, session-law PDFs, and reputable reproductions (Justia, LawServer, Cornell LII, PocketLaw) — re-verify every quote against capitol.hawaii.gov for the Run 2 audit (see gaps).

1.1 Legal status — medical only; what is and is not legal for non-patients

Legal for registered persons only: a qualifying patient, primary caregiver, qualifying out-of-state patient, or caregiver of a qualifying out-of-state patient, validly registered with DOH, may acquire, possess, cultivate (in-state patients/caregivers only, at a registered grow site), use, and transport (subject to §1.5) cannabis for medical use, within an “adequate supply,” and may buy from a licensed dispensary within dispensing limits (§§1.3–1.4). Hemp that meets the federal/state definition and manufactured hemp products that meet HAR chapter 11-37 may be sold to the general public (subject to age and registration rules in §1.6). This is not adult-use legalisation: hemp products are capped at 0.3% total THC and at the mg caps in §1.6.

Not legal for non-patients: adult-use possession, sale, distribution, cultivation, and public use of cannabis are not legal. Possession of ≤3 grams is decriminalised to a $130 violation (not legalisation); possession above 3 grams, sale/barter in any amount, and cultivation at scale remain criminal (§1.3). No adult may buy at a dispensary without a valid 329 card (in-state) or 329 out-of-state registration. Dispensaries may not deliver off-premises (HRS §329D-6(n)). Smoking/vaping cannabis in public, in a vehicle, at work, on school grounds, at parks/beaches/recreation centres, or any other place open to the public is prohibited for patients (§329-122(e)); HRS chapter 328J (Smoke-Free Law) is listed by DOH as directly applicable to smoking/vaping cannabis. Bringing cannabis into or out of Hawai‘i by air or sea, including interisland by air, is outside state-law protection (§1.5). Federal law is not displaced by a 329 card.

1.2 Every statute and regulation

A. HRS chapter 329, Part IX — Medical Use of Cannabis (§§329-121 to 329-131)

Origin: Act 228, SLH 2000 (SB 862). Hawai‘i was the first state to legalise medical cannabis by legislature rather than ballot initiative — DOH Laws and Rules page, health.hawaii.gov — Laws and Rules (accessed 1 Nov 2026). Summary: creates the registry, defines qualifying conditions, adequate supply, certifying providers, registration, protections, and conditions/prohibited places of use. Administered by DOH since 1 January 2015 (DOH statistics page: “On January 1, 2015, when DOH began administration of the program, there were 11,402 [estimated] registered patients”). Key later amendments (from DOH Laws and Rules page and Act texts): Act 178, SLH 2013 (adequate supply / usable amount changes, fee $25→$35); Act 242, SLH 2015 (non-discrimination, §329-125.5); Act 170, SLH 2017 (terminology “marijuana”→“cannabis” throughout Part IX, chapter 329D and related sections, complete by 31 Dec 2019); Act 116, SLH 2018 (certification validity, safe pulmonary administration products, out-of-state programme, parent/guardian caregivers, telehealth continuation after face-to-face establishment); Act 41, SLH 2017; Act 108, SLH 2023 (3-year certification, waiting-room definition, dispensary-to-dispensary rulemaking, signage/packaging/security); Act 46, SLH 2025; Act 241, SLH 2025. Current consolidated text: DOH links to the Legislature via the Laws and Rules page above; DOH itself warns: “Any NEW legislative action, or actions that change existing laws, will not be reflected until November (ish) of the same year.”

Verbatim — §329-121, “adequate supply” (current, DOH Growing Cannabis page, opened directly: health.hawaii.gov — Growing Cannabis, accessed 1 Nov 2026):

“‘Adequate supply’ means an amount of medical cannabis jointly possessed between the qualifying patient and the primary caregiver that is not more than is reasonably necessary to ensure the uninterrupted availability of cannabis for the purpose of alleviating the symptoms or effects of a qualifying patient’s debilitating medical condition; provided that an ‘adequate supply’ shall not exceed: ten cannabis plants, whether immature or mature, and four ounces of usable cannabis at any given time. The four ounces of usable cannabis shall include any combination of usable cannabis and manufactured cannabis products, as provided in chapter 329D, with the cannabis in the manufactured cannabis products being calculated using information provided pursuant to section 329D-9(c).”

Superseded figure — do not use: older reproductions (e.g. 2009 Justia) state “three mature marijuana plants, four immature marijuana plants, and one ounce of usable marijuana per each mature plant.” That is not current law. The 10-plant / 4-ounce definition above controls.

Verbatim — §329-121, “debilitating medical condition” (pre-Act 241 list, as reproduced in LawServer §329-122 terms, accessed 1 Nov 2026 — this list is now only one of three qualification routes after Act 241; see §1.4):

“(1) Cancer, glaucoma, lupus, epilepsy, multiple sclerosis, rheumatoid arthritis, positive status for human immunodeficiency virus, acquired immune deficiency syndrome, or the treatment of these conditions; (2) A chronic or debilitating disease or medical condition or its treatment that produces one or more of the following: (A) Cachexia or wasting syndrome; (B) Severe pain; (C) Severe nausea; (D) Seizures, including those characteristic of epilepsy; (E) Severe and persistent muscle spasms, including those characteristic of multiple sclerosis or Crohn’s disease; or (F) Post-traumatic stress disorder; or (3) Any other medical condition approved by the department of health pursuant to administrative rules in response to a request from a physician or advanced practice registered nurse or potentially qualifying patient.”

Note on ALS: several secondary guides list ALS (amyotrophic lateral sclerosis) among named conditions. The LawServer reproduction above does not list ALS in §329-121(1). ALS as a named §329-121 condition is Verify — do not add it without opening current §329-121 on capitol.hawaii.gov. (ALS patients may in any event qualify via the Act 241 primary-treating-provider route.)

B. HRS chapter 329D — Medical Cannabis Dispensaries (§§329D-1 to 329D-27, plus new sections added by Act 241, SLH 2025)

Origin: Act 241, SLH 2015 (HB 321 of 2015 — a different bill from HB 302 of 2025; audit-corrected). Summary: licences dispensaries, production centres, retail dispensing locations, certified laboratories; sets operations, tracking, testing, packaging/labelling/advertising, zoning, dispensing limits, fees, and enforcement. DOH/OMCCR administers. Key amendments: Act 230, SLH 2016; Act 41, SLH 2017; Act 170, SLH 2017 (terminology); Act 116, SLH 2018; Act 240, SLH 2019 (zoning, per §329D-22 history); Act 70, SLH 2020 §49 (zoning conforming); Act 309, SLH 2022 (dispensary-to-dispensary transport circumstances, production-centre definition as a series of structures within the same secured perimeter fence-line, increased production centres/plants, fee structure for additional centres and dispensary-to-dispensary sales, licensing-framework assessment appropriation); Act 172, SLH 2024 (dispensary-to-dispensary 30-day review — removed by Nov 2025 HAR update); Act 108, SLH 2023; Act 241, SLH 2025.

Verbatim — §329D-2(d)–(f) (reproduction: PocketLaw — §329D-2, accessed 1 Nov 2026):

“(d) The department shall issue eight dispensary licenses statewide; provided that three dispensary licenses shall be issued for the city and county of Honolulu, two dispensary licenses each shall be issued for the county of Hawaii and the county of Maui, and one dispensary license shall be issued for the county of Kauai; provided further that no dispensary license shall be issued for the county of Kalawao. (e) No person may be granted a dispensary license in more than one county. (f) Up to two production centers shall be allowed under each dispensary license; provided that, except as otherwise specified in subsection (k), each production center shall be limited to no more than three thousand cannabis plants.”

Caution: subsection (f) as reproduced predates Act 309, SLH 2022. The current production-centre count/plant cap after Act 309 is Verify (Part 4 adopts the current HAR / DOH 2026 report position: up to three centres, 7,500/15,000 plants). Do not quote “two production centres / 3,000 plants” as the current cap without that check. The eight-licence / 3-2-2-1-0 allocation itself is confirmed by DOH’s January 2025 annual report.

Verbatim — §329D-6(a), (c), (e), (m), (n), (o) (reproduction, PocketLaw §329D-6, plus Justia 2025 §329D-6: Justia — §329D-6, accessed 1 Nov 2026):

“(a) No person shall operate a dispensary, nor engage in the production, manufacture, or sale of cannabis or manufactured cannabis products, unless the person has obtained a license from the department pursuant to this chapter.”
“(c) No person under the age of twenty-one shall be employed by a dispensary licensee.”
“(e) Retail dispensing locations shall not be open for retail sales before 8:00 a.m. or after 8:00 p.m., Hawaii-Aleutian Standard Time, Monday through Sunday.”
“(m) A dispensary shall not transport cannabis or manufactured cannabis products to another county or another island; provided that this subsection shall not apply to the transportation of cannabis or any manufactured cannabis product solely for the purposes of laboratory testing pursuant to section 329D-8, and subject to subsection (j)…”
“(n) A dispensary shall be prohibited from off-premises delivery of cannabis or manufactured cannabis products to a qualifying patient, primary caregiver, qualifying out-of-state patient, or caregiver of a qualifying out-of-state patient.”
“(o) A dispensary shall not: (1) Display cannabis or manufactured cannabis products in windows or in public view; or (2) Post any signage other than one or two signs, each no greater than one thousand six hundred square inches bearing only the business or trade name in text without any pictures or illustrations; provided that if any applicable law or ordinance restricting outdoor signage is more restrictive, that law or ordinance shall govern.”

Verbatim — §329D-6(k) tracking (reproduction, same page): a dispensary “shall purchase, operate, and maintain a computer software tracking system that shall: [track] possession of that dispensary from either seed or immature plant state, including all plants that are derived from cuttings or cloning, until the cannabis or manufactured cannabis product is sold or destroyed pursuant to section 329D-7…” — full subsection text Verify against capitol.hawaii.gov; the seed-to-sale obligation itself is confirmed (see §1.8).

Verbatim — §329D-13 dispensing limits (2016 text, substantively unchanged in later reproductions; Cornell HAR §11-850-42 confirms the same limits — Cornell — HAR §11-850-42, accessed 1 Nov 2026):

“(a) A qualifying patient, primary caregiver, qualifying out-of-state patient, or caregiver of a qualifying out-of-state patient shall be allowed to purchase no more than four ounces of cannabis within a consecutive period of fifteen days, or no more than eight ounces of cannabis within a consecutive period of thirty days. (b) A qualifying patient, primary caregiver, qualifying out-of-state patient, or caregiver of a qualifying out-of-state patient may purchase cannabis from any dispensary location in the State, subject to the limits set forth in subsection (a).”

Verbatim — §329D-22 zoning (Justia 2025: Justia — §329D-22, accessed 1 Nov 2026):

“(a) Medical cannabis production centers and dispensaries shall comply with all county zoning ordinances, rules, or regulations; provided that: (1) A medical cannabis production center shall be permitted in any area in which agricultural production is permitted except as provided within this chapter; and (2) No medical cannabis production center or dispensary shall be permitted within seven hundred fifty feet of the real property comprising a playground or school. [L 2015, c 241, pt of §2; am L 2017, c 170, §2; am L 2019, c 240, §4; am L 2020, c 70, §49]”

“Playground” and “school” are defined in §329D-22(b) (playground = public outdoor recreation facility with 3+ child-recreation apparatus, including appurtenant parking; school = preschool through high school — full text Verify against capitol.hawaii.gov).

New sections added by Act 241, SLH 2025 (verbatim from the session-law PDF, Act 241, SLH 2025 PDF): Unlicensed operation of a dispensary; criminal penalty: “No person shall intentionally, knowingly, or recklessly operate a dispensary without a license issued by the department pursuant to this chapter.” (class C felony); platform advertising violation = misdemeanour, after DOH cease-and-desist notice. Cannabis cultivator; license required (eff. 1 Jan 2028): “Notwithstanding section 329D-24, it shall be unlawful for any person to cultivate cannabis without a license issued by the department pursuant to this section.”

C. Decriminalisation and expungement — Act 273, SLH 2019 (HB 1383)

Status: became law without Governor’s signature, 10 July 2019 (Gov. Msg. No. 1393); effective 11 January 2020. Summary (LegiScan, secondary, LegiScan HB 1383, accessed 1 Nov 2026): “Provides for the expungement of criminal records pertaining solely to the possession of three grams or less of marijuana. Decriminalizes the possession of three grams or less of marijuana and establishes that the possession is a violation punishable by a monetary fine of $130. Establishes a marijuana evaluation task force…” Operative effect: possession of ≤3 grams is a violation (civil, non-criminal), $130 fine, no jail. Expungement of records pertaining solely to ≤3-gram possession is provided for (detail is a Part 12 question; the Act 273 session-law text itself was not opened in this run — Verify expungement procedure and whether it is automatic or petition-based before citing procedure). What Act 273 did not do: it did not legalise possession, sale, or cultivation above 3 grams, and did not create any adult-use retail system.

D. Hemp — HRS chapter 141 (commercial production, now Part III), HRS chapter 328G (hemp processors), HAR chapter 4-161 (interim, historical), HAR chapter 11-37 (interim, current)

1. Industrial hemp pilot → USDA programme — Act 14, SLH 2020 (HB 1819, SD3, signed 27 August 2020, Gov. Msg. No. 1118). Official session-law PDF, opened via search result: Act 14, SLH 2020 PDF (accessed 1 Nov 2026). Purposes (verbatim from Act 14 §1, bill text HB 1819 SD1, accessed 1 Nov 2026): “(1) Allow the growth of hemp in the State through the USDA hemp production program; (2) Allow the processing and sale of certain hemp products in the State; (3) Expedite the substitution of the USDA hemp production program for the existing industrial hemp pilot program as required by federal law.” Act 14 added a new chapter (originally chapter 328H, now chapter 328G) for hemp processors and added HRS chapter 141, Part III for commercial hemp production. The chapter 141 commercial-production part as enacted in 2020 (Justia 2020: Justia — §141-42 (2020), accessed 1 Nov 2026) provided in substance: it shall be legal to produce hemp, as defined in 7 U.S.C. §1639o, if the person has a USDA licence under 7 U.S.C. §1639q; provided that hemp shall not be grown within 300 feet of pre-existing real property comprising a playground, childcare facility, or school, nor within 100 feet of any pre-existing house, dwelling unit, residential apartment, or other residential structure not owned or controlled [audit-corrected from the superseded 500-foot figures; §141-42] by the licence holder — with a grandfather exception for pilot-programme licensees licensed in those areas before 27 August 2020. Note: Justia marks this Part “repealed June 30, 2022. L 2020, c 14, §9.” The current location/status of commercial-production provisions after that repeal note is Verify against current HRS chapter 141 — do not cite §141-42 as current law without that check. Hawai‘i does not operate its own USDA-approved state hemp plan in sources reviewed; cultivation is through the USDA programme (§1.6). Whether any state-plan submission has since been made is Verify against USDA AMS records (Part 7).

2. HRS chapter 328G — Hemp Processors (current chapter number). HRS index (search result, capitol.hawaii.gov — HRS chapter 328G index, accessed 1 Nov 2026) lists: §328G-1 Definitions; §328G-2 Hemp processor permit application; permit revocation; §328G-3 Hemp biomass processing; manufactured hemp product sale and prohibitions; labelling; §328G-4 Rulemaking; §328G-5 Laboratory standards and testing; certification; §328G-6 Enforcement; penalty; §328G-7 Hawai‘i hemp processing special fund; §328G-8 Applicability; §328G-9 Registry of distributors and retailers; application; renewals; suspension/revocation; exceptions; … §328G-13 Tinctures; persons under twenty-one years of age. Repeal note flagged: the same index carries the note “Chapter repealed July 1, 2027. L 2023, c 263, §15. (This Note supersedes the Note in the main volume.)” This is a major Verify item: if the note is current, chapter 328G sunsets 1 July 2027 unless extended. It is reported here only as an index note found in search results, not as a verified current-law conclusion. Act 263, SLH 2023, must be opened in Run 2 / Part 7. Penalty (Act 14 as introduced, §328H-F, now §328G-6): “Any person who violates this chapter or any rule adopted by the department pursuant to this chapter shall be fined not more than $10,000 for each separate offense.” — via LegiScan — HB 1819 text (accessed 1 Nov 2026). Confirm current §328G-6 text on capitol.hawaii.gov.

3. Act 269, SLH 2025 (HB 1482, CD1, signed 2 July 2025, Gov. Msg. No. 1372). Summary (BillTrack, secondary, BillTrack — HB 1482, accessed 1 Nov 2026): “Beginning 1/1/2026, requires manufactured hemp product distributors and manufactured hemp product retailers to obtain a certificate of registration from DOH. Establishes the offense of unlawful hemp distribution or retailing. Prohibits the sale of tinctures to persons under twenty-one years of age. Establishes that a violation of the hemp processor laws constitutes an unfair or deceptive act or practice… Allows funds in the Hawaii Hemp Processing Special Fund to be used by DOH for the registry… Authorizes [OMCCR] to administer the hemp processors program. Establishes that every building, premises, or place used for the purpose of violating the hemp processors law is a nuisance subject to abatement.” DOH confirmation (opened directly): DOH — hemp retail registration (4 Nov 2025) (accessed 1 Nov 2026): “Act 269, which was signed into law July 2, 2025, creates a state registry for retailers and distributors of manufactured hemp products (MHP). Act 269 requires all hemp businesses selling MHP in Hawai‘i, including online sellers, to register with the OMCCR. The registration requirement takes effect on Jan. 1, 2026… A registration fee of $50 is required for a five-year period.” Act 269 also underlies HAR chapter 11-37 §§11-37-11 to 11-37-16 (distributor/retailer registration, display, suspension/revocation, buyer due diligence, tincture 21+ rule, location restriction).

4. HAR chapter 11-37 (Interim Rules) — Hemp Processing and Manufactured Hemp Products. History: eff. 9 Aug 2021; amended/compiled 24 Feb 2022, 29 Apr 2022, 6 Dec 2024, and 28 Jan 2026 (compilation opened directly: HAR 11-37, 28 Jan 2026 (PDF), accessed 1 Nov 2026; authority cited: HRS §§328G-4, 328G-5, SLH 2025, Act 269, §1). DOH Statutes and Rules page confirms the 6 Dec 2024 compilation as the prior version: health.hawaii.gov — Statutes and Rules (accessed 1 Nov 2026 via search result). Interim-rule status matters: these are interim rules, not permanent chapter 91 rules. Act 46, SLH 2025 extended the interim-rule sunset for medical-cannabis rules; whether an equivalent extension applies to chapter 11-37 interim rules, and the interaction with the chapter 328G 1 July 2027 repeal note above, is Verify.

5. HAR chapter 11-850 (Interim Rules) — Medical Cannabis Dispensaries. History per DOH Laws and Rules page: “Chapter 11-850 interim rules, effective December 6, 2024.” Amended 17 November 2025 (DOH release, opened directly, §0.11). Earlier compilations: 14 Dec 2015 (original), 24 Feb 2022, 29 Apr 2022, 17 Nov 2022, 7 Aug 2023, 5 Apr 2024, 6 Dec 2024 (Cornell §11-850-42 history). The full November 2025 chapter 11-850 compilation PDF was not opened in the Part 0–1 pass — Verify the full text for Part 4 / Part 11, and do not cite November 2025 rule numbers beyond the DOH release summary. A secondary source also reports interim rules effective 17 November 2025 as the latest compilation — consistent with the DOH release, but the compilation itself remains Verify.

6. HAR chapter 11-160 — Medical Use of Cannabis (Registry). Listed by DOH as “DOH Administrative Rules for the medical use of cannabis (Registry Program)” (Laws and Rules page). Key provision cited in DOH growing guidance: §11-160-31(b) — each cultivated plant must bear a legible identification tag showing the 329 registration number and expiration date (DOH Growing Cannabis page). Full chapter text not opened in this run — Verify for fee, application and grow-site rule detail in Run 2.

7. Other HRS chapters that touch cannabis/hemp (listed by DOH, not fully researched here): HRS chapter 329 (Uniform Controlled Substances Act, Parts I–VIII for scheduling/prescription controls; Part IX for medical use). Hawai‘i’s state scheduling of cannabis after the federal Schedule III change — Verify: no Hawai‘i statute amending HRS §329-14 (Schedule I listing) to reflect Schedule III for state-licensed medical cannabis was located in this run. Do not state that Hawai‘i state scheduling has changed. HRS chapter 712, Part IV (§§712-1240 et seq., promoting detrimental drugs; §§712-1249.4–1249.6 commercial promotion; §712-1270 drug nuisance) — penalties in §1.3. HRS chapter 328J (Smoke-Free Law) — applicable to smoking/vaping cannabis per DOH. HRS chapter 328 (Food, Drug, Cosmetic Act) — referenced in HAR §11-37-3(c) food definition. HRS §§321-30.1 (Medical Cannabis Registry and Regulation Special Fund), 28-131 (Drug Nuisance Abatement Unit), 453-1.3 (telehealth / physician-patient relationship), 457-2 (APRN telehealth), 378 (employment — see §1.9; no current patient-employment clause in §329-125.5, see below), 421J-16 / 514B-113 (condominium / association documents — Act 242, SLH 2015, voiding discriminatory association provisions except where tobacco smoking is prohibited and cannabis is smoked — Verify current text), 521-39 (landlord-tenant — see §1.9 note), 846-2.7 (background checks). HRS chapter 141, HAR chapter 4-161 (Hemp Production Interim Rules, historical pilot-programme rules) — superseded for cultivation by the USDA programme; do not cite chapter 4-161 as the current cultivation rule without checking its current status (Verify).

E. Cannabis authority / adult-use framework law — NONE ENACTED

No chapter creating a Hawai‘i Cannabis Authority, Cannabis and Hemp Office, or adult-use framework has been enacted as of 1 November 2026. The AG’s January 2024 report and 315-page draft bill (AG News Release 2024-01, 5 Jan 2024: AG — cannabis legalization report (5 Jan 2024), accessed 1 Nov 2026) proposed such an authority and a Hawai‘i Cannabis Law, but the AG expressly stated the Department “does not support the legalization of adult-use cannabis” and the draft “is not ‘the Department of the Attorney General’s cannabis bill.’” That draft is not law. See §1.10 for the bills that carried its concepts forward and failed.

1.3 Penalties for non-patients; cultivation; paraphernalia; public consumption; medical product forms

Possession — non-patients (HRS chapter 712)

Amount / conductClassification (as summarised by DOH and secondary HRS 712 summaries)Maximum penalty as summarised
≤3 grams, marijuanaViolation (non-criminal) — Act 273, SLH 2019$130 fine, no jail
>3 grams but <1 ouncePetty misdemeanour — §712-1249, promoting a detrimental drug in the 3rd degreeUp to 30 days jail, up to $1,000 fine
≥1 ounce but <1 poundMisdemeanour — §712-1248, 2nd degreeUp to 1 year jail, up to $2,000 fine
≥1 poundClass C felony — §712-1247, 1st degree (possession prong)Up to 5 years, up to $10,000 fine
≥2 pounds (possession) / ≥1 pound (distribution) / ≥50 plants / any cultivation on another’s land without permissionClass B felony — §712-1249.5, commercial promotion 2nd degreeUp to 10 years, up to $25,000 fine (fine figure from secondary summaries — Verify against HRS §706-640)
≥25 pounds (possession) / ≥5 pounds (distribution) / ≥100 plants / cultivation of ≥25 plants on another’s land without permissionClass A felony — §712-1249.4, commercial promotion 1st degreeUp to 20 years (fine figure Verify)

Sources for the table structure: DOH Laws and Rules page (above), which summarises §712-1247 as “cultivating 25–49 plants = class C felony,” §712-1248 as “possession of one ounce+ = misdemeanor,” §712-1249 as “possession of any cannabis (and not registered) = petty misdemeanor,” §712-1249.4 and §712-1249.5 as above, and §712-1249.6 as class C felony (commercial promotion 3rd degree — conduct Verify). Secondary penalty tables corroborate the jail/fine maxima: LegalClarity — marijuana in Hawaii (accessed 1 Nov 2026).

Cautions: (1) DOH’s summary does not separately list the Act 273 ≤3-gram violation carve-out in §712-1249. The table above reconciles DOH’s summary with Act 273 by treating ≤3 grams as the exception to the §712-1249 petty-misdemeanour rule. The exact current subsection that creates the violation (and whether the $130 is a fixed fine or a maximum) is Verify against current HRS §712-1249 on capitol.hawaii.gov. (2) General felony/misdemeanour fine maxima are in HRS chapter 706 and were not opened in this run; fine figures above $1,000/$2,000 are secondary and marked Verify where noted. (3) Distribution vs sale: secondary summaries distinguish distributing (giving, without payment) from selling/bartering: distributing <1 ounce = misdemeanour (§712-1248); distributing ≥1 ounce or selling/bartering any amount = class C felony (§712-1247). This distinction was not verified against current §712-1247/1248 text in this run — Verify before Run 2. (4) Patients exceeding limits lose protection: possession above an adequate supply, or use for non-medical purposes, is outside Part IX protection (§329-122(e)(3)) and falls back to chapter 712.

Cultivation — non-patients

  • 25–49 plants: class C felony (§712-1247, per DOH summary).
  • 50+ plants: class B felony (§712-1249.5, per DOH summary).
  • 100+ plants: class A felony (§712-1249.4, per DOH summary).
  • Any cultivation on land owned by another without express permission / legal right to occupy: class B felony (§712-1249.5, per DOH summary); cultivation of 25+ plants on another’s land without permission: class A felony (§712-1249.4, per DOH summary).
  • Cultivation below 25 plants by a non-patient is not separately listed in DOH’s summary; it would fall under possession/distribution provisions depending on amount and intent. Do not state a specific penalty for 1–24 plants by a non-patient without opening §712-1247 — Verify.
  • Future law (not current): Act 241, SLH 2025, Part IV, eff. 1 Jan 2028, will require a DOH cannabis cultivator licence for any cultivation (§0.2). Until then, patient/caregiver cultivation under Part IX and dispensary cultivation under chapter 329D are the lawful cultivation routes; all other cultivation is governed by chapter 712 as above.

Paraphernalia — Verify (gap)

“Medical use” in §329-121 expressly includes “paraphernalia relating to the administration of cannabis” for qualifying patients. Dispensaries may now sell cannabis-related supplies (vaporizers, grinders, storage containers, rolling papers, filters, cones) under the November 2025 HAR chapter 11-850 amendments. No paraphernalia-specific penalty table was verified in opened sources in this run. HRS §329-43.5 (paraphernalia) and chapter 712 paraphernalia provisions were not opened. Do not cite a paraphernalia penalty in Run 2 without opening those sections.

Public consumption

Verbatim — §329-122(e) (LawServer reproduction, LawServer — §329-122, accessed 1 Nov 2026):

“The authorization for the medical use of cannabis in this section shall not apply to: (1) The medical use of cannabis that endangers the health or well-being of another person; (2) The medical use of cannabis: (A) In a school bus, public bus, or any moving vehicle; (B) In the workplace of one’s employment; (C) On any school grounds; (D) At any public park, public beach, public recreation center, recreation or youth center; or (E) At any other place open to the public; provided that [transport in a sealed container, not visible to the public, not removed/consumed in the public place, is not prohibited]; and (3) The use of cannabis by a qualifying patient, parent, primary caregiver, qualifying out-of-state patient, or caregiver of a qualifying out-of-state patient, for purposes other than medical use permitted by this part.”

Additional notes: use on dispensary premises is also prohibited in dispensary guidance (patients may not medicate on dispensary premises; transport through public places in a sealed container is permitted — dispensary FAQ, secondary: Hawaiian Ethos FAQ, accessed 1 Nov 2026). HRS chapter 328J (Smoke-Free Law) applies to smoking/vaping cannabis (DOH Laws and Rules page). No per se THC driving limit was located in opened sources for this Part; impaired driving is addressed under Hawai‘i’s OVUII law (HRS §291E) and is a Part 12 question. Do not state a ng/mL limit for Hawai‘i.

Allowed medical product forms and THC limits

Statutory definition — “manufactured cannabis product,” §329D-1 (as amended by Act 108, SLH 2023 drafts and Act 241, SLH 2025 context; reproduction via 2023 bill text HB 1218 (2024) bill text, accessed 1 Nov 2026, showing the definition as then proposed/amended):

“‘Manufactured cannabis product’ means any capsule, lozenge, oil or oil extract, tincture, ointment or skin lotion, pill, transdermal patch, or prefilled and sealed container used to aerosolize and deliver cannabis orally or by inhalation, such as an inhaler, nebulizer, or device that provides safe pulmonary administration, that has been manufactured using cannabis, edible cannabis product, prerolled cannabis flower product, or any other products as specified by the department pursuant to section 329D-10(a)(11).”

Forms therefore allowed through licensed dispensaries (patients/caregivers only): Flower (cannabis itself, not a manufactured product) — yes, including pre-rolled cannabis flower products (“pre-rolls”), added as a manufactured product type by DOH amendments 27 March 2024 per DOH’s January 2025 annual report, in accordance with Act 108, SLH 2023. Edibles / edible cannabis products — yes, as specified by DOH. Vapes / inhalable products — yes: prefilled sealed containers for aerosolized delivery, plus, under November 2025 HAR amendments, inhalable oil extracts/concentrates that do not contain nicotine, hemp or hemp-derived cannabinoids, tobacco products, or other non-cannabis substances; and sale of dry-herb vaporizers as supplies. Concentrates / oil extracts — yes, on the same inhalable basis above (previously categorised as edible-only; November 2025 amendments expressly permit inhalable marketing subject to ingredient restrictions — DOH release, §0.11). Topicals — yes: ointment / skin lotion. Tinctures, capsules, lozenges, pills, transdermal patches — yes, by definition. Other products as specified by DOH under §329D-10(a)(11) — DOH discretion; no exhaustive list was opened in this run.

THC limits for medical products: Unavailable / Verify. No mg-per-serving, mg-per-package, or percentage THC cap for medical cannabis products was located in any opened statute, rule summary, or DOH release in this run. Do not import the hemp mg caps (1 mg / 5 mg / 0.5 mg / 3 mg) into the medical programme — they apply only to manufactured hemp products under HAR chapter 11-37. If a medical-product potency rule exists in full HAR chapter 11-850, it must be located in the full compilation. Prohibited ingredients for inhalable medical products (Nov 2025): nicotine, hemp/hemp-derived cannabinoids, tobacco, other non-cannabis substances (DOH release).

1.4 Medical programme in full

Qualifying conditions — three routes after Act 241 (2025)

Route 1 — Named debilitating conditions (§329-121, pre-Act 241 list, quoted in §1.2A): cancer, glaucoma, lupus, epilepsy, multiple sclerosis, rheumatoid arthritis, HIV-positive status, AIDS, or treatment thereof; or a chronic/debilitating disease/condition or its treatment producing cachexia/wasting, severe pain, severe nausea, seizures (including epilepsy), severe and persistent muscle spasms (including MS/Crohn’s), or PTSD; or any other condition approved by DOH by rule on request of a physician/APRN or potentially qualifying patient.

Route 2 — Primary treating medical provider discretion (NEW, Act 241, SLH 2025, §3, verbatim from session-law PDF, Act 241, SLH 2025 PDF):

“‘Primary treating medical provider’ means a physician or an advanced practice registered nurse located in, and with an active unrestricted license to practice in, the State who, within the physician’s or advanced practice registered nurse’s scope of practice and individual competency, is primarily responsible for the treatment and ongoing care of the qualifying patient and has determined that the potential benefits of the medical use of cannabis are likely to outweigh the associated health risks for the qualifying patient.”
“‘Qualifying patient’ means a person who (1) Has been diagnosed as having a debilitating medical condition by a physician or advanced practice registered nurse who has certified in writing that, in the physician’s or advanced practice registered nurse’s professional opinion, the benefit of the medical use of cannabis would likely outweigh the health risks for the person; (2) Has been diagnosed as having a condition other than a debilitating medical condition by the person’s primary treating medical provider who has certified in writing that, in the primary treating medical provider’s professional opinion, the potential benefits of the medical use of cannabis would likely outweigh the health risks for the person; or (3) Is receiving hospice care and the hospice provider licensed in the State has certified in writing that the person is receiving hospice care.”

Route 3 — Hospice: as in (3) above. Important limit: Route 2 is not “any provider may certify any condition on a first telehealth visit.” The certifier must be the patient’s primary treating provider — primarily responsible for treatment and ongoing care. Secondary guides that state “any condition qualifies if your provider says so” without the primary-treating qualifier overstate Act 241.

Certifying providers

  • Physicians licensed under HRS chapter 453 with authority to prescribe drugs and registered under §329-32, and advanced practice registered nurses (APRNs) with prescriptive authority under HRS §457-8.6 — §329-121 definitions (LawServer terms).
  • Hospice providers licensed in Hawai‘i may certify hospice patients (Act 241, SLH 2025).
  • Dispensary licensees, officers, employees and agents may NOT provide written certification for any person (§329D-6(b)). November 2025 HAR amendments prohibit in-person or telehealth certification visits at dispensary facilities.
  • Bona fide relationship: §329-123(a) as amended by Act 241 requires a bona fide physician-patient or APRN-patient relationship, but expressly provides it need not be established by an initial in-person consultation. §329-126(b) as amended: “a bona fide physician-patient relationship may be established via telehealth, as defined in section 453-1.3(j), and a bona fide [APRN]-patient relationship may be established via telehealth, as defined in section 457-2.” Act 241 §9 also amended §453-1.3(c) — the interaction between the general opiate rule and the cannabis telehealth rule is Verify against the enrolled Act 241 text for §453-1.3(c) before citing it as a standalone proposition.
  • Fee cap (Act 241 §5, verbatim from session-law PDF): “Any fees assessed by a certifying physician or advanced practice registered nurse to issue a written certification pursuant to this subsection shall not exceed an amount equal to three times the amount of the fee charged by the department of health to issue a registration certificate pursuant to subsection (b).” If the DOH fee is $35, the provider cap is $105 for a one-year certification / $210 for a two-year certification (secondary guides, corroborated by the 3× formula). Provider fees are separate from the DOH registration fee.

Registry and cards — fully online; electronic only

Application is online only through the DOH Medical Cannabis Registry portal (formerly medmj.ehawaii.gov; new portal from 30 Sep 2026 — §0.6). Steps in DOH guidance: certifying provider completes an Electronic Certification (E-Cert); patient creates/logs into an account, completes the application, uploads valid ID (driver’s licence, state ID, or passport for in-state patients), pays the fee; DOH reviews typically within 3–5 business days; on approval, email notification and electronic 329 card download. Incomplete applications are returned electronically for correction.

Fees — conflicting figures, flagged: DOH registration fee is $35 (statutory fee). Total amount paid online is reported as $38.50 ($35 + $3.50 administrative fee) in most secondary guides and DOH-linked summaries, for a one-year card, and $77 for an eligible two-year renewal. One secondary guide (Leafwell, accessed via search 1 Nov 2026: Leafwell — Hawaii medical card) reports $36.05 ($35 + $1.05).
