The $3.50 vs $1.05 administrative fee is a conflicting figure — Verify against the live DOH fee page / eHawaii checkout before Run 2 publishes a single total. Fees are non-refundable (DOH application page).

Validity: written certifications are valid for one year from signing; DOH may allow three-year validity if the physician/APRN states the condition is chronic (Act 241 §3, amending §329-121 “written certification”). In practice, DOH issues one-year initial registrations and up to two-year renewals for chronic conditions with the same provider (secondary guides; DOH renewal guidance states renewals may be started 60 days before expiration, only one card valid at a time, new card auto-activates on expiry of the old). Reconcile “3-year certification” (statute) vs “2-year renewal card” (practice) in Run 2 — Verify whether DOH implements the full 3-year authority or caps renewals at 2 years by rule. Renewal notices: DOH sends email renewal notifications 60 days before expiration (DOH application page). Patients are not authorised until they hold the card: DOH application guidance: “Patients are not authorized for the medical use of cannabis until they receive their 329 registration card in hand from the DOH.” SB 3315 (2026), which would have allowed a one-time purchase on submission of the form before confirmed registration (up to 25% of dispensing limits), was NOT enacted (§1.10). Do not present pre-card purchase as lawful. Confidentiality: registry information is confidential under HAR chapter 11-160, subchapter 9 (§11-160-56), with limited exceptions (law-enforcement verification, patient consent, etc.) — full text Verify; DOH registry page notes law-enforcement verification access in secondary summaries only.

Caregivers

  • Definition (§329-121, DOH Growing Cannabis page, opened directly): “‘Primary caregiver’ means a person eighteen years of age or older, other than the qualifying patient and the qualifying patient’s physician or [APRN], who has agreed to undertake responsibility for managing the well-being of the qualifying patient with respect to the medical use of cannabis. In the case of a minor or an adult lacking legal capacity, the primary caregiver shall be a parent, guardian, or person having legal custody.”
  • One caregiver per patient at a time, except a parent/guardian/person having legal custody may serve as caregiver for more than one minor qualifying patient of whom they are parent/guardian/custodian (DOH Growing Cannabis page).
  • Cap raised by Act 46, SLH 2025: from 1 to 5 qualifying patients per primary caregiver at any given time (§329-123(c) as amended; DOH summary and SB 1429 CD1 text: SB 1429 CD1, accessed 1 Nov 2026).
  • Caregiver cultivation restored: Act 46 repealed, retroactive to 1 January 2025, the prohibition on primary caregivers cultivating for their patients. A caregiver may now cultivate up to an adequate supply for the patient, provided each cultivation location is used for not more than five qualifying patients (DOH summary; SB 1429 CD1 text).
  • Caregiver registration: caregivers register with DOH; the caregiver’s name/date of birth and the grow-site location (if growing) appear on the patient’s 329 card (DOH Growing Cannabis page). Secondary guides state no separate caregiver fee (patient’s fee covers both) — Verify against DOH fee schedule.
  • Out-of-state caregivers: only a parent/guardian/person having legal custody of a qualifying out-of-state patient under 18 may serve as caregiver of a qualifying out-of-state patient (§329-121 definition, LawServer terms). Adult out-of-state patients have no caregiver designation in Hawai‘i law as reproduced — Verify against §329-123.5.

Possession and purchase limits (in-state)

  • Possession (adequate supply, patient + caregiver jointly): 10 plants (mature or immature) and 4 ounces usable cannabis at any given time, including manufactured products (cannabis content calculated via §329D-9(c) information) — §329-121, quoted in §1.2A.
  • Purchase (dispensing limits, §329D-13): no more than 4 ounces in any consecutive 15 days, or 8 ounces in any consecutive 30 days, counted across all dispensary locations statewide. A patient/caregiver may buy from any dispensary location in the State subject to those limits. Dispensaries must check purchases from other dispensaries (HAR §11-850-42(b), Cornell).
  • Dispensary-to-dispensary petition / excess: November 2025 HAR amendments clarify an 800-ounce/30-day dispensary-to-dispensary sales limit and a petition process to exceed it — this is a wholesale limit, not a patient limit. Do not conflate the two.
  • What counts: the 4-ounce possession figure includes manufactured products; the 4/8-ounce purchase figures are in “cannabis” weight equivalents as calculated under chapter 329D. Exact conversion for edibles/concentrates is in HAR chapter 11-850 / §329D-9(c) and was not opened in full — Verify for Part 4/11.

Patient and caregiver home grow and grow-site rules

  • Who may grow: (1) licensed dispensaries (production centres); (2) qualifying patients for their own use; (3) primary caregivers for their patients (restored 1 Jan 2025, Act 46). No one else may handle a patient’s plants/usable cannabis: “Hawaii state law does not authorize transferring cannabis and paraphernalia or handling a qualifying patient’s medical cannabis plant or usable cannabis outside the registered patient-primary caregiver relationship. Only you (registered patient) and your one reported primary caregiver stated on your valid 329 registration card are allowed to handle your usable cannabis or your medical cannabis plants” (DOH Growing Cannabis page).
  • Where: only at one location, identified on the 329 card: the patient’s residence, the caregiver’s residence, or another site owned or controlled by the patient or caregiver (DOH Growing Cannabis page).
  • Tagging: every plant must bear a legible tag showing the 329 registration number and expiration date (HAR §11-160-31(b), per DOH Growing Cannabis page).
  • Indoor/outdoor: secondary summaries state plants must be in an enclosed, secure area not visible to the public; HAR chapter 11-160 “does not specify whether a cannabis plant may be cultivated indoors or outdoors” (secondary, Maui guide). Indoor/outdoor and security/fencing requirements for home grows are Verify against HAR chapter 11-160 full text.
  • Grow-site registration / change: grow-site address must appear on the registry card (§329-123(a) as amended by Act 241: “The form shall require the address of the location where the cannabis is grown and shall appear on the registry card”). Change procedures and any cultivation-site registry fee proposed in earlier bills (e.g. SB 962, 2023, proposing a cultivation-site registry) were not enacted in the form reviewed — do not present a separate grow-site licence as current law for patients (contrast the future 2028 cultivator licence in §0.2, which is a different, commercial licence).

Out-of-state patient registration (“329 card reciprocity”) and fees

  • Eligibility (§329-122(c), LawServer reproduction): a qualifying out-of-state patient 18+ legally authorised in another state, U.S. territory, or DC may use cannabis medically in Hawai‘i only if they: (1) provide DOH a valid home-jurisdiction medical-use card with an explicit, unexpired expiration date and valid photo ID/driver’s licence from the same jurisdiction; (2) attest under penalty of law (HRS §710-1063) that their home condition is a debilitating condition under §329-121; (3) consent to DOH obtaining verification information from their certifying provider and issuing entity; (4) pay the required fee; (5) register with DOH; (6) receive a Hawai‘i medical cannabis registry card; and (7) abide by all Hawai‘i medical-use laws, including adequate-supply limits.
  • Adequate supply for out-of-state patients (§329-121, via LawServer terms): no more than 4 ounces usable cannabis at any given time, and no live plants. “Medical use” does not include cultivation or distribution by a qualifying out-of-state patient or their caregiver.
  • Minors (out-of-state): §329-122(d) — caregiver provides the §329-122(c) information and consents in writing to allow use, manage well-being, and control acquisition/dosage/frequency.
  • Terms and fee (DOH guidance, via secondary guides corroborating DOH application pages — fee and terms are consistent across guides, but the DOH out-of-state application page itself was not separately opened in this run, so mark as secondary-confirmed): $47.41 [audit-corrected application fee], non-refundable, per 60-day term; up to two 60-day terms per calendar year (max 120 days/year); may apply up to 60 days before the requested start date; registration ends on the earlier of term expiry or home-card expiry. International medical cards are not accepted. Home-state certification alone (without a government-issued card) is insufficient.
  • Purchase limits for out-of-state patients: same 4 oz / 15 days and 8 oz / 30 days as in-state patients (§329D-13).
  • Terminology: DOH and guides refer to the visitor card as a “329V” / “329-OSP” card. That is an administrative label, not a separate HRS chapter.

Minors’ rules

  • In-state minors (§329-122(b) as amended by Act 241, verbatim from session-law PDF): medical use by a patient under 18 is permitted only if (1) the physician/APRN/hospice provider who determined the patient to be a qualifying patient has explained risks/benefits to the patient and to a parent/guardian/person having legal custody, and documented that both understand them; and (2) a parent/guardian/person having legal custody consents in writing to (A) allow use, (B) serve as primary caregiver, and (C) control acquisition, dosage and frequency.
  • Caregiver for minors: must be a parent/guardian/person having legal custody (see above). One or both parents/guardians may register as caregivers for one or more minor patients (Act 116, SLH 2018, per DOH summary).
  • Out-of-state minors: see §329-122(d) above.
  • Dispensary employment: no person under 21 may be employed by a dispensary (§329D-6(c)). This is an employment age, not a patient age — do not conflate.
  • Hemp tincture age: 21+ only (see §1.6). Other manufactured hemp products carry a “Not for sale to persons under the age of 21” label statement under HAR chapter 11-37, but whether that is a sale prohibition for all manufactured hemp products or a labelling requirement is Verify — DOH consumer FAQ lists the label statement for all products and a specific 21+ sale prohibition for tinctures (HAR §11-37-15 / HRS §328G-13). Do not state a universal 21+ purchase ban for all hemp products without that check.

1.5 Interisland transport

Patients/caregivers: prohibited interisland, by any means, for any purpose. See verbatim §329-122(f) paragraph in §0.8. The prohibition is not limited to air travel: it says “by any means.” It applies to transport between a patient/caregiver/out-of-state patient/caregiver and any other entity or individual, including another patient/caregiver.

Dispensaries: prohibited interisland except two narrow carve-outs: (1) Laboratory testing only (§329D-6(m), §329D-8, subject to §329D-6(j) tracking), in an amount/manner prescribed by DOH rule, with the understanding state protections do not apply outside State jurisdiction. (2) Dispensary-to-dispensary sales to the extent authorised by §329D-6(r) (800 oz / 30 days, manifest before sale, etc., as amended by Act 241, SLH 2025). Whether §329D-6(r) in practice authorises inter-county/interisland wholesale transfers, or only intra-county transfers, is a point on which the §329-122(f) text (“allowable only between dispensaries to the extent authorized by section 329D-6(r)”) suggests an interisland wholesale route exists, but the full §329D-6(r) and HAR §11-850 transport rules were not opened in full — Verify the geographic scope of dispensary-to-dispensary transport before advising any operator.

Within-island transport (patients/caregivers/dispensary staff): transport in a public place is permitted only in a sealed container, not visible to the public, not removed or consumed while in the public place (§329-122(e)(E) proviso). For laboratory testing, a patient/caregiver may transport up to 1 gram per test to a certified laboratory, only with a secured appointment and electronic/written confirmation showing time/date, product description and amount, available during transport (§329-122(f)(5)).

Federal airspace and waters issue: Hawai‘i law itself states its protections do not apply outside the jurisdictional limits of the State (§329-122(f); §329D-6(m) proviso). Airports, aircraft in federal airspace, and vessels in federal waters are federal jurisdiction. A 329 card is not a defence to federal possession law, and the federal baseline (Schedule III for state-licensed medical, Schedule I for non-medical) does not create a federal right to transport cannabis between islands or to/from the mainland. No Hawai‘i law authorising patient interisland air transport was located. Bills that would have changed interisland rules were not identified as enacted in this run (§1.10 — interisland-transport bills: none enacted found; search gap flagged in gaps). Practical advice that follows from the statute (not a separate law): patients should not carry cannabis on interisland flights or interisland ferries; they should purchase on the destination island or grow on-island. This is dispensary/DOH guidance, not a statutory safe harbour.

1.6 Hemp and intoxicating hemp

Definition and THC measurement — three layers that do not perfectly align (flagged)

Layer 1 — Federal / HRS 329-1 “hemp” (Act 14, SLH 2020, via bill text LegiScan — HB 1819, accessed 1 Nov 2026):

“‘Hemp’ means all parts of the plant Cannabis sativa L., whether growing or not, including the seeds thereof and all derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts, and salts of isomers, whether growing or not, with a delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol concentration of not more than 0.3 per cent on a dry weight basis, as measured post-decarboxylation or by other similarly reliable methods.”

Layer 2 — HAR chapter 11-37 “total THC” (Jan 2026 compilation, opened directly, §§11-37-2):

“‘Total tetrahydrocannabinol’ or ‘total THC’ means the sum of the percentage by weight of: (1) Delta-9-tetrahydrocannabinolic acid (D9-THCA) multiplied by 0.877; and (2) Delta-9-tetrahydrocannabinol (D9-THC); and (3) Delta-8-tetrahydrocannabinol (D8-THC).”
“‘Decarboxylated’ means the completion of the chemical reaction that converts naturally occurring cannabinoid acid into a cannabinoid, including delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol’s acids (THCA) into delta-9-tetrahydrocannabinol. The decarboxylated value for delta-9-tetrahydrocannabinol may be calculated using a conversion formula that sums delta-9-tetrahydrocannabinol and eighty-seven and seven tenths (87.7) per cent of THCA.”

Layer 3 — HAR chapter 11-37 product cap: “No person shall sell, hold for sale, offer, or distribute any crude extract or manufactured hemp product with a total tetrahydrocannabinol concentration of more than 0.3 per cent.” (§11-37-3(d))

Tension to preserve: the HRS definition is delta-9-only (post-decarboxylation); the HAR product rule counts THCA×0.877 plus delta-8. A product that is ≤0.3% delta-9 but >0.3% total THC as defined in HAR chapter 11-37 is prohibited in Hawai‘i. Do not cite the HRS delta-9 definition alone as the product-compliance test. Cultivation definition (for Part 7, noted here to avoid contradiction): hemp production is lawful only with a USDA licence under 7 U.S.C. §1639q (Act 14, §141-42 as enacted 2020; current status Verify per §1.2D). Hawai‘i does not, in sources reviewed, operate its own USDA-approved state plan.

Delta-8, delta-9, THCA, and other hemp-derived THC — status table

Substance / productHawai‘i status (manufactured hemp product rules)
Delta-9 THC, naturally occurring, in a compliant manufactured hemp productAllowed only within caps: ≤0.3% total THC and 1 mg/serving / 5 mg/package (edibles except tinctures/beverages), 0.5 mg/container (beverages 6–12 fl oz), 3 mg/serving / max 1 oz container (tinctures).
Delta-8 THC, artificially derived (converted from CBD)Prohibited. “Artificially derived cannabinoid” = “a chemical substance that is created by a chemical reaction that changes the molecular structure of any chemical substance derived from the plant genus cannabis (e.g., changing cannabidiol into delta-8-tetrahydrocannabinol)” (HAR §11-37-2). Manufactured products “shall not contain… Synthetic cannabinoids; Artificially derived cannabinoids” (§11-37-4(d)(11)–(12)). DOH consumer FAQ lists delta-8, THCO, HHC, CBN created from CBD as prohibited.
Delta-8 THC, naturally occurring (extracted, not converted)Not prohibited as a category in DOH FAQ: “To the extent delta-8 THC and other cannabinoids are naturally occurring in the plant, they can be extracted and used in a manufactured hemp product. It is the responsibility of any business selling these products to ensure all manufactured hemp products only contain naturally occurring cannabinoids.” In practice, naturally occurring delta-8 is present only in trace amounts; any product with pharmacologically active delta-8 levels will be presumed converted unless the seller substantiates a natural route. Total THC counts delta-8 (see above).
Delta-10 THCSame as delta-8: converted delta-10 is an artificially derived cannabinoid and prohibited (DOH FAQ; HAR §11-37-4(d)). Naturally occurring trace delta-10, if substantiated, follows the natural-route rule.
THCA (as a standalone intoxicating product / THCA flower)No lawful retail category. THCA is counted toward total THC (×0.877). Smokable THCA flower is hemp flower/leaf and prohibited (see below). A manufactured product containing THCA must still meet ≤0.3% total THC and the mg caps; heating/conversion does not create a separate exemption.
THCO, HHC, THC-P, THC-H, THCjd, HHCO, synthetic cannabinoidsProhibited as artificially derived / synthetic cannabinoids (DOH FAQ Q15, opened directly; HAR §11-37-4(d)).
CBD (naturally occurring)Allowed in compliant manufactured hemp products; no Hawai‘i mg cap on CBD has been set (DOH FAQ Q5). CBD in a non-compliant form (vape, smokable, food) is still prohibited by form, even if CBD itself is not capped.
CBN created from CBDProhibited as artificially derived (DOH FAQ Q4/Q15). Naturally occurring CBN, if substantiated, follows the natural-route rule — Verify how DOH treats CBN in practice, as CBN is commonly produced by degradation/oxidation.

DOH consumer FAQ, opened directly (current as of 16 Dec 2025): health.hawaii.gov — Consumers of MHP FAQ (accessed 1 Nov 2026). This is the clearest official plain-English source for the table above and should be cited alongside HAR chapter 11-37 in Run 2.

Smokable hemp ban — yes, and it predates Act 269

  • HAR §11-37-3(f): “No person shall sell, hold for sale, offer, or distribute any hemp leaf, hemp floral material, including but not limited to hemp cigars or hemp cigarettes, or viable hemp seeds, except for sale… to (1) A hemp producer holding a [USDA] license… or (2) A permitted hemp processor.”
  • HAR §11-37-3(e): no hemp-derived cannabinoid / artificially derived / synthetic product “used to aerosolize for respiratory routes of delivery, such as an inhaler, vape pen, or other device.”
  • HAR §11-37-3(g): no manufactured product containing living hemp plants, viable seeds, leaf or floral materials.
  • DOH FAQ Q4 (opened directly): “All viable hemp seeds, hemp flower or leaf material, including hemp pre-rolls, regardless of the labeled percentage or amount of THC or THCA” are prohibited for sale; “All vape cartridges, concentrates, etc. purporting to contain cannabinoids, including CBD” are prohibited.
  • Secondary reporting (High Times, 2026: High Times — Hawaii smokable hemp enforcement, accessed 1 Nov 2026) quotes DOH: Act 14 (2020) already prohibited sale of hemp leaf/floral material intended to be smoked/inhaled and cannabinoid products for aerosolized delivery; Act 269 (2025) did not newly ban flower/pre-rolls/vapes, it created registration and expanded inspection/seizure. Do not date the smokable ban to 2025 or 2026. Enforcement intensity changed in 2026; the prohibition did not.

DOH hemp product rules — allowed forms, prohibited ingredients, labelling, testing (summary of HAR chapter 11-37, Jan 2026 compilation)

Allowed manufactured hemp products (§11-37-4(c)): (1) edible types specified in §11-37-5; (2) topical form for skin/hair. Edible types (§11-37-5(a)): tablets, capsules, powders (each serving sealed in a separate packet), softgels, gelcaps, oil-based tinctures (see caps below), gummies, beverages (see caps below).

Prohibited ingredients (§11-37-4(d)): unlisted colour additives; bithionol; vinyl chloride; halogenated salicylanilides; chloroform; methylene chloride; prohibited cattle material; mercury compounds; hexachlorophene; ephedrine alkaloids; synthetic cannabinoids; artificially derived cannabinoids; tobacco, nicotine, caffeine, alcohol, kava, or any other non-cannabis substance that is psychoactive or would increase potency/toxicity/addictive potential or create a potentially unsafe combination; any ingredient DOH determines injurious/hazardous.

Child-appeal ban (§11-37-4(e)): products shall not be designed to resemble commercially available candy or child-marketed products, nor be in the shape of / depict a human, animal or fruit (including cartoon/caricature renderings); “candy”/“candies” shall not be used on packaging, labelling, advertising, product lists or menus.

THC mg caps (§11-37-5, verbatim in substance, Jan 2026 compilation): tinctures: max 1 ounce per container; max 3 mg total THC per serving; no non-hemp-derived flavours, sugars or sweeteners; packaged with dropper/spray top; labelled for use in drops/dropperfuls/sprays. DOH FAQ adds: up to 85 mg per 1-oz bottle (see §0.9 flag). Beverages: 6–12 fl oz per container; no more than 0.5 mg total THC per container. Other edibles (tablets, capsules, softgels, gelcaps, gummies, powder packets): no more than 1 mg total THC per serving (one serving = one tablet/capsule/softgel/gelcap/gummy/packet); no more than 5 mg total THC per multi-serving package. All edibles: ≤0.3% total THC (§11-37-5(c)). Edibles must be homogeneous, made only with food-safe components, not be time/temperature-control-for-safety products; multi-serving packages must meet child-resistant special packaging (16 CFR part 1700) (§11-37-5(d)–(e)). Products for sale outside Hawai‘i (§11-37-6): a processor may manufacture/hold products exceeding the §11-37-5(a)–(b) per-serving/package/container THC limits, at ≤0.3% total THC, for sale outside the State, complying with all other chapter requirements. This is an export-manufacturing allowance, not a Hawai‘i retail allowance. Verify whether the forthcoming federal 0.4 mg/container cap affects this export allowance after 11 December 2026.

Minimum age: tinctures: 21+ only, ID check required (HRS §328G-13; HAR §11-37-15; DOH FAQ: “Restricted sale to consumer 21 or older — I.D. check required”; retailer registration not required for tincture-only sellers in DOH FAQ, but the 21+ rule still applies). Other manufactured hemp products: DOH FAQ requires the label statement “Not for sale to persons under the age of 21” (or similar) and “Keep out of reach of children.” Whether that label statement is also a universal sale prohibition is Verify (see §1.4 minors note). No minimum age for medical cannabis patients is 21 — minors may be patients with parental consent (§1.4); do not conflate hemp tincture 21+ with medical patient age. Registration exceptions (DOH FAQ Q16): from January 2026, retailers/distributors/online sellers need a Certificate of Registration unless an exception applies (sale of tinctures, topicals, GRAS products, or industrial hemp). The full exception list in HRS §328G-9 was not opened in full — Verify before advising any seller they are excepted.

Testing and labelling (HAR chapter 11-37, subchapters 3–4; DOH FAQ Q9, Q13): batch testing required for cannabinoid content and contaminants (solvents, pesticides, heavy metals, microbials, mycotoxins) before sale; no sale of product failing subchapter 3 testing (§11-37-3(b)). Each package must be labelled with: product name, net weight, ingredient statement (hemp ingredient as “Hemp [+ part of plant used]”), allergen declaration if applicable, responsibility statement (manufacturer/distributor name/address), batch number, QR code or website to access batch-matched lab results, directions for use, serving size/number of servings (edibles), cannabinoid content in mg per serving (edibles: total THC + any advertised cannabinoid) or per container (topicals), and statements: “This product is not intended to diagnose, treat, cure, or prevent any disease” (edibles), “Use under guidance of physician if you have medical condition or are pregnant or lactating,” “Keep out of reach of children,” “Not for sale to persons under the age of 21,” “For external use only” (topicals). Packaging/labelling/advertising shall not make unsubstantiated, false or misleading health/benefit claims (§11-37-3(k)), except for FDA-approved cannabinoid drugs. No samples from open packages (§11-37-3(j)); samples in compliant sealed packaging must meet all subchapters 1–4 requirements. No food (HRS §328-1) into which a cannabinoid / artificially derived / synthetic cannabinoid / hemp / hemp biomass / manufactured product has been added may be sold, except as allowed by §11-37-5, and except for FDA-GRAS hemp as intended in a public GRAS notification (§11-37-3(c)).

THC beverages — Hawai‘i position: hemp beverages are an allowed manufactured form, but only at 0.5 mg total THC per 6–12 fl oz container and ≤0.3% total THC. This is not a hemp-THC-beverage market as understood on the mainland: a typical 5 mg or 10 mg hemp THC seltzer is prohibited in Hawai‘i. After 11 December 2026, the federal baseline cap (0.4 mg/container) would, if applied as described, make even Hawai‘i’s 0.5 mg beverage federally non-compliant as hemp — no Hawai‘i conforming amendment was located (§0.10). Do not market Hawai‘i as a hemp-beverage state without both caps.

State response to the new federal hemp definition and the 12 November / 11 December 2026 dates: None located as of 1 November 2026. No Hawai‘i statute, HAR amendment, DOH guidance, or AG opinion responding to PL 119-37 §781 or PL 119-103 §2019 was found in opened sources. Hawai‘i’s total-THC definition (THCA×0.877 + delta-9 + delta-8) already anticipates a total-THC approach, but its mg caps and its delta-8 counting differ from the federal baseline as summarised, and its 0.3% threshold differs from any federal change that would redefine hemp itself. The 27-day gap (12 Nov vs 11 Dec) for synthetically derived cannabinoids in secondary federal summaries was not verified against Hawai‘i law and is not adopted as a Hawai‘i rule. Recommended follow-up for Run 2 / Part 7: check capitol.hawaii.gov and health.hawaii.gov — Hemp Program after 11 December 2026 for any emergency rule, guidance, or 2027 bill conforming chapter 328G / HAR chapter 11-37 to the federal definition; check whether the chapter 328G 1 July 2027 repeal note interacts with any conforming bill.

1.7 Packaging, labelling, advertising, delivery, zoning and buffer rules

Medical cannabis — packaging / labelling / advertising

  • Statutory duty: DOH “shall establish standards regarding the advertising and packaging of cannabis and manufactured cannabis products” (§329D-7, via 2023 bill text HB 1218, accessed 1 Nov 2026, §329D-7(13)–(14) context: packaging standards sufficient for law enforcement to reasonably determine contents of an unopened package; signage size/format limits complying with §329D-6(o)(2) and not including cartoon/child-appealing designs).
  • HAR chapter 11-850 implementation: labelling for retail sale is HAR §11-850-143 — the most common violation category in DOH’s 2024 enforcement data (DOH Jan 2025 annual report). Full labelling content requirements (potency, batch, warnings, etc.) are in HAR chapter 11-850 and were not opened in full in this run — Verify for Part 11. Do not import hemp labelling rules into medical labelling.
  • Signage (§329D-6(o), quoted in §1.2B): no display in windows/public view; one or two signs only, each ≤1,600 square inches, business/trade name in text only, no pictures/illustrations; stricter local signage law governs if more restrictive.
  • Advertising (November 2025 HAR amendments, DOH release): prohibited depictions in advertising, signage, menus, or waiting-room displays of: cannabis use, children, celebrities, influencers, cartoons appealing to children, or products resembling commercial food/candy.
  • Waiting room: storage, display and retail sale prohibited in the waiting room; a designated caregiver may accompany a patient there (Act 241, SLH 2025 §7, amending §329D-1 “waiting room”; November 2025 HAR amendments).
  • Hours: retail sales only 8:00 a.m.–8:00 p.m. HST, Monday–Sunday (§329D-6(e)).

Medical cannabis — delivery

Prohibited: “A dispensary shall be prohibited from off-premises delivery” (§329D-6(n), quoted above). No patient delivery service, courier delivery, or mail delivery of medical cannabis is lawful under chapter 329D as reproduced. Dispensary-to-dispensary transport and lab-sample transport are the only transport routes (§1.5).

Medical cannabis — zoning and buffers

  • County zoning compliance required (§329D-22(a), quoted above).
  • Production centres: permitted in any area where agricultural production is permitted, except as provided in chapter 329D.
  • Buffer: 750 feet from real property comprising a playground or school — for both production centres and dispensaries. No other statewide buffer (e.g. church, park, residential) is stated in §329D-22 as reproduced. County zoning may be stricter (Part 3 question; do not assume counties have not added buffers).
  • Hemp cultivation buffers (historical Act 14 text, superseded — current law is 300/100 feet, §141-42): 500 feet (historical only) from pre-existing playground/childcare/school and from pre-existing non-licensee residential structures, with pilot-programme grandfathering (§1.2D). Do not apply the 500-foot hemp buffer to medical dispensaries or vice versa.

Hemp — packaging / labelling / advertising

See §1.6: child-resistant packaging (edibles), child-appeal bans, “candy” word ban, required label elements and QR/website lab access, no unsubstantiated health claims, no open-package sampling, Certificate of Registration display (HAR §11-37-12: display at the registered location and on the website if applicable — full text Verify against the Jan 2026 compilation).

1.8 Seed-to-sale tracking system and requirements

  • Statutory requirement (§329D-6(k), quoted in substance in §1.2B): every dispensary licensee “shall purchase, operate, and maintain a computer software tracking system” tracking cannabis from seed or immature-plant state, including cuttings/cloning derivatives, until sold or destroyed under §329D-7. The system must also track: total amount in possession of all dispensaries; total manufactured-product inventory including equivalent cannabis weight; and amounts purchased by each patient/caregiver/out-of-state patient/caregiver from all retail locations in any 15-day period (for dispensing-limit enforcement) (§329D-6(k) / (j) context, Justia 2025 §329D-6 reproduction).
  • Alternative tracking if the system fails: if DOH’s computer software tracking system is inoperable, DOH may implement an alternate tracking system so dispensaries need not cease operations (§329D-6(k)(3) context, same reproduction).
  • Transport manifests: dispensary-to-dispensary sales require a transport manifest submitted to DOH before sale, with a completed manifest within one week after sale, and notice of changes before transport (November 2025 HAR amendments, DOH release).
  • Vendor — secondary only, Verify against OMCCR: secondary compliance databases list Hawai‘i’s state system as BioTrack (now BT Government, Inc., the BioTrack/Metrc government joint entity formed August 2025). No opened DOH/OMCCR source in this run names the vendor, contract, or API. Do not state “Hawai‘i uses BioTrack/Metrc” as an officially verified fact in Run 2 without an OMCCR or procurement source. The statutory obligation (a DOH tracking system exists and dispensaries must use it) is verified; the vendor name is not. Part 5 notes the same caution: BioTrack is a licensee/regulator system, not a public source, and any monthly BioTrack sales figure in secondary reporting is secondary reporting of BioTrack data, not a DOH publication.
  • Patient home grows: home-grow tracking is by 329-card grow-site designation and plant tagging (HAR §11-160-31(b)), not by the dispensary seed-to-sale system. Out-of-state patients may not grow and are not in the home-grow tracking system.

1.9 Employment and drug-testing protections for patients

Current law — what §329-125.5 actually says (Act 242, SLH 2015, as amended 2017 c. 170 §2 and 2018 c. 116 §8; text via bill reproductions and secondary summaries, with operative subsections quoted below — verify against current capitol.hawaii.gov §329-125.5 before Run 2):

“(a) No school shall refuse to enroll or otherwise penalize, and no landlord shall refuse to lease property to or otherwise penalize, a person solely for the person’s status as a qualifying patient or primary caregiver in the medical cannabis program under this part, unless failing to do so would cause the school or landlord to lose a monetary or licensing-related benefit under federal law or regulation; provided that the qualifying patient or primary caregiver strictly complied with the requirements of this part; provided further that the qualifying patient or primary caregiver shall present a medical cannabis registry card or certificate and photo identification, to ensure that the qualifying patient or primary caregiver is validly registered with the department of health pursuant to section 329-123.”
“(b) For the purposes of medical care, including organ transplants, a registered qualifying patient’s use of cannabis in compliance with this part shall be considered the equivalent of the use of any other medication under the direction of a physician and shall not constitute the use of an illicit substance or otherwise disqualify a registered qualifying patient from medical care.”
“(c) No qualifying patient or primary caregiver under this part shall be denied custody of, visitation with, or parenting time with a minor, and there shall be no presumption of neglect or child endangerment, for conduct allowed under this part; provided that this subsection shall not apply if the qualifying patient’s or primary caregiver’s conduct created a danger to the safety of the minor, as established by a preponderance of the evidence.”
“(d) This section shall apply to qualifying patients, primary caregivers, qualifying out-of-state patients, and caregivers of qualifying out-of-state patients who are validly registered with the department of health pursuant to this part and the administrative rules of the department of health.”

Employment — the gap: current §329-125.5 as reproduced above contains NO employer anti-discrimination clause and NO drug-test clause. The employer clause appears only in bills that would amend §329-125.5 (see below). Secondary guides that state Hawai‘i already prohibits employer discrimination against patients are overstating current law on the evidence opened in this run.

  • Dispensary employment is separately regulated and is an exception, not a protection: dispensaries shall deny employment to persons convicted of murder (any degree), class A/B felonies, or class C felonies involving trafficking/distributing/promoting Schedule I/II substances other than cannabis within the last 10 years; and may deny employment for class C felonies involving fraud/deceit/misrepresentation/embezzlement/theft or endangering a minor (§329D-6(d), Justia 2025 reproduction). Dispensary employment is exempt from HRS §378-2(a)(1) (arrest/court-record discrimination) and §378-2.5 as stated in §329D-6(d).
  • Workplace use: medical use “in the workplace of one’s employment” is expressly not authorised (§329-122(e)(2)(B)). A patient has no right to use cannabis at work under Part IX.
  • Drug testing: no statute located in this run prohibits an employer from acting on a positive cannabis drug test for a patient, or requires impairment-based testing, as current law. Courts have, per SB 2998 findings (2026 bill text, cited below), “consistently ruled in favor of employers when qualifying patients challenge drug-free workplace policies.” That finding is a legislative finding in a bill, not a court holding cited here — Verify case law before citing any case.

Pending employment bills (NOT law): HB 325 (2025, HD1, carried to 2026) and SB 2998 (2026, introduced 23 Jan 2026): both would add a new §329-125.5 subsection prohibiting employer discrimination in hiring, termination, or any term/condition of employment based on (1) status as a medical cannabis registry card holder, or (2) a registered patient’s positive drug test for cannabis components/metabolites, unless the patient was impaired during work hours or in a potentially dangerous occupation, and unless non-discrimination would cause loss of a monetary/licensing benefit under contract or federal law; SB 2998 would also authorise fit-for-duty testing in potentially dangerous occupations and prohibit professional/vocational licensing boards from revoking/disciplining a patient for lawful medical use (with an impairment exception). SB 2998 findings state: “Although the medical use of cannabis has become increasingly accepted, qualifying patients in the State continue to risk losing their jobs and occupational licenses because the State lacks clear protections against employment and licensing discrimination” and “twenty-four of the forty states with medical cannabis laws have medical cannabis anti-discrimination employment protections.” Official bill text: SB 2998 (2026) bill PDF (accessed 1 Nov 2026); HB 325 HD1: HB 325 HD1 PDF (accessed 1 Nov 2026). Neither bill’s final 2026 disposition was confirmed on a capitol measure page in this run — status Verify; do not present either as enacted. Earlier employment bill HB 673 (2019) proposed a similar HRS chapter 378 clause; it was not enacted in the form reviewed and is not current law.

Other protections (for Part 12 cross-reference, stated here only to avoid contradiction): schools and landlords: protected as in §329-125.5(a) above, subject to the federal-benefit exception and strict-compliance + card/photo-ID presentation conditions. Federally subsidised housing is the classic federal-benefit exception — secondary guidance notes patients in federally subsidised housing may not have the landlord protection; Verify against HUD guidance / HRS §521-39 in Part 12. Medical care / organ transplants: protected as in §329-125.5(b) above. Custody/visitation/parenting time: protected as in §329-125.5(c) above, unless danger to the minor is proved by a preponderance. Treating physicians/APRNs: §329-126 protections for certifying providers (full text Verify — not opened in this run beyond the telehealth amendment in Act 241 §6). Dispensary owners/employees: §329-125.6 protections (full text Verify).

1.10 Pending legislation — bill numbers, summaries, status

Hawai‘i biennium note: Hawai‘i’s Legislature sits in a two-year biennium (33rd Legislature: 2025–2026). A bill introduced in 2025 that was not enacted in 2025 may carry over to 2026 (recorded as “Carried over to 2026 Regular Session” on 8 Dec 2025 for several 2025 bills). Carry-over does not mean enactment, and a bill that does not pass by the end of the biennium (sine die, 2026) dies and must be reintroduced in the next biennium. Exact 2026 sine die / deadline dates were not verified against the capitol calendar in this run — Verify if Run 2 needs deadline analysis. No bill below is current law unless its status is stated as “Enacted (Act …).”

Bills that are NOT cannabis bills (correction — do not include in a cannabis pending list)

  • SB 3199 (2026) is a mental-health emerging therapies (psychedelics) task-force bill (psilocybin/MDMA breakthrough therapies), not a cannabis bill, despite appearing in some 2026 cannabis round-ups. It passed both chambers in different forms and went to conference (last conference action 29 Apr 2026, reconvene 1 May 2026, per LegiScan: LegiScan — SB 3199, accessed 1 Nov 2026); no Act is recorded in the sources reviewed. It must not be listed as a cannabis task-force bill.
  • HB 496 / Act 242, SLH 2025 (māmaki tea) was signed on the same day as HB 302 but is not a cannabis bill. Do not confuse Act 242, SLH 2025 (māmaki) with Act 242, SLH 2015 (medical-cannabis non-discrimination).

Interisland-transport bills — none enacted found

No 2025 or 2026 bill that would authorise patient/caregiver interisland transport was identified as enacted in this run. Act 241 (2025) clarified dispensary-to-dispensary transport only. A 2018 bill (HB 2740) that would have amended §329-122(f) interisland language was reviewed as a historical draft only and is not law. Search gap: a dedicated capitol keyword search for “interisland” across 2025–2026 measures was not completed in this run — Run 2 should run that search before stating definitively that no interisland bill was introduced.

PART 2 — TAXES

Method note for this Part: an attempt to open the DOH January 2026 annual report PDF directly failed in the Parts 2–3 pass and was not retried. Figures attributed to that report below are therefore taken from search-indexed excerpts of the report itself, not from a fresh full-page read in that pass. They are marked accordingly and should be re-verified against the PDF before Run 2 is finalised. Report PDF: DOH Annual Report Jan 2026 (PDF). The Parts 4–5 pass did open that PDF and confirms the same totals (see Part 5.2) — the totals are therefore adopted, with the PDF-recheck still listed as a gap.

2.1 Headline: there is NO cannabis-specific excise tax in current Hawai‘i law

Hawai‘i has no separate state cannabis excise tax, no adult-use tax, and no hemp-specific product tax in force. Medical cannabis sold by a licensed dispensary is taxed like other retail business activity: under the General Excise Tax (GET), Hawai‘i Revised Statutes (HRS) Chapter 237, plus a county surcharge where adopted. DOH, January 2026 annual report (search excerpt): “A 4% general excise tax is assessed on cannabis and manufactured cannabis products purchased at the licensed dispensaries by qualifying registered patients.” — DOH Annual Report to the 2026 Legislature, January 2026, DOH Annual Report Jan 2026 (PDF) — accessed via search excerpt 1 Nov 2026; full PDF not re-opened in the Parts 2–3 pass — Verify against PDF. Secondary summary consistent with this, but not a substitute for the statute: “Medical cannabis is subject only to Hawaii’s standard excise tax of 4% (4.5% in Oahu), without additional cannabis-specific taxes.” — HawaiiStateCannabis.org, Cannabis Business in Hawaii (accessed 1 Nov 2026).

2.2 The General Excise Tax (GET) — what it is, base, and rates

2.2.1 Nature of the tax — it is a tax on the business, not a sales tax on the customer

Department of Taxation (DOTAX), General Excise Tax (GET) Information, page last updated 27 August 2026, opened and read 1 Nov 2026: tax.hawaii.gov — GET:

“The General Excise Tax (GET) is Hawaii’s tax on business activity. It is imposed on the gross income or gross receipts of virtually all business activity conducted in Hawaii, including sales of goods, services, rents, commissions, construction, and other business transactions. Unlike a traditional sales tax, GET is levied on the business rather than the customer, though many businesses choose to pass the cost on to customers.”
“GET is NOT a sales tax.” … “Businesses may choose to pass the GET on to customers, but they are not required to do so.” … “If GET is passed on to customers, it must be included in gross receipts.”
“Gross income is the total of all business income before deducting business expenses.”

Consequence for dispensaries: GET is charged on gross receipts, not on profit. Rent, payroll, product cost, and other expenses do not reduce the GET base. This is distinct from income-tax deductions, addressed at §2.7 below.

2.2.2 Statutory rates by activity — HRS Chapter 237

DOTAX GET page, opened 1 Nov 2026, table as published:

Activity typeTax rate as published by DOTAX
Selling retail goods and services, renting, leasing real property, construction contracting, earning commissions, all others4.5%*
Wholesaling goods, producing, providing wholesale services, business activities of disabled people0.50%
Insurance commissions0.15%
“*This 4.5% rate includes a 0.5% county surcharge. The county surcharge does not apply to activities taxed at the 0.5% rate (for example selling wholesale goods) or insurance commissions.” — tax.hawaii.gov — GET

Application to cannabis, stated plainly: retail sale to a patient/caregiver at a retail dispensing location: 4% state GET + 0.5% county surcharge = 4.5% in every county as of 2026 (see §2.3 for the Maui timing correction). Wholesale / producing activity taxed at the 0.5% rate: no county surcharge applies. DOTAX states this expressly. Whether a particular vertically-integrated dispensary transaction is characterised as retail (4%) or wholesale/producing (0.5%) is a DOTAX classification question for the transaction — Verify for any inter-dispensary or production-centre transfer, as DOH’s annual report describes only the retail-patient sale at 4%. There is no published DOTAX cannabis-specific GET ruling or cannabis industry guide located in this research — Verify / Unavailable if the parent run requires a DOTAX cannabis guidance document. Do not cite a general GET guide as cannabis-specific guidance.

2.2.3 Registration, returns, and penalties — general GET machinery that applies to dispensaries

From DOTAX GET page, opened 1 Nov 2026, tax.hawaii.gov — GET:

  • Register by completing Form BB-1, State of Hawaii Basic Business Application, and paying a one-time $20 licence fee. Submission in person, by mail, or online via Hawaii Tax Online (5–7 business days online; 4–6 weeks by mail as published).
  • A branch licence (Form G-50) is required for each additional place of business / trade name; no additional charge for a branch licence. This matters for a licensee with multiple retail locations and production centres.
  • Periodic return Form G-45: semi-annual if expected annual GET liability ≤ $2,000; quarterly if ≤ $4,000; monthly if > $4,000. Monthly filers must file online. Deadline is the 20th day after the period ends.
  • Annual return Form G-49, due the 20th day of the fourth month after the tax year ends (20 April for calendar-year filers).
  • Late filing: 5% of unpaid tax per month, up to 25%; late payment: 20% penalty if tax on a timely filed return is not paid within 60 days; interest 2/3 of 1% per month on unpaid tax and penalties; plus 2% for failure to e-file/e-pay when required and 10% for failure to properly complete Part V, as published.

A dispensary applicant must also already hold a GET licence as part of the HRS §329D-3/§329D-4 licensing package (see Part 4; the GET licence itself is the $20 DOTAX licence above, not the $75,000 dispensary licence fee in §2.5).

2.2.4 No GET exemption for medical cannabis found in current law

No current HRS §237 exemption for sales of medical cannabis by chapter 329D dispensaries was located. A proposed exemption — SB 2537 (2018), which would have added HRS §237-__ providing “This chapter shall not apply to amounts received by medical cannabis dispensaries licensed under chapter 329D for sales of cannabis or manufactured cannabis products intended for medical use to qualifying patients or primary caregivers” — was not enacted and must not be cited as law. Source: SB 2537 text, SB 2537 (2018) text (accessed 1 Nov 2026). Status: PROPOSED ONLY, 2018, not law — Verify on capitol.hawaii.gov if a later exemption bill is to be tracked.

2.3 County surcharge on GET — HRS §§46-16.8, 237-8.6, 238-2.6

2.3.1 Rate, base, and sunset

  • Rate: 0.5% on top of the 4% state rate, only on activities taxed at the 4% rate. It does not apply to the 0.5% wholesale/producing rate or the 0.15% insurance rate.
  • Combined retail rate: 4.5%.
  • Maximum visible pass-on rate: 4.7120%. This is not a second statutory rate. Because tax passed on to the customer is itself gross income and therefore itself taxable, 4.5% ÷ (1 − 0.045) = 4.712%. DOTAX publishes 4.7120% as the maximum pass-on rate. Businesses may absorb the tax instead of passing it on.
  • Authorised through 31 December 2030 in all four counties, per DOTAX county-surcharge table as summarised in multiple DOTAX-derived sources and in the State Procurement Office table dated 24 February 2026 (see below).

DOTAX county-surcharge page (referenced by DOTAX and by counties): tax.hawaii.gov — county surcharge — not separately opened in full in the Parts 2–3 pass; the table values below are corroborated by the county and State Procurement sources cited — Verify the DOTAX table itself in Run 2. State Procurement Office table, 24 February 2026, SPO table, 24 Feb 2026 (PDF) (accessed 1 Nov 2026), reproducing DOTAX rates:

CountyCounty surchargeState GETMax pass-onSurcharge expiry
City and County of Honolulu0.50%4.0%4.7120%12/31/2030
Hawai‘i (Hawaii County)0.50%4.0%4.7120%12/31/2030
County of Maui (including Moloka‘i and Lāna‘i)0.50%4.0%4.7120%12/31/2030
Kaua‘i0.50%4.0%4.7120%12/31/2030

2.3.2 Adoption history by county — and a material conflict in the DOH report

CountySurcharge startAuthority / evidence
City and County of Honolulu (O‘ahu)1 January 2007Ordinance adopted 2005 (DOTAX testimony on HB 2587 SD1, 4 Apr 2018, DOTAX testimony, HB 2587 SD1); extended by Ordinance 16-1; authority extended to 31 Dec 2030. Purpose: rail transit.
Kaua‘i County1 January 2019Kaua‘i Ordinance No. 1021 (11 Dec 2017), per DOTAX testimony above. DOTAX table shows 4.4386% pass-on for the stub period 1 Jan–31 Dec 2019 in some secondary reproductions, then 4.7120% from 2020 — Verify the 2019 stub rate against the DOTAX table; it does not affect the current 4.5% rate.
Hawai‘i County0.25% in calendar 2019, then 0.50% from 1 January 2020Per DOTAX-derived tables (e.g. APM Help — Hawaii taxes, summarising DOTAX). Verify the 2019 0.25% step against the DOTAX table. Current rate is 0.50%.
Maui County (Maui, Moloka‘i, Lāna‘i)1 January 2024, through 31 December 2030Maui County Council Bill 49 (2023), Ordinance No. 5551, signed 19 July 2023. County of Maui news release, 19 Jan 2024, Maui County newsflash; and Maui County Council release, 7 Feb 2024, Maui County Council release (accessed 1 Nov 2026). Purpose as stated by the County: housing infrastructure (water, drainage, sewers, waste disposal/treatment, pedestrian paths/sidewalks).

CONFLICT — flagged, not resolved by choosing silently: the DOH January 2026 annual report excerpt states: “The City and County of Honolulu, County of Kauai, and County of Hawaii have current additional 0.5% surcharges … The County of Maui has not adopted a county surcharge.” That statement is stale or incorrect as of 1 January 2024 against the DOTAX table, the Maui County Council release, and the Maui County government news release above, all of which show Maui at 0.5% from 1 January 2024. Resolution adopted for this report: DOTAX / Maui County (the taxing authorities) control for the tax rate; DOH controls only for the collections series in §2.6, whose Maui component for 2024–2025 must therefore be treated as Verify as to whether it includes the Maui surcharge. The same stale “Maui has no surcharge” line is repeated in secondary summaries that copy the DOH report (e.g. CannBus Q3 2026) and should not be relied on for the current rate.

Illustration (DOH-report style, updated for Maui): on a $100 pre-tax retail sale, 4% state GET = $4.00, 0.5% surcharge = $0.50, total tax cost $4.50, for a $104.50 total if the full statutory amount is passed on as a tax amount (before the gross-up arithmetic that produces the 4.712% maximum visible pass-on). This is an illustration, not a price control — a dispensary may absorb GET.

2.3.3 What the surcharge revenue is for, and who keeps what

  • The surcharge is a state-administered county surcharge, not a separate county tax return. DOTAX levies, assesses, collects, and administers it, then remits to the county, retaining an administrative share (secondary summaries cite 1% retained for administration under HRS §248-2.6 — Verify against current HRS §248-2.6 text).
  • Uses are county-specific and set by ordinance / authorising statute: Honolulu — mass transit / rail; Maui — housing infrastructure; Kaua‘i and Hawai‘i County — transportation / county infrastructure as authorised at adoption. Verify each county’s current authorised use against its surcharge ordinance before stating a use for a future year, because the Legislature has amended permitted uses (e.g. Act 48, SLH 2023, for Maui housing infrastructure).
  • Multi-county businesses must allocate gross income by taxation district on Form G-75 with Forms G-45/G-49. This applies to a dispensary group only if it operates in more than one county — which HRS §329D-2(e) prohibits for a single licensee (“No person may be granted a dispensary license in more than one county”), but which can arise for a parent, subcontractor, or affiliated group. Verify Form G-75 treatment for affiliated licensees with DOTAX.

2.5 Dispensary, laboratory, and programme fees (fees, not taxes — but routinely confused with taxes)

Fees are not GET. They are deposited in the Medical Cannabis Registry and Regulation Special Fund (HRS §321-30.1), not the general fund (see §2.8). The two fee regimes must not be conflated: the statutory $50,000 renewal figure is superseded in practice by the HAR formula below.

2.5.1 Statutory application and initial licence fees — HRS §329D-4

HRS §329D-4 (2022 text via Justia; last amendment history in the section note must be re-checked against the current capitol.hawaii.gov text): Justia — §329D-4 (2022) (accessed 1 Nov 2026).

“(c) A nonrefundable application fee of $5,000 for each license application shall be submitted to the department by certified or cashier’s check. Within seven days of approval, a dispensary license fee of $75,000 for each license approved shall be submitted to the department by certified or cashier’s check or the department shall issue a license to the next qualified applicant.”
“(d) All fees collected pursuant to this section shall be deposited in the medical cannabis registry and regulation special fund pursuant to section 321-30.1.”
“(n) A dispensary license may be renewed annually by payment of an annual renewal fee of $50,000 and subject to verification by the department through an unannounced inspection that the individual licensee and entity licensee continue to meet all licensing requirements from the date the initial licenses were issued.” — Note: subsection (n) as printed in the 2022 Justia text states $50,000, but HRS §329D-7 as amended now authorises a rule-based fee structure capped as described in §2.5.2, and HAR §11-850-28 implements that structure. The $50,000 figure should therefore be treated as the historic statutory cap/formula starting point, not as the amount any current licensee actually pays. Verify the current §329D-4(n) / §329D-7(2) text on capitol.hawaii.gov.

2.5.2 Current renewal / additional-location fee formula — HAR §11-850-28

Hawaii Administrative Rules §11-850-28, License renewal fee, effective 17 November 2022 (as compiled 7 August 2023 / 5 April 2025 in secondary compilations): Cornell — HAR §11-850-28 and Justia — HAR §11-850-28 (accessed 1 Nov 2026).

“(a) The license renewal fee shall be calculated by the department by determining and adding the applicable base fee for each production center … and the base fee for each retail dispensing location … and discounting the total by the sum of the discount percentages applicable to the licensee …”

Base fees as published:

  • Per production centre (by maximum plants + manufacturing type): up to 2,500 plants: $25,000 / $35,000 / $40,000 (by manufacturing type column); up to 5,000 plants: $50,000 / $60,000 / $65,000; up to 7,500 plants: $75,000 / $85,000 / $90,000.
  • Per retail dispensing location: $20,000 base fee.
  • County market-condition discount: O‘ahu (City and County of Honolulu) 0%; Hawai‘i County 5%; Maui and Kaua‘i discount percentages were truncated in the search excerpt — Verify the full §11-850-28(d) table before quoting a Maui or Kaua‘i discount.
  • Market-share discount: based on the licensee’s prior-year gross sales as a share of all licensees’ prior-year gross sales, as calculated by DOH — full table Verify.
  • New production centre / retail location: an additional, prorated fee on the same base-fee basis, discounted as above, prorated on a calendar-day basis for the remaining licence term, payable before DOH final approval under HAR §§11-850-32 / 11-850-33.

Why this matters for revenue figures: the DOH licensing-fee collections in §2.6 therefore do not equal 8 × $50,000. They reflect this per-site formula.

2.5.3 Testing-laboratory certification fees — Verify

Secondary compilations (e.g. MPP, MPP — dispensary laws: fees and taxes) list a $3,000 testing-facility certification fee and $3,000 renewal fee. This figure was not confirmed against HAR Chapter 11-850 in this session — flagged Verify. Do not present $3,000 as confirmed until HAR Chapter 11-850 laboratory provisions are opened.

2.5.4 Patient registry fee — not a business tax, but part of programme revenue

HRS §321-30.1(c), Justia — §321-30.1 (accessed 1 Nov 2026):

“(c) The department, upon completion of the transfer of the medical use of cannabis program, shall charge a medical cannabis registration fee to each qualifying patient, other than a qualifying out-of-state patient, of no more than $35 per year.”

History note in that section: L 2013, c 177, §2; am L 2015, c 241, §4; am L 2017, c 170, §2; am L 2018, c 116, §3. The $35 figure is a cap, not necessarily the amount currently charged — Verify the current DOH registry fee schedule for the amount actually charged in 2026. See also Part 1.4: total paid online is reported as $38.50 ($35 + $3.50) in most guides, vs $36.05 ($35 + $1.05) in one guide — conflict, Verify.

2.5.5 Hemp fees that touch tax-adjacent revenue — flagged for Part 7, not a hemp tax

  • No Hawaii hemp product tax was located.
  • Secondary sources report: USDA hemp producer licence — no USDA fee; DOH hemp processor registration — $500 nonrefundable (payable to the Hawaii Department of Health, per HawaiiStateCannabis.org, Cannabis Business in Hawaii); and, from 1 January 2026, DOH manufactured-hemp-product distributor/retailer registration — $50 application / $50 renewal, 5-year certificate (per secondary hemp-industry summaries of Act 269 / HB 1482, 2025). All three hemp fee figures are secondary in this report — Verify against DOH/HAR Chapter 11-37 (or successor) and the 2025 Act text before use in Part 7. They are fees, not GET, and hemp sales remain subject to ordinary GET (§2.6.4).
