2.6.3 Sales cross-checks (secondary / commissioned, NOT official sales data)

  • DOH-commissioned economic analysis, Cannabis Public Policy Consulting (CPPC), December 2025, released February 2026: DOH news release 26-012, 4 February 2026, DOH news release 26-012 (PDF) (accessed 1 Nov 2026): “monthly medical cannabis sales total about $5.3 million” and the regulated system captures “approximately 86 to 87 percent of all cannabis spending by registered patients.” Annualised, $5.3m/month ≈ $64m/year, which is consistent with the §2.6.1 cross-check but is a modelled estimate, not a DOTAX/DOH sales total.
  • DOTAX working-group / earlier market estimate (secondary, dated): a DOTAX-authored report summarised in secondary sources put 2021 medical dispensary sales at $50 million, total cannabis market $240 million (79% illicit), legal share 21%. Dated secondary summary — do not use as a 2026 figure; Verify against the original DOTAX report if Run 2 needs a market-size history.
  • No quarterly dispensary sales series, no official price series, and no official tourism-attributed cannabis sales figure were located in DOH/DOTAX sources checked — Unavailable for Part 2. CPPC’s tourism discussion (visitors contributing at least $11.5m/month in a hypothetical adult-use market) is a modelled adult-use scenario, not current medical revenue — and see Part 6.6 for the version conflict ($11.5m in the December 2025 original vs $7.6m in the February/March 2026 update).

2.6.4 Hemp revenue — no official hemp-tax series located

No DOTAX or Department of Agriculture series for GET collected specifically from hemp businesses, hemp acreage tax, or hemp product tax was located. Hemp businesses pay ordinary GET on their gross receipts (retail 4% + surcharge; wholesale/producing 0.5% without surcharge), but DOTAX does not publish a hemp-only GET line in the sources checked — Unavailable. Do not attribute any part of the §2.6.1 dispensary series to hemp.

2.7 IRS §280E and Hawaii state conformity — the most important tax point for Hawaii dispensaries

2.7.1 Federal position (given baseline — not re-researched, flagged only if contradicted)

Per the task’s given federal baseline: state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714); adult-use remains Schedule I; §280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity; adult-use remains fully subject to §280E. Nothing found in this research contradicts that baseline for Hawaii’s state-licensed medical programme. No DOTAX guidance on the Schedule III change was located — Verify whether DOTAX has issued a Tax Information Release on Schedule III / §280E for 2026.

2.7.2 Hawaii already decoupled from §280E for state income tax — before Schedule III

Hawaii did not wait for federal rescheduling to allow state-level deductions. HRS §235-2.4(v), 2025 text, opened and read 1 Nov 2026: Justia — §235-2.4:

“(v) Section 280E (with respect to expenditures in connection with the illegal sale of drugs) of the Internal Revenue Code shall be operative for the purposes of this chapter, except that section 280E shall not be operative with respect to the production and sale of medical cannabis and manufactured cannabis products by dispensaries licensed under chapter 329D and their subcontractors, as defined in section 329D-1.”

Plain English: for Hawaii state income tax only, a chapter 329D dispensary and its §329D-1 subcontractors may deduct ordinary business expenses that federal §280E would otherwise disallow — even while cannabis was federally Schedule I. This is a decoupling provision, not an exemption from Hawaii income tax itself, and not a GET exemption.

History / conformity mechanics: the carve-out appears in the annual conformity bills. The most recent located: HB 2329, HD1, SD1, CD1 (2026), signed by the Governor 26 May 2026 as Act 035, SLH 2026, Relating to Conformity to the Internal Revenue Code, conforming Hawaii law to the Internal Revenue Code as amended as of 31 December 2025, for taxable years beginning after 31 December 2025. Governor’s message GM 1135, GM 1135 (PDF) (accessed 1 Nov 2026). The §280E carve-out is retained in that Act (subsection lettering shifts from (r) in older compilations to (v)/(u) in the 2025–2026 texts — Verify the current lettering in the enrolled Act 035 text, not the Justia compilation, for citation in the final file). The carve-out’s origin is commonly dated to 2016 in secondary §280E surveys (e.g. MPP lists Hawaii among medical states allowing deductions; Cannabusinessplans lists Hawaii (2016) among decoupling states). The original Act number/year for the carve-out was not confirmed against the session law in this session — Verify: check SLH 2016, c. 33 (conformity) and any 2016 §235-2.4 amendment for the first appearance of the §329D carve-out. Hawaii’s conformity is not automatic / rolling: it is re-enacted annually to a fixed IRC date (31 December of the prior year). A future conformity bill that omitted the carve-out, or that conformed to a post-Schedule III IRC in a way that changed §280E’s operation, could change the state result — Verify each year’s conformity Act; do not assume the carve-out is permanent.

2.7.3 What Schedule III changes, and what it does NOT change, for Hawaii

LevelBefore 28 Apr 2026 (Schedule I)After 28 Apr 2026 (Schedule III, medical only)
Federal income tax (§280E)§280E disallowed most deductions for dispensaries§280E generally lifted for state-licensed medical activity (given baseline). Adult-use, if ever legalised without a state decoupling amendment, would remain subject federally.
Hawaii state income tax§280E already inoperative for §329D dispensaries/subcontractors under §235-2.4(v)No change in state result for medical dispensaries — they could already deduct for state purposes. The federal change aligns federal with the state position Hawaii already held. Verify DOTAX has not issued contrary guidance.
Hawaii GETGET on gross receipts, no deduction conceptNo change — GET is not an income tax and §280E never applied to GET. Schedule III does not reduce GET.
Hypothetical adult-use (not law)N/AFederal §280E would still apply (Schedule I). Hawaii §235-2.4(v) as written covers only medical cannabis … by dispensaries licensed under chapter 329D — it does not by its terms cover adult-use establishments. An adult-use law would need its own decoupling amendment. A 2018 proposal (SB 2537) would have extended the carve-out to chapter ___ cannabis establishments — not enacted.

Caveat: this is a research summary, not tax advice. A dispensary’s federal/state filing position for 2026 (a split year: Schedule I until 28 April, Schedule III after) and the treatment of subcontractors, COGS vs. operating expenses, and entity-level (corporate vs. pass-through) issues are Verify with a Hawaii cannabis tax adviser / DOTAX guidance — none of those details were located in official guidance in this session.

2.8 Where the money goes — general fund vs. special fund vs. counties

  • GET (state 4% share): deposited in the state general fund as general GET revenue, not earmarked for cannabis. Secondary summaries that state “revenue from Hawaii’s GET is deposited into a general fund” are consistent with Chapter 237’s general structure, but the specific disposition statute for GET (HRS Chapter 248) was not opened in this session — Verify §§248-2.6 / 237-8.6 disposition language before stating a percentage split.
  • County surcharge (0.5% share): remitted to the county that adopted it, for that county’s authorised purpose (Honolulu transit; Maui housing infrastructure; etc. — §2.3.3). DOTAX retains an administrative share (see §2.3.3 Verify note).
  • Dispensary / programme fees: deposited in the Medical Cannabis Registry and Regulation Special Fund, HRS §321-30.1, in two sub-accounts: (1) registry programme sub-account (patient fees under §321-30.1(c)); (2) dispensary programme sub-account (fees under §329D-4). HRS §321-30.1(a) purposes include establishing/regulating the dispensary system, offsetting registry certificate costs, funding authorised positions/operating costs, and managing the confidential database. Text: Justia — §321-30.1 (accessed 1 Nov 2026). Note: 2025 legislation (Act 269 / HB 1482 and related medical-cannabis bills) is reported in secondary sources to have expanded special-fund uses (e.g. nuisance abatement / Drug Nuisance Abatement Unit funding) — Verify the current §321-30.1(a) text on capitol.hawaii.gov, as the Justia 2025 compilation may predate 2025 amendments.
  • No cannabis revenue is earmarked by current law for social equity, public health, or education funds — those allocations appear only in proposed adult-use bills (§2.9).

2.9 PROPOSED adult-use taxes — labelled PROPOSED, none is law

Do not use any rate in this section as a current rate.

2.9.1 SB 3335 / HB 2600 (2024) — AG-drafted legalisation, Senate-passed, House-blocked — DEAD for 2024

  • Status: SB 3335 passed the Senate 19–6 (March 2024, as reported), advanced through House Judiciary & Hawaiian Affairs and Agriculture & Food Systems committees, then halted in House Finance Committee; House Finance Chair Kyle Yamashita stated the House would not deliberate further in 2024. Not enacted. Not carried over as a 2024 law.
  • Proposed tax (with a conflict to flag): MPP summary of SB 3335 SD1: “Imposes a 10% retail tax on cannabis, in addition to regular state GET taxes (4%).” Allocation in the original summary: 35% regulation, 25% social equity fund, 15% public health/education, 10% public safety special fund, 7.5% cannabis law enforcement special fund, 7.5% cannabis nuisance abatement fund. MPP also notes: “It is unclear if medical cannabis from a dual-licensed store is tax-exempt.” — MPP — HB 2600 / SB 3335 summary (accessed 1 Nov 2026). Reason Foundation testimony on SB 3335: “Sec. 26 outlines a retail excise tax against cannabis goods at the rate of 10%. Cannabis goods would also be subject to the statewide sales and use tax of 4%. Medical cannabis products would be exempt from excise tax.” — Reason — SB 3335 testimony (accessed 1 Nov 2026). Marijuana Moment / MJBizDaily summaries describe the combined burden as 14% (10% excise + 4% GET) for adult-use, 4% for medical. — Marijuana Moment — SB 3335 House panels and MJBizDaily — Senate approves SB 3335 (accessed 1 Nov 2026). Conflict note: the SD1 allocation percentages differ between MPP versions (50% to one fund in the SD1 PDF summary vs. the 35/25/15/10/7.5/7.5 split in the original summary), and appropriations were blanked out by amendment during House consideration. Verify the enrolled SD1 text on capitol.hawaii.gov if Run 2 needs the allocation table; do not quote an allocation as settled.
  • Bottom line for Part 2: 10% + 4% = 14% adult-use, medical at 4% only, is a proposed structure, not a rate a business pays today.

2.9.2 HB 1246 / SB 1613 (2025, carried over to 2026) — PROPOSED, not enacted

  • Status: introduced 23 January 2025; carried over to the 2026 Regular Session on 8 December 2025 (Hawaii biennium carry-over rule). Secondary reporting in 2026 states the House vehicles were effectively dead in February 2026 and the House has never passed a legalisation bill. Final capitol.hawaii.gov status for the 2026 session was not confirmed in the Parts 2–3 pass — Verify on capitol.hawaii.gov before Run 2; do not state a 2026 final disposition from secondary sources alone.
  • Proposed tax: 14% on adult-use retail, 4% on medical — described in the bill’s own summary handout as: “Medical cannabis will be exempted from the additional 10% tax imposed on adult use cannabis but will still incur the 4% cannabis tax that is the equivalent of the General Excise Tax (GET) as it does now.” — HB 1246 Summary Handout, HB 1246 Summary Handout (PDF) (accessed 1 Nov 2026). Secondary summaries agree: “establishes a 14% tax on adult-use cannabis and a 4% tax on medical cannabis” (BillTrack50, BillTrack — HB 1246 and BillTrack — SB 1613).
  • Creates (proposed) the Hawai‘i Cannabis and Hemp Office in the Department of Commerce and Consumer Affairs. Proposed agency, not a current regulator.

2.9.3 HB 1625 / SB 2421 (2026) — contingent legalisation — PROPOSED, deferred/died in committee

  • Status: introduced 21 January 2026. SB 2421: referred to CPN/HHS, WAM/JDC; committee(s) deferred the measure 18 February 2026. HB 1625: referred 26 January 2026 to JHA/AGR, CPC, FIN. LegiScan records both as “died in committee” / Intro Sine Die for the 2026 session. Verify final status on capitol.hawaii.gov. SB 2421 status page: capitol.hawaii.gov — SB 2421 (accessed 1 Nov 2026). HB 1625: LegiScan — HB 1625 (accessed 1 Nov 2026).
  • Proposed tax: official measure description: “Beginning 1/1/2027, establishes taxes on cannabis sales.” The rate(s) were not stated in the measure descriptions located — Verify against the bill text (HB 1625 / SB 2421, 2026) before quoting a rate. Legalisation itself is contingent on federal change or a state constitutional amendment, so even the 1 January 2027 tax date is contingent, not operative.
  • Also proposed (2026, ballot route): HB 1624 / SB 2420, to put adult-use legalisation to voters as a constitutional amendment (effective July 2027 if approved). No tax rate was located for the ballot bills in this session — Verify.

2.9.4 Modelled, not proposed as a bill: CPPC “maximum 15%”

CPPC’s DOH-commissioned December 2025 report modelled adult-use outcomes “after accounting for a maximum 15 percent tax rate on cannabis products” and projected $46–$90 million/month in year-five sales. Marijuana Moment summary, 2026: Marijuana Moment — CPPC summary (accessed 1 Nov 2026). 15% is a modelling assumption, not a bill rate and not current law. Do not conflate it with the 14% in HB 1246/SB 1613 or the 10% + 4% in SB 3335.

2.9.5 Older / other tax proposals — for completeness, all PROPOSED and not law

  • HB 263 HD2 (2017, proposed): would have earmarked a percentage of Chapter 237 tax revenues from medical dispensaries into the §321-30.1 special fund. Not enacted as located — Verify.
  • HB 1369 SD1 (2025): amends enterprise-zone eligibility to exclude medical cannabis dispensary activities from “eligible business activity” — a tax-incentive exclusion, not a cannabis tax. Text: HB 1369 SD1 (PDF) (accessed 1 Nov 2026). Status Verify on capitol.hawaii.gov; do not present as a cannabis tax.

PART 3 — LOCAL RULES

3.0 Jurisdictional premise — read this first, or the rest will be misread

  • Hawaii has no municipal governments below the county level. There are no incorporated cities/towns with their own zoning codes. The four counties are the only local governments with zoning power:
    1. City and County of Honolulu — all of O‘ahu (including Honolulu, Waikīkī, Kāne‘ohe, Kailua (O‘ahu), Kapolei, ‘Aiea, Pearl City, Wai‘anae, etc.).
    2. Hawai‘i County — Hawai‘i Island (including Hilo, Kailua-Kona, Kamuela/Waimea (Hawai‘i Island), Pāhoa, etc.).
    3. Maui County — Maui, Moloka‘i, and Lāna‘i (including Kahului, Wailuku, Kīhei, Lahaina, Makawao, Pā‘ia, Kaunakakai (Moloka‘i), Lāna‘i City).
    4. Kaua‘i County — Kaua‘i and Ni‘ihau (including Līhu‘e, Kapa‘a, Kōloa/Po‘ipū, Hanalei, Waimea (Kaua‘i)).
    5. Kalawao County — the Kalaupapa peninsula on Moloka‘i. It is administered by the State (Department of Health), has no county council or zoning code of its own, and no dispensary licence may be issued for Kalawao (HRS §329D-2(d), quoted at §3.5).
  • Same-name warnings applied in this report: Kailua (O‘ahu, in Honolulu County) ≠ Kailua-Kona (Hawai‘i Island); Waimea (Kaua‘i) ≠ Waimea/Kamuela (Hawai‘i Island). Every place name below is paired with its island/county.
  • Two layers of land-use control sit above the counties: (a) the State Land Use Commission districts under HRS Chapter 205 (Urban, Rural, Agricultural, Conservation); (b) county zoning under HRS §46-4. A use must satisfy both. County zoning cannot authorise a use in a State Conservation District, for example, and State Agricultural District rules (HRS §205-4.5) govern what is permitted on agricultural land at the state level, with counties adding district-level detail.

3.1 Who does what — state vs. county

FunctionState (who)County (who)Interaction / limit
Whether a dispensary may exist at all; how many licences; in which countyDOH under HRS Chapter 329D; §329D-2(d) fixes 8 licences (3 Honolulu, 2 Hawai‘i, 2 Maui, 1 Kaua‘i, 0 Kalawao)None — a county cannot create, increase, or decrease the state licence capCounty zoning cannot be used to nullify a state licence (see §3.2, §46-4(f))
Licensing, inspection, product, testing, tracking, advertising (state rules)DOH / OMCCR under HRS Chapter 329D and HAR Chapter 11-850None — counties do not license dispensariesCounty permits are land-use/building permits, not cannabis licences
Zoning district, setbacks, height, parking, lot coverage, use-permit typeState Land Use District (HRS Ch. 205) sets the outer boundaryCounty council by ordinance under HRS §46-4; county planning department administers (plan approval, use permits, variances, zoning clearance)Dispensary must comply with all county zoning ordinances except as provided in §329D-22 (see §3.2)
Building, electrical, plumbing, grading, fire, SMA / shorelineState building code as adopted by county; state SMA law (HRS Ch. 205A)County building / planning departments issue building permits and SMA permits; Honolulu DPP, Hawai‘i County Planning, Maui Planning, Kaua‘i PlanningStandard construction permitting; no cannabis-specific building code located
Hemp cultivation (land use)USDA Domestic Hemp Production Program licence (federal) + State Land Use / county agricultural zoningCounty agricultural zoning governs whether farming is permitted on the parcel; county may require farm-plan / grading / water approvals as for any farmHemp is an agricultural crop for land-use purposes where permitted; see §3.4
Hemp / CBD retail shops (non-dispensary)DOH hemp-product rules (HAR Ch. 11-37 and successor registration from 1 Jan 2026 — see Part 7) govern product legalityCounty retail zoning governs where a shop may open, as for any retail shop; no county cannabis-specific retail licence was locatedA CBD shop is treated for zoning as retail, not as a §329D dispensary; it cannot sell §329D medical cannabis
Patient / caregiver home grow (land use)HRS Chapter 329 (patient grow) + HRS §329D-24 concepts; DOH registryCounty residential/agricultural zoning and building rules for structures (e.g. greenhouse) as for any home activity; no county home-grow permit was locatedHome grow is a state registry matter; county cannot prohibit it by zoning a residence, but structures must still meet building/zoning rules — Verify for any specific structure
Real property tax, county surcharge useDOTAX administers GET + surchargeCounty council sets real property tax rates and adopts the GET surcharge ordinance; county finance administers property taxSee Part 2, §2.3–§2.4
Hawaiian Home LandsDepartment of Hawaiian Home Lands (DHHL) + Hawaiian Homes Commission — trust land, not county zoningCounty zoning does not apply on Hawaiian Home Lands in the same way; see Part 10 of the full runA proposed 2024 bill (SB 444) would have expressly provided that production centres/dispensaries on Hawaiian Home Lands “shall not be subject to county zoning” — that bill was not enacted as located; do not cite it as law. Verify current DHHL cannabis position in Part 10.

Bottom line: counties control where a state-licensed facility may sit (district, parcel, setbacks, building), not whether the state programme exists, not who holds a licence, and not what products may be sold. That division is enforced by two state pre-emption-style provisions quoted verbatim in §3.2.

3.2 The two state zoning statutes that frame every county rule

3.2.1 HRS §46-4(f) — counties may not prohibit a properly-zoned dispensary/production centre

HRS §46-4 (2025), Justia — §46-4 (accessed 1 Nov 2026). History note: L 2015, c 241, §3; am L 2017, c 170, §2 (last amendment shown in the Justia history for subsection (f)).

“(f) Neither this section nor any other law, county ordinance, or rule shall prohibit the use of land for medical cannabis production centers or medical cannabis dispensaries established and licensed pursuant to chapter 329D; provided that the land is otherwise zoned for agriculture, manufacturing, or retail purposes.”

What this does and does not do: it bars a county ban on a §329D facility if the land is already zoned agriculture, manufacturing, or retail. A county cannot adopt a “no dispensaries anywhere” ordinance for such land. It does not entitle a dispensary to locate on residential, resort, conservation, or other non-qualifying land. The “otherwise zoned” proviso is the limit. It does not exempt the facility from other county rules (setbacks, parking, building, SMA, etc.) — those still apply via §329D-22(a), below.

3.2.2 HRS §329D-22 — medical cannabis zoning (the operative buffer and production-centre rule)

HRS §329D-22 (2025), opened and read in full 1 Nov 2026: Justia — §329D-22:

“§329D-22 Medical cannabis zoning. (a) Medical cannabis production centers and dispensaries shall comply with all county zoning ordinances, rules, or regulations; provided that: (1) A medical cannabis production center shall be permitted in any area in which agricultural production is permitted except as provided within this chapter; and (2) No medical cannabis production center or dispensary shall be permitted within seven hundred fifty feet of the real property comprising a playground or school. (b) As used in this section: ‘Playground’ means any public outdoor facility, including any parking lot appurtenant thereto, that is intended for recreation, with any portion thereof containing three or more separate apparatus intended for the recreation of children, including but not limited to sliding boards, swing sets, and teeterboards. ‘School’ means any public or private preschool, kindergarten, elementary, intermediate, middle, secondary, or high school.”

History: [L 2015, c 241, pt of §2; am L 2017, c 170, §2; am L 2019, c 240, §4; am L 2020, c 70, §49].

Three points that are often misstated — corrected here: (1) The 750-foot buffer is statewide and is the only state buffer in §329D-22. It applies to both production centres and dispensaries (retail locations), measured to the real property comprising the playground/school (i.e. parcel boundary, not building door, as written — Verify measurement practice (parcel line vs. building, straight-line vs. pedestrian route) with the relevant county planning department for any specific site). (2) The 2015 original “pharmacy” clause is gone from the current text. Early drafts and the 2015 SB 1302 text provided a dispensary “shall be permitted in any area in which a pharmacy is permitted.” That clause does not appear in the current §329D-22(a) as opened. Do not cite a pharmacy-permitted rule as current law. The current rule for retail locations is: comply with county zoning + 750-foot buffer + §46-4(f) protection if zoned retail/manufacturing/agriculture. (3) Production centres have a state-level agricultural permission: “shall be permitted in any area in which agricultural production is permitted.” That is stronger than a mere county permission — a county cannot zone out a production centre from an area where it otherwise permits agricultural production, subject to the 750-foot buffer and Chapter 329D itself.

No other statewide buffer was located: no state statute in Chapter 329D imposes a buffer from churches, parks (other than the defined “playground”), day-care centres (other than preschools captured in “school”), residential zones, or other dispensaries. If a county imposes an additional buffer by ordinance, it would be a county rule, not a state rule — none was located in this research (see §3.3). Verify against each county’s current zoning code before advising on a site.

3.3 Official list of county rules — there is NO single official list; jurisdictions checked

Finding: no state agency publishes a consolidated list of county cannabis zoning rules. DOH publishes state licensing and registry information; it does not publish a county-by-county zoning table. Each county publishes its own code and planning resources. The table below is therefore the inventory of what was checked, not a claim that a consolidated list exists.

Jurisdiction checkedZoning code / planning sourceCannabis-specific zoning provision located?
City and County of Honolulu (O‘ahu)Revised Ordinances of Honolulu (ROH) 2021, Chapter 21, Land Use Ordinance (LUO), Article 5, Table 21-5.1 (Permitted Use Table); DPP (Department of Planning and Permitting)No cannabis-specific use category located in the LUO excerpts checked. Dispensaries/production centres are therefore handled under the general agricultural / manufacturing / retail use categories plus §§46-4(f) and 329D-22. Verify by a DPP zoning-clearance search for “medical cannabis” in the current LUO — the LUO is large and a text search in this session did not return a cannabis-specific section. Code portal: American Legal — Honolulu Chapter 21 index (accessed 1 Nov 2026).
Hawai‘i County (Hawai‘i Island)Hawai‘i County Code, Chapter 25, Zoning Code; Planning Department zoning resourcesNo cannabis-specific section located in the Chapter 25 excerpts checked. Planning Department page confirms Chapter 25 “defines permitted land uses within the State Land Use ‘Urban’ and ‘Agricultural’ classifications” and provides a Permitted Uses Table. Hawai‘i County Planning — zoning / land use and Hawai‘i County Planning — resources (accessed 1 Nov 2026). Verify by a Chapter 25 text search before advising on a Hawai‘i County site.
Maui County (Maui, Moloka‘i, Lāna‘i)Maui County Code, Title 19, Zoning; Planning DepartmentNo Maui County cannabis-specific zoning ordinance located in this research. Maui’s Title 19 is the governing zoning title; no §329D-specific chapter was found. Verify by a Title 19 / Maui County Council ordinance search for “cannabis” / “dispensary.” Note: Moloka‘i and Lāna‘i are in Maui County for zoning, but have no licensed retail location in the DOH lists in §3.6 — that is a licensing outcome, not a county ban.
Kaua‘i CountyKaua‘i County Code 1987, Chapter 8, Comprehensive Zoning Ordinance (CZO); Planning Department / Planning CommissionNo cannabis-specific CZO provision located in this research. Chapter 8 is the governing ordinance (Ord. No. 935 replaced Chapter 8; Ord. No. 1166, 25 Oct 2024, amended Arts. 8-1, 8-2, 8-15). Code index: eCode360 — Kaua‘i County Code index (accessed 1 Nov 2026). Verify by a Chapter 8 text search.
Kalawao CountyNo county code; administered by the State (DOH)No zoning code to check. No dispensary licence may be issued for Kalawao (HRS §329D-2(d)). Any land-use question on Kalaupapa is a State/DOH/National Park matter, not a county zoning matter.

What this absence means in practice: a prospective licensee does not apply for a “county cannabis permit.” It applies for (a) a state DOH licence, (b) county plan approval / building permits for the specific parcel, and (c) if needed, a county zoning clearance / confirmation that the parcel’s district permits the use and satisfies the 750-foot buffer. Honolulu’s written zoning clearance fee is the only county clearance fee quantified in §3.5 — the other counties’ clearance/plan-approval fees are Unavailable in this report (see §3.5).

Unreliable secondary claim — corrected: a secondary site (StateRegsToday, Local Opt-Outs in Hawaii) claims counties may “opt-out” of dispensaries by ordinance subject to DOH approval, that Honolulu and Maui “passed ordinances prohibiting recreational cannabis” in 2019, and that Hawaii legalised adult-use in 2018. All three claims contradict HRS §§46-4(f) and 329D-22 and the enacted-law record (adult-use has never been enacted). Do not rely on that page. Source checked: StateRegsToday — local opt-outs in Hawaii (accessed 1 Nov 2026, rejected as unreliable for law).

3.4 County zoning, moratoriums, caps, and bans — finding: none located

3.4.1 Moratoriums

No county moratorium on medical cannabis dispensaries or production centres was located in any county code, council ordinance list, or DOH report checked. This is consistent with §46-4(f): a moratorium that operated as a prohibition on properly-zoned land would conflict with state law. Caveat: a county could impose a general development moratorium (e.g. for water/sewer capacity) that incidentally affects a parcel, but no cannabis-specific moratorium was found. Verify by a council-minutes search for “moratorium” + “cannabis” in each county if Run 2 requires a negative assurance with a date range.

3.4.2 Caps and bans

  • State cap, not a county cap: 8 dispensary licences statewide (§329D-2(d)), with the per-licence site limits in §329D-2(f), (g), (l) (see §3.6). Counties do not impose an additional numerical cap in any ordinance located.
  • County bans: None located. No county has a “no dispensaries” ordinance in the codes checked. The absence of a retail location on Moloka‘i, Lāna‘i, or Ni‘ihau is not a ban — it reflects that Maui County’s two licences are exercised on Maui island and Kaua‘i County’s one licence is exercised on Kaua‘i island (see §3.6), and that §329D-2(j)/(l) additional licences/locations are DOH determinations based on patient need / rural-underserved criteria, not county bans.
  • Adult-use bans are moot: because adult-use is not legal under state law, there is no county adult-use ban to report. Any county resolution opposing adult-use legalisation is a policy position, not a zoning ban on a lawful use.

3.4.3 Agricultural land rules (state + county)

  • State Land Use Agricultural District (HRS §205-4.5): farming, including crop production, is a permitted use in the Agricultural District. A §329D production centre is permitted where agricultural production is permitted (§329D-22(a)(1)), and hemp cultivation is an agricultural use where farming is permitted. Verify whether a specific parcel is in the State Agricultural District vs. Urban/Rural/Conservation before applying the county district table — the county district and the State district must both allow the use.
  • County agricultural districts: each county’s agricultural zoning district (e.g. Honolulu AG-1/AG-2 in the LUO; Hawai‘i County A-districts in Chapter 25; Maui County Agricultural District in Title 19; Kaua‘i County Agriculture District in CZO Chapter 8) permits farming and farm structures subject to district standards (lot size, setbacks, height, farm-dwelling rules). A production centre’s buildings (greenhouse, processing/manufacturing structures, security fencing) must meet those district standards and building codes. No county agricultural-district cannabis rule (e.g. a cannabis-specific setback from a farm dwelling or a cannabis-specific lot-size minimum) was located — Verify against the district tables for a specific parcel.
  • Important distinction — patient home grow vs. commercial production: patient/caregiver cultivation under HRS Chapter 329 is not a §329D production centre and is not governed by §329D-22(a)(1). It is governed by the registry/grow-site rules in Chapter 329 (see Part 1 of the full run). County residential zoning does not create a separate patient-grow licence, but a greenhouse or other structure for home grow must still comply with county building/zoning rules for accessory structures — Verify for a specific structure.

3.4.4 Hemp / CBD shops — county treatment

  • No county hemp-shop licence, hemp-shop zoning category, or hemp-shop buffer was located in any county code checked. A hemp/CBD retail shop is zoned as general retail in a retail/commercial district, subject to the same parking, signage, and building rules as any retail shop.
  • Product legality is state, not county: whether a hemp product may be sold (tincture, topical, etc.), its THC limit, labelling, and the DOH registration requirement from 1 January 2026 are DOH/HAR matters (Part 7 of the full run), not county zoning matters. A county cannot authorise a product the State prohibits, and a county retail permission does not authorise a prohibited product.
  • Smokable hemp / hemp flower retail: no county ordinance specifically permitting or prohibiting smokable-hemp retail was located. Verify the current DOH hemp-product rules for product legality; do not infer product permission from retail zoning.

3.5 County permit fees and buffer rules — what exists, what does not

3.5.1 Buffer rules (summary — only the state buffer is confirmed)

BufferDistanceApplies toSource
School / playground750 feet from the real property comprising the school/playgroundBoth production centres and dispensaries (retail locations)HRS §329D-22(a)(2), quoted at §3.2.2
Any other buffer (church, park, residence, other dispensary, etc.)None located in state law—Verify against each county code; none found in this research
Hemp processor / hemp retail buffer (secondary)Secondary sources report a 500-foot buffer for hemp processors from schools/parks/neighbourhoods under interim DOH hemp rulesHemp processors (not §329D dispensaries)Secondary only — Verify against HAR Chapter 11-37 / DOH hemp rules in Part 7. Do not apply the 500-foot figure to §329D facilities.

Measurement Verify note: §329D-22 does not state in its text whether 750 feet is measured as a straight-line radius, by pedestrian route, or from parcel line to parcel line. County planning departments apply their own measurement practice for zoning buffers generally. For any site, obtain a written county measurement / zoning clearance — do not assume a measurement method from the statute alone.

3.5.2 County permit fees — only Honolulu’s general schedule is quantified here

There is no county cannabis-specific permit fee. The fees a dispensary/production centre actually pays at county level are the general land-use and building fees that any comparable commercial/agricultural project pays. Only Honolulu’s schedule was opened in sufficient detail to quote:

City and County of Honolulu — ROH §6-41.1, Fee Schedule for Land Use Ordinance applications (via American Legal Publishing compilation, American Legal — Honolulu §6-41.1, accessed 1 Nov 2026; and Honolulu DPP fee table, Honolulu DPP fee appendix (PDF), accessed 1 Nov 2026):

Honolulu application (general, not cannabis-specific)Fee as published
Zone change$700 + $300 per acre or major fraction, max $15,000
Conditional use permit (major); CUP (minor) for meeting facility, daycare, K-12 school$1,200 + $300 per acre, max $15,000
Conditional use permit (minor), other$600
Plan review use$1,200 + $300 per acre, max $15,000
Written zoning clearance or confirmation, and flood hazard district interpretation$150 per request or per tax map key (multiple parcels); $300 per tax map key for confirmation of nonconforming status
Zoning variance$2,400
Zoning adjustment (other than sign master plan)$600
Building permitBy valuation under ROH Chapter 18, Table 18-A (e.g. $20 for $0.01–$500 valuation, scaling to $4,300 + $4.50 per $1,000 above $2,000,000 — full table in the DPP appendix)

Which Honolulu fee a dispensary actually triggers depends on the parcel and the use: a dispensary in a district where retail is permitted outright may need only a zoning clearance ($150) + building permit (by valuation) + plan approval, not a zone change or CUP. A production centre in an agricultural district may need plan approval / agricultural site approval ($600 for exclusive agriculture site approval in the schedule). Do not quote a single “Honolulu cannabis permit fee” — there is none.

Other counties — Unavailable in this report: Hawai‘i County: plan approval, use permit, variance, and building permit fees exist under Hawai‘i County Code Chapters 25 and related fee schedules, but no fee amounts were opened and verified in this session — Unavailable. Verify against the Hawai‘i County Planning Department fee schedule. Maui County: planning / building permit fees exist under Maui County Code, but no fee amounts were opened and verified — Unavailable. Verify against Maui County Planning Department fee schedule. Kaua‘i County: planning / zoning permit fees exist under Kaua‘i County Code Chapter 8 / Planning Department schedule (including the §8-3.5(b) civil-fine provision noted in council materials), but no cannabis-relevant fee amounts were opened and verified — Unavailable. Verify against Kaua‘i Planning Department fee schedule. Kalawao County: no county fee schedule; State administration. State fees are not county fees: the $5,000 / $75,000 / HAR §11-850-28 fees in Part 2, §2.5 are DOH fees. Do not add them to a county fee total as if they were a county charge.

3.6 Business concentration — ONLY what official sources say, by island

Method constraint honoured: no ranking, clustering analysis, or estimate by this report. The counts and addresses below are as published by DOH in its news releases approving retail locations, and the island totals are arithmetic sums of those DOH-published lists. Where DOH sources conflict, the conflict is stated, not smoothed over.

3.6.1 State licence structure (for context — HRS §329D-2, opened 1 Nov 2026)

Justia — §329D-2:

“(d) The department shall issue eight dispensary licenses statewide; provided that three dispensary licenses shall be issued for the city and county of Honolulu, two dispensary licenses each shall be issued for the county of Hawaii and the county of Maui, and one dispensary license shall be issued for the county of Kauai; provided further that no dispensary license shall be issued for the county of Kalawao.”
“(f) No more than three production centers shall be allowed under each dispensary license; provided that, except as otherwise specified in subsection (k), each production center shall be limited to no more than five thousand cannabis plants.”
“(g) A dispensary licensee may establish up to two retail dispensing locations under the licensee’s dispensary license, except as otherwise specified in subsection (l).”
“(l) … the department may determine whether dispensary licensees shall be allowed no more than two additional retail dispensing locations per licensee.”
“(i) Retail dispensing locations shall not be at the same location as the dispensary licensee’s production centers.”

History: [L 2015, c 241, pt of §2; am L 2016, c 230, §15; am L 2017, c 41, §5 and c 170, §2; am L 2022, c 309, §5]. In practice, DOH has allowed licensees to exceed the base two retail locations under subsection (l) — hence retail counts above 16 statewide (8 licences × 2) in §3.6.2. The number of licences remains 8; the number of retail locations is a DOH-approved site count, not a licence count. Note: the §329D-2(f) text quoted here (three centres, 5,000 plants base) is the post-Act 309, SLH 2022 text as reproduced in the Parts 2–3 pass; Part 4 adopts the fuller HAR/DOH position (5,000 base + up to 2,500 additional = 7,500 max per centre, 15,000 per licensee) — the amending Act for the +2,500 discretionary element remains Verify (Part 4 gap G1). Do not mix the two wordings without that note.

3.6.2 Retail locations as published by DOH — April 2025 list (25 locations) vs. January 2026 report (24 locations)

CONFLICT — flagged: April 2025 (DOH approval of Noa Botanicals’ 4th retail site): DOH stated the new Honolulu site at 345 Royal Hawaiian Ave., Honolulu, would be “O‘ahu’s 11th retail dispensary and the 25th retail dispensary for the state.” Maui Now report of the DOH approval, 17 April 2025, Maui Now — Noa Botanicals 4th retail (17 Apr 2025) (accessed 1 Nov 2026). The Governor’s news release for the prior approval (Cure Oahu, Kailua, 5 Dec 2024) similarly stated that site would be O‘ahu’s 10th and the state’s 25th at that time, reflecting a site that subsequently closed or was replaced — see below. Governor’s release: Governor — Cure Oahu approval (accessed 1 Nov 2026). January 2026 (DOH annual report, via CannBus summary): “Operating Retail Dispensary Sites (Dec 23, 2025): 24.” CannBus — Hawaii mirror (accessed 1 Nov 2026, citing the DOH annual report to the 2026 Legislature). Resolution adopted: the April 2025 DOH list (25 sites) and the December 2025 DOH count (24 sites) differ by one. Which site closed, relocated, or was not yet operating on 23 December 2025 was not stated in the excerpts checked — Verify against the full January 2026 DOH report’s location table and any DOH closure/relocation release between April and December 2025. Do not present “25” as the current operating count without that check. The April 2025 list below is therefore labelled as the April 2025 DOH-published list, not a confirmed 1 November 2026 operating list. Part 4.1.6 gives the December 2025 county breakdown (24 sites) from Table 1 of the January 2026 report as opened in the Parts 4–5 pass; the site-level reconciliation between the two lists remains Gap G5 in Part 5.

What official sources do NOT say (and this report therefore does not say): DOH does not publish a “cluster” or density ranking, a per-capita dispensary ratio by county in the sources checked, or a statement that any town is “underserved” beyond the §329D-2(j)/(l) rural/underserved criteria DOH applies when considering additional licences/locations. Any such ranking would be this report’s own estimate and is expressly not made, per the task instruction.

PART 4 — LICENCE TYPES

Access date for all sources below: 1 November 2026. Every fact carries its source link. Where a page was opened directly it is marked Opened; where a fact rests only on a search-result extract it is marked Search-extract only — Verify. No personal phone numbers or emails of individuals are reproduced. Business entity names below are the eight public dispensary licensees named in DOH annual reports. Federal baseline applied, not re-researched: state-licensed medical marijuana moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714); adult-use remains Schedule I; IRS §280E generally lifted for Schedule III medical, remains fully applicable to adult-use; no federal cannabis banking law exists. No contradiction with that baseline was found in the Hawaii sources checked for Parts 4–5. Hawaii’s programme is state-licensed medical only, so its eight dispensary licensees fall on the medical side of that split; this report does not give tax advice on how §280E relief flows through to a particular LLC/S-corporation licensee.

Headline answer: Hawaii has exactly one commercial medical-cannabis licence type — the vertically integrated medical cannabis dispensary licence under HRS Chapter 329D — plus a separate certified laboratory certification under §329D-8, plus hemp authorisations that are not cannabis licences (USDA hemp producer licence; DOH hemp processor permit; DOH manufactured-hemp-product distributor/retailer registration). There is no standalone cultivation, manufacturing, wholesale, distribution, delivery or adult-use licence in current law. There have been no new dispensary licences since 29 April 2016, and DOH is not currently accepting new dispensary applications. As of 23 December 2025, the eight licensees operated 34 facilities: 10 production centres and 24 retail locations.

4.1 The only current commercial medical-cannabis licence

4.1.1 Medical cannabis dispensary licence — HRS Chapter 329D, Act 241, Session Laws of Hawaii 2015

What it allows. One licence, in one county only, authorises the licensee to produce, manufacture, package and dispense cannabis and manufactured cannabis products to qualifying patients and primary caregivers in that county, through its production centres and retail dispensing locations. There are no separate grow, manufacture or retail licences to stack underneath it — the licence is vertically integrated by statute.

Verbatim operative text — §329D-2, HRS (as reproduced in secondary statute compilations; current capitol.hawaii.gov text to be re-checked line-by-line — see Gap G1):

“(a) No person shall operate a medical cannabis dispensary unless the person has a license issued by the department pursuant to this chapter.”
“(c) Each medical cannabis dispensary license shall allow production, manufacture, and dispensing of cannabis and manufactured cannabis products only in the county for which the license is granted.”
“(d) The department shall issue eight dispensary licenses statewide; provided that three dispensary licenses shall be issued for the city and county of Honolulu, two dispensary licenses each shall be issued for the county of Hawaii and the county of Maui, and one dispensary license shall be issued for the county of Kauai; provided further that no dispensary license shall be issued for the county of Kalawao.”
“(e) No person may be granted a dispensary license in more than one county.”

Source: §329D-2, HRS, via Justia/PocketLaw compilations — Justia — Chapter 329D and PocketLaw — §329D-2 (accessed 1 Nov 2026). Search-extract only — Verify against the current capitol.hawaii.gov §329D-2 text before republication, because the production-centre and retail-location subsections have been amended since 2015 (see §4.1.3 conflict note).

Governing rules. HAR Chapter 11-850 (Interim Rules), “Medical Cannabis Dispensaries,” as amended and compiled 6 December 2024 (Ramseyer copy): HAR 11-850, 6 Dec 2024 (PDF) — opened via search extract; file itself opened in search index. A secondary source also reports interim rules effective 17 November 2025 — Verify: the December 2024 compilation is the latest DOH rules PDF actually located for this report, and any November 2025 compilation was not opened in the Parts 4–5 pass (Gap G2). Part 1.2D notes the DOH release for the 17 November 2025 amendments, which corroborates that an update exists, but the compilation itself remains Verify.

Regulator / info pages. DOH medical cannabis programme hub: health.hawaii.gov — Medical Cannabis — cited in DOH releases as the programme site; the dispensary licensing section sits under the Office of Medical Cannabis Control and Regulation (OMCCR). DOH Medical Cannabis Registry Program (patient/caregiver cards — not a business licence): health.hawaii.gov — Registry Locate — search-extract only; a direct open of this page failed on a network grant in the Parts 4–5 pass and was not retried, so its current layout is Verify (Gap G3). DOH Statutes and Rules page (links HRS Chapter 328G, HAR 11-37 and the hemp permit application): health.hawaii.gov — Statutes and Rules (accessed 1 Nov 2026, search-extract only).

4.1.2 How the eight licences were awarded — 2016 merit selection, closed ever since

  • Application window: online applications accepted 12 January 2016 through 29 January 2016. 66 applications received.
  • Selection announced: 29 April 2016.
  • No new licences since: DOH Annual Report to the 2026 Legislature states verbatim: “There have been no new licenses issued since April 29, 2016.” Source: DOH, Annual Report — Medical Cannabis Dispensary Licensing System, January 2026 (transmitted 20 January 2026), DOH Annual Report Jan 2026 (PDF) — Opened, accessed 1 Nov 2026.
  • First operations: first production centres given notice to proceed for cultivation 1 February 2017; first dispensary given notice to proceed to retail 8 August 2017 (Maui Grown Therapies, Kahului). Same source.
  • Selection method: merit-based, scored application, segregated by county; if a selected applicant failed to pay the $75,000 licence fee within seven days, DOH selected the next highest-scoring applicant for that county (HAR §11-850-20/§11-850-21, 2024 compilation). Secondary reporting of the 2016 round records Maui Wellness Group scoring 510 of a possible 520 points — secondary only, Verify.
  • Whether new licences are open now: NO. DOH is not accepting new dispensary licence applications. This is stated in DOH-derived secondary summaries verified June 2026 and is consistent with the 2026 annual report (no new licences, no open window announced). Confirm on the day of any application decision with OMCCR directly, because HAR §11-850-5(b) preserves a power to issue additional licences (see §4.1.4).

4.1.3 How many sites each licence may operate — and a live statute-vs-rules conflict to flag

Current operational position (DOH 2026 annual report, the most authoritative and most recent official source — adopted here):

Per dispensary licenceMaximum allowedPlant cap
Production centresUp to 35,000 plants base per centre, plus up to 2,500 additional per centre at DOH’s determination; no more than 7,500 at a single centre; no more than 15,000 in total across all of the licensee’s centres
Retail dispensing locationsUp to 2 as of right; DOH may authorise 2 additional (total up to 4) to serve patients in a rural or underserved geographical arean/a
Co-locationRetail locations shall not be at the same location as the licensee’s production centresn/a

Sources: DOH January 2026 annual report, “Number and Location of Licensed Facilities,” footnoted to §329D-2(f), (g), (i), (k), (l), HRS — DOH Annual Report Jan 2026 (PDF) — Opened; HAR §11-850-6(a)–(b) (“A dispensary licensee shall be allowed to operate up to three production centers”) and §11-850-7(a) (“allowed to operate up to two retail dispensing locations; provided that the department may determine whether a dispensary licensee shall be allowed no more than two additional retail dispensing locations”) in the 6 December 2024 compilation — HAR 11-850, 6 Dec 2024 (PDF) — search-extract only.

Conflict — do not silently pick the old figure. Older secondary compilations of §329D-2 still read “Up to two production centers” and “no more than three thousand cannabis plants” per centre, and “one additional” retail location. Those are the 2015 Act 241 figures. The 2022 bill HB 2260, HD1/SD1, proposed amending §329D-2(f) from two to three production centres and 3,000 to 5,000 plants, and DOH testimony on that bill describes exactly that increase — but the Parts 4–5 pass did not locate the enacting Act that carried the 2→3 / 3,000→5,000 change into current §329D-2, and HB 2260’s own final disposition was not confirmed in the sources opened. The operational figures above (3 centres; 7,500/15,000 plants; up to 4 retail) are therefore adopted from the current HAR compilation and the DOH 2026 annual report, and the amending Act number/year for §329D-2(f), (g), (k) and (l) is flagged Verify (Gap G1). Any republication must quote the current capitol.hawaii.gov §329D-2 text, not the 2015/2016 Justia text. Part 3.6.1 quotes a post-Act 309 §329D-2(f) text (three centres, 5,000 plants base) — that is consistent with the base figure here; the +2,500 discretionary element is the HAR/DOH layer that remains Verify as to its amending Act.

4.1.4 Additional licences beyond eight — power exists, never used

HAR §11-850-5(b) (2024 compilation) provides, in extract: “Beginning October 1, 2018, the department may issue dispensary licenses in addition to those authorized by subsection (a), based on qualifying…” — the extract cuts off before the patient-count threshold, so the qualifying-patient threshold and any other condition in §11-850-5(b)–(c) are Verify (Gap G4). What is confirmed is the outcome: zero additional licences have been issued under that power through 23 December 2025 (2026 annual report: “There have been no new licenses issued since April 29, 2016”). No dispensary licence may be issued for Kalawao County (§329D-2(d); HAR §11-850-5(a)). Kalawao is administered by the State; it has no separate county licensing track.

Reconciliation with the prior year — a change that must not be smoothed over. The DOH January 2025 annual report (data as of 10 December 2024) reported 38 operational facilities: 13 production centres and 25 retail locations. The January 2026 report reports 34: 10 and 24. The drop is therefore −3 production centres and −1 retail location year-on-year. The 2026 Table 1 excerpt located for the Parts 4–5 pass shows Pono Life Maui with only two retail locations (Kīhei and Lahaina), whereas the 2024 Table 1 showed four (Kahului, Lahaina, Pā‘ia, Kīhei); the 2025 report also no longer lists a second Pono production centre that was shown as “NOT OPERATIONAL YET” in 2024. Whether the Kahului and Pā‘ia Pono retail sites closed, were decommissioned, or were omitted from the 2025 table excerpt is Verify (Gap G5) — the full 2026 Table 1 must be re-read in full before any site-by-site list is published. The statewide totals (34; 10; 24) are nevertheless confirmed by the 2026 report’s own summary sentence and by secondary reporting of the same report, so the totals are adopted and the site-level reconciliation is flagged, not guessed.

Theoretical maximum vs. operating reality. At the current caps, 8 licences × 3 production centres = 24 production centres and 8 × 4 retail = 32 retail locations are theoretically possible. Operating reality (10 and 24) is well below both caps. Do not present the theoretical maxima as the operating count.

Cross-check to Part 3.6.2: Part 3’s April 2025 DOH-published retail list sums to 25 sites (O‘ahu 11 + Hawai‘i Island 6 + Kaua‘i 2 + Maui island 6 = 25, with Pono at 3 Maui sites in that list vs 2 in the December 2025 count, and Maui Grown at 3 in both). The December 2025 county breakdown above (O‘ahu 11, Hawai‘i 6, Maui 5, Kaua‘i 2 = 24) is consistent with the April 2025 list except for one Maui site — the same unresolved Pono discrepancy. Do not publish a per-county retail table that mixes the two dates without this note.

4.1.7 Fees — statutory fees vs. the post-2023 schedule (conflict preserved, not hidden)

Statutory fees still in §329D-4, HRS (2015 Act 241, as amended 2016, Act 184, §5): non-refundable application fee: $5,000 per licence application (certified or cashier’s check). Dispensary licence fee on selection: $75,000 per licence, payable within seven days of written notice of selection, failing which DOH issues the licence to the next qualified applicant. Annual renewal fee: $50,000, subject to verification by unannounced inspection that the licensee continues to meet all licensing requirements.

Verbatim extract, §329D-4(c) and (n): “A nonrefundable application fee of $5,000 for each license application shall be submitted to the department by certified or cashier’s check. Within seven days of approval, a dispensary license fee of $75,000 for each license approved shall be submitted…” / “A dispensary license may be renewed annually by payment of an annual renewal fee of $50,000…” Source: §329D-4 via Justia — §329D-4 (2022) — search-extract only, Verify against current capitol text (Gap G1). HAR §11-850-18/§11-850-21 (2024 compilation) repeat the $5,000 application fee (payable to “State of Hawaii Department of Health,” delivered/mailed by certified mail to DOH Medical Cannabis Dispensary Licensing Section, 601 Kamokila Blvd., Rm. 337, Kapolei, HI 96707, postmarked by 4:30 p.m. HST on the last day of the open period) and the $75,000 fee within seven days of selection notice.

Post-2023 reality — the $50,000 renewal fee is no longer what was collected. The DOH annual reports state: “In 2023 a new licensing fee schedule was introduced consisting of four factors: (1) Base fee for production center(s); (2) Base fee for retail center(s); (3) Discount percentage based on market conditions in each county; (4) Discount percentage based on market share.” Total renewal fees collected under that schedule:

YearTotal collected (all 8 licensees)Source
2016 (initial)$600,000 (8 × $75,000)DOH Jan 2026 report, Table 2
2017–2022$400,000 per year (8 × $50,000)DOH Jan 2026 report, Table 2
2023$885,250DOH Jan 2026 report, Table 2
2024$961,250DOH Jan 2025 and Jan 2026 reports, Table 2
2025$915,250DOH Jan 2026 report, Table 2
Total to date (2026 report)$5,761,750DOH Jan 2026 report

Sources: DOH Annual Report Jan 2026 (PDF) (Opened) and DOH Annual Report Jan 2025 (PDF) (Opened).

Two flags on fees: (1) Per-factor dollar amounts are Unavailable. Neither annual report publishes the base-fee amounts or the discount percentages, and no per-licensee 2023–2025 fee is published. The implied average (≈$114,406 per licensee in 2025) is arithmetic on the published total, not a published fee — do not quote it as a fee. See Part 2.5.2 for the HAR §11-850-28 base-fee table (per production centre by plants/manufacturing type; $20,000 per retail location) — that table is the formula, not a per-licensee bill. (2) Narrative/table inconsistency in the reports themselves. The 2025 and 2026 reports’ narrative says the $50,000 renewal was collected “in 2017, 2018, 2019, 2020, and 2022” — omitting 2021 — while Table 2 shows $400,000 for 2021 as well. The table is adopted; the narrative omission is treated as a drafting slip and flagged (Gap G6). Whether the 2023 schedule was adopted by rule, by DOH policy, or by statute was not established in the sources opened — Verify (Gap G7). Until that is resolved, quote the statutory $50,000 as the statute figure and the 2023–2025 totals as the amounts actually collected, side by side, exactly as done above.

4.1.8 Qualifications and residency — §329D-3, HRS

Verbatim operative provisions (secondary compilation; Verify against current capitol text — Gap G1):

“§329D-3 Qualifications for licensure. (a) Each application for a dispensary license shall include both an individual applicant and an applying entity.”
Individual applicant must: “(A) Has been a legal resident of the State for not less than five years preceding the date of application; (B) Is not less than twenty-one years of age; and (C) Has had no felony convictions;”
Applying entity must: have a Hawai‘i tax identification number; and “(E) Is not less than fifty-one per cent held by Hawaii legal residents or entities wholly controlled by Hawaii legal residents who have been Hawaii legal residents for not less than five years immediately preceding the date the application was submitted;”
Financial resources: “not less than $1,000,000 for each license applied for, plus not less than $100,000 for each retail dispensing location allowed under the license applied for, in the form of bank statements or escrow accounts, and … under the control of the applying entity for not less than ninety days immediately preceding” application. (§329D-4(g)(17) in the application form refers to proof of “$1,200,000” — i.e. $1,000,000 + $100,000 × 2 base retail locations — which reconciles the two figures.)

Source: Justia — §329D-3 — search-extract only. HAR §11-850-13 repeats the individual-applicant qualifications (21+, 5-year legal residence, no felony convictions or other disqualifying background history, authorised by the applying entity as primary point of contact): Cornell — HAR §11-850-13 — search-extract only.

Other application contents (from §329D-4(g) extract): eCrim validation code from the Hawai‘i Criminal Justice Data Center; county designation; date continuous legal residence began for each Hawai‘i-resident owner; total percentage owned by Hawai‘i legal residents; business, security, inventory-control and zoning-compliance plans (per HAR Chapter 11-850 subchapters and DOH application materials — full application checklist Verify, Gap G8, as no current DOH application form was opened because the window is closed). Background checks: fingerprint-based criminal-history checks via the State Identification Bureau/FBI are described in secondary summaries of the process; the eCrim requirement in §329D-4(g)(3) is the statutory anchor located. Full current background-check procedure is Verify (Gap G8).

4.1.9 Ownership, transfer, renewal, relocation — what current law actually says

  • Licence sale/transfer is prohibited by statute. Verbatim, §329D-3 (closing sentence, 2015 Act 241, pt of §2): “A dispensary license may not be sold or otherwise transferred from one person to another person.” Source: Justia — §329D-3 — search-extract only.
  • What that prohibition does and does not cover is Verify. The sources opened do not resolve: (a) whether an internal change in entity ownership (short of a licence transfer) requires DOH approval or only notice; (b) succession on death/incapacity of the individual licensee; (c) whether the “individual applicant + applying entity” dual-licensee structure permits substitution of the individual. A 2019 proposal, HB 673, would have created a sale/transfer process on death, incapacity, disability, resignation or termination — it is cited here only as a proposal; its final disposition was not confirmed and it must not be presented as law (Gap G9).
  • Renewal: annually, on payment of the renewal fee and verification by unannounced inspection (§329D-4(n), statutory text). Renewal timeline (e.g. how many days before expiry to apply), late-renewal consequences, and the post-2023 fee-schedule renewal mechanics are Unavailable in the sources opened (Gap G7/G8).
  • Ownership caps: no per-person cap on number of licences beyond §329D-2(e) — “No person may be granted a dispensary license in more than one county” — was located. There is no published cap on number of licences per owner within a county beyond the one-licence-per-county rule, because the county allocation itself (max 3/2/2/1) is the binding constraint. Verify whether HAR imposes any additional ownership-concentration rule (Gap G10).
  • Relocation / new sites: a licensee must obtain DOH approval (notice to proceed after inspection) before operating a new production centre or retail location — evidenced by the notice-to-proceed dates in Table 1 of each annual report and by DOH news releases for each new retail site (e.g. Maui Grown Therapies Kīhei, notice 17 Nov 2023; Pono Life Kīhei, 10 Oct 2023). The formal relocation application procedure and timeline are Verify (Gap G8).
  • Surrender/revocation, insurance and bonding minimums: no insurance or bonding minimum for dispensary licensees was located in §329D or in the HAR extracts opened — Unavailable/Verify (Gap G11). Do not import another state’s bonding figure. Enforcement is by inspection and violation (see Part 5, §5.2); revocation/surrender procedure is in Part 11 territory and was not established here.

4.1.10 Social equity — current law: none; proposed law: extensive

  • Current Chapter 329D contains no social-equity licence, fee waiver, priority, or set-aside provision located in this research. The 2016 merit selection had no equity track. State this plainly: a new entrant cannot apply as a “social equity applicant” under current Hawai‘i medical law.
  • Proposed only: HB 1246 / SB 1613 (2025, carried over to 2026 — see §4.3) would create a social-equity grant programme and a 50% waiver of application and licensing fees for social-equity applicants for their first five years if conditions are met, plus a craft-cannabis licence aimed at small farmers and indigenous crops/practices. Those provisions are PROPOSED, not law.

4.1.11 Estimated startup costs — no official estimate exists; do not invent one

No DOH or other official Hawai‘i source publishing an estimated startup cost for a dispensary was located — Unavailable. The only official, citable floor is the statutory financial-resources test: at least $1,200,000 under the applicant’s control for 90 days ($1,000,000 + $100,000 × 2 base retail locations) plus the $5,000 application fee and $75,000 licence fee (statutory figures; post-2023 renewal economics differ — see §4.1.7). That $1.2 million is a liquidity test, not a startup-cost estimate: build-out, lease, equipment, BioTrack integration, testing, security, staffing, working capital and GET are all additional and unquantified in official sources. Any third-party “cost to open a dispensary in Hawai‘i” figure found online is non-official and was not adopted; if the parent report needs a planning range, it must be labelled as a non-official estimate built from vendor quotes, not from DOH. Flag for follow-up: obtain a current build-out estimate from a Hawai‘i licensee or contractor and label it as such — do not backfill from other states.

4.2 Certified laboratories — §329D-8, HRS; HAR Chapter 11-850; State Laboratories Division

This is a certification, not a dispensary licence, and it is the only other cannabis authorisation in Chapter 329D.

What it allows. A certified laboratory tests cannabis and manufactured cannabis products for dispensary licensees (potency, contaminants) before dispensing. Dispensary licensees must have product tested by a DOH-certified laboratory (HAR §11-850-81, per secondary summary). Patients/caregivers may also transport up to one gram per test to a certified laboratory, by appointment with electronic confirmation (§329-122(f)(5), quoted on the DOH certified-facilities page).

Legal basis. §329D-8, HRS, “Laboratory standards and testing; laboratory certification” (Chapter 329D contents list: Justia — Chapter 329D — search-extract only). The certification process is administered by the DOH State Laboratories Division (SLD), not OMCCR alone: application materials are delivered/sent by certified mail to State Laboratories Division, 2725 Waimano Home Road, Pearl City, HI 96782 (public agency address from the SLD form).

Process (from the SLD application form and secondary summaries — fee and current form status Verify, see below): (1) submit the Medical Marijuana Testing Facility Application (legal name, facility address, areas of interest — chemistry/potency, pesticides, microbiology, etc. — with minimum required analytes including delta-9 THC and THCA). (2) Show ISO/IEC 17025 accreditation status (accredited, or at minimum applied for accreditation), with QA manual/QA plan, most recent proficiency-test studies per analyte with scores and corrective actions, and most recent accreditation-body inspection(s) and responses. (3) Undergo on-site inspection/evaluation and demonstrate capacity and proficiency to test cannabis products. (4) On certification, comply with DOH testing standards; certification is by analyte/area, not a blanket “cannabis lab licence.” Source for form contents: SLD Medical Marijuana Testing Facility Application (form file EHASB_BR_ADM_Medical_Marijuana_Testing_Facility_Application1v1.pdf) via TemplateRoller mirror of DOH form (PDF) — secondary mirror of a DOH form, Verify against the current DOH/SLD original (Gap G12).

Fee — Verify, do not quote as confirmed. Secondary sources (non-official) state a $3,000 certification fee (initial and renewal each, by certified/cashier’s check or money order; personal/company checks not accepted). No official DOH fee schedule confirming $3,000 was opened in this research, and the SLD form extract located does not show the fee. Quote $3,000 only as “reported by secondary sources, Verify against SLD” (Gap G12).

How many certified labs exist now — Unavailable from an official list. The DOH Certified Cannabis Testing Facilities page (health.hawaii.gov — Certified Cannabis Testing Facilities — Opened 1 Nov 2026) contains, in the text extract returned, no laboratory names at all — only the §329-122(f)(5) transport text and an Info Line number. The page may render its list by script or embed that the text extract did not capture; the current certified-lab count and names therefore cannot be confirmed from this page and are flagged Unavailable/Verify (Gap G13). Historical/secondary names that must not be presented as current: Steep Hill Hawaii (first provisional certification, August 2017, secondary news reports); AgLabs Hawaii, LLC, MedCan Hawaii, LLC, Spectra Analytical Laboratories, LLC (listed in a July 2022 secondary wiki compilation — stale, Verify). The DOH January 2026 annual report does not publish a lab count (secondary summary of that report notes: “Count not published in the DOH report”). Part 6.3 notes the same: 1 certified testing laboratory as of September 2024 (secondary summary of DOH) — do not carry “1 lab” forward as a 2026 fact without checking.

Whether lab certification applications are open: no closed-window statement for labs was located, unlike dispensaries. The SLD application route appears rolling, but current open/closed status is Verify (Gap G12) — do not assume it is open.

No separate hemp-testing lab licence exists in Hawai‘i law located here; hemp testing is by qualified/DEA-registered labs under the federal framework (see Part 7 cross-reference, §4.4).

4.3 PROPOSED adult-use and new-authority licences — NOT law

Status rule applied throughout: every item in this section is PROPOSED. None has been enacted as of 1 November 2026 on the evidence located. The 1 January 2026 dates inside these bills are bill text, not effective law — the bills did not take effect on those dates.

4.3.1 HB 1246 (2025) / SB 1613 (2025) — Hawai‘i Cannabis and Hemp Office; dual-use legalisation

  • Official status (capitol.hawaii.gov via data.capitol.hawaii.gov): HB 1246, HD1 — current referral JHA/AGR, CPC, FIN; last action 8 December 2025: “Carried over to 2026 Regular Session.” On 6 February 2025 it was reported from JHA/AGR as amended (HD1) and then recommitted to JHA/AGR. Companion: SB 1613. SB 1613, SD1 — likewise carried over to 2026 Regular Session on 8 December 2025; its CPN/WAM decision-making scheduled 26 February 2025 was deleted. Sources: capitol.hawaii.gov — HB 1246 and LegiScan/BillTrack50 mirrors of the capitol record — search-extract only; final 2026-session disposition (passed, deferred, or died) was not confirmed in the sources opened and is Verify (Gap G14) — do not state a 2026 outcome.
  • What it would create (PROPOSED): the Hawai‘i Cannabis and Hemp Office (HCHO) within the Department of Commerce and Consumer Affairs (DCCA) for administrative purposes only, run by a governor-nominated, Senate-confirmed administrator (DOH OMCCR programme manager as interim administrator); transfer of DOH medical-cannabis personnel/assets and Department of Agriculture cannabis assets to HCHO; regulation of adult-use cannabis, medical cannabis and hemp under one office (“one plant, one agency”).
  • PROPOSED licence types (from the bill’s sponsor summary handout, secondary but bill-derived): cultivator, processor, retail store, medical cooperative, craft cannabis licence (small cultivator growing and selling its own product from its premises, like a farm stand, and selling to other dispensaries; granting “shall promote social equity and small farms with a focus on indigenous crops or farming practices”), plus future delivery licences, special-use permits for events and social consumption, and a dual-use conversion licence for existing dispensaries. Grow-licence size limit in sponsor material: 3,200 sq ft indoors (secondary summary — Verify against bill text §A-116 et seq.). Source: HB 1246 Summary Handout (PDF) — search-extract only.
  • PROPOSED social equity: 50% fee waiver for social-equity applicants for first five years if conditions met; social-equity, public-health, public-safety and hemp grant subaccounts funded from cannabis tax revenue (30% social equity in sponsor material — Verify against bill text).
  • PROPOSED taxes (for Part 2 cross-reference, labelled proposed): 14% retail tax on adult-use cannabis; existing 4% medical tax continued; GET would not apply to retail/wholesale cannabis sales under the bill.
  • PROPOSED personal-use dates (not law): personal adult use (21+) from 1 January 2026 in bill text; interim HCHO rules by 31 December 2025, final rules by 31 December 2030 in sponsor material. None of these dates took effect, because the bill was not enacted.

4.3.2 Other 2024–2026 proposals — listed only to prevent them being mistaken for law

  • SB 3335 (2024) — the prior session’s adult-use/HCHO vehicle, referenced in HB 1246 sponsor material as “last year’s” bill. Final disposition Verify (Gap G14); it did not become law and no HCHO exists.
  • HB 302 (2025), enacted as Act 241, SLH 2025 — this is NOT an adult-use bill and NOT the 2015 Act 241. It is a medical-cannabis access/enforcement bill (inter-dispensary sales up to 800 oz per 30 days with DOH approval and transport manifest; cultivation-licence provisions; Attorney General Drug Nuisance Abatement Unit enforcement; Medical Cannabis Registry and Regulation Special Fund). Its 2025 Act 241 number collides with the 2015 Act 241 number — always cite the year (Act 241, SLH 2015 = dispensary system; Act 241, SLH 2025 = HB 302 medical amendments). Whether HB 302 creates a standalone “cannabis cultivator licence” distinct from a dispensary production centre, and its effective dates (bill text shows a 31 December 2050 general effective date with 1 January 2027 provisions — a common Hawai‘i drafting device), are Verify against the enrolled Act 241, SLH 2025 text (Gap G15). Do not list a “cultivator licence” as current law on the basis of HB 302 summaries alone. Source: Act 241, SLH 2025 (PDF) — search-extract only. Note: Part 1.2B/§0.2 treats the cultivator provision as Part IV effective 1 January 2028 — the 2027 vs 2028 effective-date difference between summaries is itself Verify against the enrolled Act; do not publish a cultivator effective date without that check.
  • HB 602 (2025, carried to 2026) — “Hawaii Medical Use of Cannabis Act of 2025,” proposing a Medical Cannabis Cultivation Site Collective System chapter and a “cannabis testing facility” definition tied to §329D-8(a). PROPOSED only; disposition Verify (Gap G14).
  • SB 3275 / low-THC proposals and HB 1624 / SB 2420 ballot-question proposals (2026) — reported in secondary sources as Senate-advanced low-dose (5 mg THC/serving) possession without a commercial licensing system, and as ballot-question vehicles. No commercial licence type can be cited from these without the capitol record; Verify (Gap G14). In particular, a low-dose possession bill creates no retail licence even if enacted.
  • SB 2178 (2026) — industrial-hemp infrastructure bill (proposed HRS Chapter 141 part, USDA-licence-based, from 1 July 2027 in bill text). PROPOSED only; see Part 7.

Bottom line for Part 4: as of 1 November 2026, the current-law licence inventory is: (1) medical cannabis dispensary licence (8, closed); (2) certified laboratory certification (count unavailable). Everything else in this section is proposed.

(2) Processors who hold a valid permit are issued registration certificates automatically for sale/distribution as indicated on the permit application (HAR §11-37-11) — processor and registrant lists will therefore overlap and must not be added together. (3) The page’s older text (Food and Drug Branch registry lineage, pre-OMCCR administration) vs. Act 269’s OMCCR administration must not be conflated — current administrator is OMCCR

Related hemp rules file (direct download): HAR 11-37 standard copy, 28 Jan 2026 — Standard11-37HAR-20260128.pdf — Standard11-37HAR-20260128.pdf (§11-37-11 registration, $50 + online processing fee; §11-37-16 location restriction; subchapter 5 processor permits).

5.7 Department of Agriculture / USDA hemp records for Hawai‘i

5.7(a) USDA — the actual hemp licensing authority for Hawai‘i cultivation

ItemDetail
Plan-status URLUSDA — state and tribal plan review — table updated 17 Feb 2026 in extract; Hawai‘i row: “USDA Hemp Producer License” (i.e. no state plan; USDA issues the licence)
Programme URLUSDA — hemp programme (programme overview; DEA-lab testing enforcement delayed to 31 Dec 2026 — matches the federal baseline)
Application platformUSDA — HeMP (Hemp eManagement Platform); requires Login.gov/eAuth account; licensees apply/renew/amend/print licences, file disposal (AMS-27) and annual (AMS-28) reports, view lab results and FSA lots. Login-gated — no public holder list
Public searchUSDA states law-enforcement access is via hempsearch.ams.usda.gov (request access by email). Whether a general-public hemp producer search returning Hawai‘i licensee names exists, and its URL, is Verify (Gap G17) — no public search result set for Hawai‘i producers was opened. Do not cite hempsearch.ams.usda.gov as a public source until that is resolved
Format / downloadPlan-status table is a web table (no download located). HeMP data is account-gated. No USDA CSV/XLSX of Hawai‘i licensees was located
Latest data / countsPlan table updated 17 Feb 2026 (extract). Producer count: 51 licensed growers in Hawai‘i as of July 2025 — secondary only (USDA public-search-derived, reported secondarily). No official USDA Hawai‘i count table was opened — Verify (Gap G17)
LimitationsUSDA licensing covers cultivation only. It does not list DOH hemp processors or DOH distributor/retailer registrants (§5.5), and it does not list cannabis dispensaries. HDOA’s former pilot licences (§4.4) are historical and are not in HeMP

5.7(b) Hawai‘i Department of Agriculture and Biosecurity (HDOA) — hemp cultivation/transport guidance, not a current licence list

  • Hemp programme contact page lineage: dab.hawaii.gov — hemp (cited secondarily as the HDOA hemp page). HDOA’s current role for hemp is guidance on cultivation/transport and related agricultural programmes; cultivation licensing itself is USDA’s (see §5.7(a)). No current HDOA hemp licensee list was located — if HDOA still publishes any legacy pilot list, it is superseded and must not be used as a current holder list (Gap G24).
  • Historical HDOA pilot data (for Part 9, not current Part 5): first licences June 2018, 3 growers (Hawai‘i County ×2, Kaua‘i ×1), 10 applications received by June 2018. Source: secondary reporting of HDOA announcement — Verify against HDOA archive if Part 9 needs it.

5.8 Hawai‘i Business Express — DCCA Business Registration Division (entity search, NOT a cannabis licence register)

ItemDetail
URL (new portal, secondary-sourced — Verify live)hbe.dcca.hawaii.gov — Search & Buy — “Search & Buy” on the new Hawai‘i Business Express portal. Secondary sources report the portal moved from hbe.ehawaii.gov to hbe.dcca.hawaii.gov effective 6 July 2026 (old system offline 5 p.m. HST 2 July 2026), with old bookmarks redirecting. The new URL was not opened directly in the Parts 4–5 pass — Verify live (Gap G25)
Holder typeBusiness entities generally (LLCs, corporations, trade names, trademarks/service marks) — including, incidentally, the entities behind dispensary licences, but the search does not show cannabis licence status
FormatInteractive web search (name-based; “Begins with” / “Contains”). No bulk download for the public; no cannabis filter; no export button for search results located. Paid documents (e.g. Certificate of Good Standing, reported $5 standard) are purchasable from a record.
