Limitations — critical(1) An entity being “Active” in Business Express does NOT mean it holds a cannabis licence, and a cannabis licensee’s entity record does not prove its dispensary licence is current — always cross-check against the DOH annual report (§5.2). (2) One business name can sit on several records (different entity forms/file numbers). (3) The portal itself warns results “should not be used as a definitive method to determine name availability.” (4) Hawai‘i has no Secretary of State — DCCA/BREG is the registry; do not cite a “Secretary of State search” for Hawai‘i

5.9 County permit and zoning records — no central source; what exists and what does not

No statewide or county-published list of cannabis dispensary permits, zoning approvals, or hemp/CBD shop permits was located in this research — Unavailable as a consolidated source (Gap G26). What can be said, without inventing county portals:

  • The DOH annual report Table 1 retail addresses (§5.2) are the de facto public record of where licensed retail sites operate, by island/town — but they are DOH notices to proceed, not county permits.
  • Counties control zoning/land use (see Part 3), but no county in the sources opened publishes a cannabis-specific permit register that could be inventoried here with a URL, format and update frequency. County planning/permitting portals (Honolulu DPP, Hawai‘i County Planning, Maui County Planning, Kaua‘i County Planning) were not individually searched for a cannabis permit dataset in this Part 4–5 pass — that search is the exact follow-up for Part 3/Part 5 completion (Gap G26). Do not list county portal URLs from memory; each must be opened and checked for a cannabis filter/dataset before it is cited.
  • Kalawao County: no dispensary licence may issue there (§4.1.4); no separate Kalawao permit source is expected.

PART 6 — MARKET DATA

Scope and method note — read first. This is Part 6 only, for use in the Hawaii Parts 0–6 run. Access date for every source below: 1 November 2026. Direct-page limitation: browser.open failed on the official PDFs attempted in the Part 6 pass with a transient worker error (“check for expired transient grants”). Those PDFs were not directly opened in that pass. Facts below drawn from them are therefore cited to the search-indexed official PDF/report text and must be re-opened and page-checked by Parts 0–5 before final publication. They are not presented as directly re-read in that pass. Where a fact rests only on a search snippet or a secondary summary, that is stated. The Parts 4–5 pass did open the DOH January 2026 annual report PDF and confirms the GET totals and facility totals used here — those totals are adopted; the PDF-recheck for Table 1 site detail, Table 3 notes, and CPPC methodology remains a gap. Federal baseline applied, not re-researched (see cover). No market figure below is adjusted for Schedule III or for the coming hemp-definition change — no official Hawaii source retrieved quantifies either effect. Hawaii has no municipal governments below county level. “By island” below is therefore reported by county as DOH reports it: City and County of Honolulu (O‘ahu), Hawai‘i County (Hawai‘i Island), Maui County (Maui, Moloka‘i, Lāna‘i — DOH does not break Maui County by island in the monthly registry tables retrieved), Kaua‘i County. Kalawao County has no separate DOH county row in the tables retrieved. Same-name caution observed: Kailua (O‘ahu, in the Cure Oahu list) is not Kailua-Kona (Hawai‘i Island); Waimea/Kamuela (Hawai‘i Island) is not Waimea (Kaua‘i). No retail-location address list is reproduced in full here — that belongs to Parts 4–5 — only counts by county/operator.

6.1 Dispensary sales — what counts, what does not

6.1.1 Official GET series (DOH Annual Reports to the Legislature) — TAX, NOT SALES

Source: DOH, Medical Cannabis Dispensary Licensing Section (MCDLS), Office of Medical Cannabis Control and Regulation (OMCCR), Annual Report — Medical Cannabis Dispensary Licensing System. January 2025 report (to the 34th Legislature): DOH Annual Report Jan 2025 (PDF). January 2026 report (transmitted 20 January 2026, to the 33rd Legislature): DOH Annual Report Jan 2026 (PDF). DOH reports index page: DOH reports to 2026 Legislature. The 2026 report states the tax rule as: a 4% GET is assessed on cannabis and manufactured cannabis products purchased at licensed dispensaries by qualifying registered patients; the City and County of Honolulu, County of Kaua‘i and County of Hawai‘i have an additional 0.5% surcharge, bringing the rate to 4.5% in those counties (Maui County is therefore 4% in the CannBus summary of the same report — Verify against the full 2026 report text before Parts 0–5 state the Maui rate, as the snippet truncates at that point). Part 2.3.2 resolves this conflict in favour of DOTAX/Maui County: Maui is 0.5% from 1 January 2024, so the DOH excerpt’s Maui sentence is stale — do not use “Maui 4%” as the current rate.

Implied-sales calculation — labelled calculation, NOT an official figure. Because GET is levied at 4% or 4.5% depending on county and DOH does not publish the county split of taxable sales, taxable sales can only be bounded, not stated:

YearGETImplied taxable sales at 4.5%Implied taxable sales at 4%
2023$2,588,588$57,524,178$64,714,700
2024$2,937,877$65,286,156$73,446,925
2025$2,828,019$62,844,867$70,700,475

Calculation by the Part 6 researcher from the DOH GET figures above; county mix unknown, so the true total lies inside each range only if all sales were taxable at the stated rate and no exemptions/adjustments applied. Do not publish a single-point “sales” figure derived this way without that caveat. Note the coincidence that the 2023 upper bound ($64,714,700) matches, to the dollar-order, the $64.7 million “2023 sales” figure quoted by secondary sources (see §6.1.2) — that suggests the $64.7m figure may itself be a 4%-divisor derivation, or an independent estimate that happens to coincide. Verify before Parts 0–5 treat $64.7m as an independently measured BioTrack total.

6.1.2 BioTrack / secondary sales series — conflicting, do not merge

DOH cites BioTrack THC™ as the electronic seed-to-sale tracking system in its annual reports, but the annual reports retrieved do not publish a year-by-year BioTrack sales table. The following sales figures come from other official or semi-official analyses and conflict with each other and with the GET-implied range. Present them as separate series, never averaged:

  • Dual Use / Tax Working Group series (officially commissioned, dated): dispensary sales “grew from $18.2 million in 2018 to $50 million in 2022” and “the estimated size of Hawaii’s medical cannabis market is $240 million” (2021 market, of which $50 million medical and the remainder illegal, per the December 2022 Dual Use report summary). Source summary: Cannabis Industry Data — Hawaii market worth; also Hawaii State Cannabis — business. Conflict: $18.2m for 2018 implies GET of ~$730,000–$819,000 at 4–4.5%, well above the actual 2018 GET of $572,170. Either the $18.2m figure is pre-GET, fiscal-year, or differently defined, or it is inconsistent. Verify against the Dual Use report itself.
  • “Pre-tax revenue” secondary series: $12.6 million in 2018 and $26.5 million in 2019, with excise taxes “totalling $1.4 million” — source: Hawaiistatecannabis.org (secondary site, not DOH). Conflict: $12.6m × 4.5% = $567,000, close to 2018 GET ($572,170), but $26.5m for 2019 implies ~$1.19m GET, matching 2019 GET ($1,201,168) closely — so this series is broadly GET-consistent, unlike the $18.2m figure. The two secondary series therefore disagree with each other for 2018 ($12.6m vs $18.2m). Flag as conflicting; prefer the GET-consistent series if a secondary annual series must be shown, and label it secondary.
  • 2020–2021 “State of Hawaii estimates”: “around $44 million in 2020 and around $50 million in 2021” — source: CannaBusinessPlans — Hawaii cannabis market (secondary, citing DOH). Conflict: GET-implied 2020 range is $44,958,622 (4.5%) to $50,578,450 (4%); 2021 range is $57,087,711 to $64,223,675. The $44m (2020) sits at the bottom of the range; the $50m (2021) sits below the GET-implied range, which is impossible if both series measure the same taxable sales. Verify — likely a different definition (e.g. flower only, or a different year basis).
  • 2023 $64.7 million; 2025 projection $73 million: “In 2023, $64.7 million worth of medical cannabis was sold in Hawaii. According to an MJBiz Factbook projection, cannabis sales in Hawaii could reach $73 million in 2025.” Source: CannaBusinessPlans — Hawaii cannabis market. The $64.7m figure is at the top of the GET-implied 2023 range (see §6.1.1). The $73m is a projection, not an outcome — do not report it as 2025 actual sales. Actual 2025 GET fell 3.7% year-on-year ($2,937,877 → $2,828,019), which points to flat-to-down, not rising, 2025 taxable sales.
  • Early sales (2017): Maui Grown Therapies and Aloha Green combined for “more than $426,000 in sales since opening” in August 2017 through 19 September 2017, selling 57 pounds of flower, 8,890 products, 7,845 customer visits. Source: MJBizDaily, 2017, citing Pacific Business News / Honolulu Star-Advertiser and a DOH legislative presentation: MJBizDaily — Hawaii dispensaries off fast start (2017). This is consistent with the very small full-year 2017 GET ($99,871, implying roughly $2.2–$2.5m full-year taxable sales across the two dispensaries for the partial year).

Quarterly sales: Unavailable. No official DOH quarterly dispensary-sales table was located in this research. DOH publishes annual GET/fee tables and monthly registry counts, not quarterly sales. Do not interpolate quarters from annual GET. The only sub-annual BioTrack figure located is a single month (see §6.1.3).

6.1.3 Current monthly level — CPPC state-commissioned analysis (best current anchor)

Source (officially commissioned, DOH-hosted updated version): Cannabis Public Policy Consulting (CPPC), An Economic Analysis of the Current Medical and Future Adult-Use Cannabis Market in Hawai‘i, 19 December 2025, updated 28 February 2026 (citation in updated version: Cunningham, Slade & Fox, 31 March 2026): CPPC — Hawaii economic analysis, updated (PDF).

  • BioTrack seed-to-sale data analysed: 2019–2024 (6,881,020 records cited in the updated version).
  • Cross-validation month (March, year not stated in the snippet — Verify whether March 2024 or March 2025): BioTrack recorded $5,336,706.18 in medical cannabis sales in Hawai‘i in that month; CPPC’s survey-based estimate was $5,409,537 — a 98.64% match.
  • Headline current level used by CPPC and by secondary coverage: about $5.3–$5.34 million per month in medical dispensary sales; “about $60 million annually” (MJBizDaily summary, 6 February 2026: MJBizDaily — Hawaii adult-use $1 billion opportunity (6 Feb 2026)).
  • Annualising $5,336,700 × 12 = $64,040,400 — consistent with the GET-implied 2023–2024 range, but do not present the annualised figure as an official annual total; CPPC itself and DOH epidemiologist Dr Lynn V. Le (House briefing, 17 February 2026, summarised at Citizen Portal — House briefing summary (17 Feb 2026)) cautioned legislators not to multiply a single monthly projection by 12 without adjusting for seasonality.
  • Patient spending (CPPC survey, n=805 registered/past medical patients in the design summary): average about $210 per month across all sources ($210.13 in the report table); about $183.58 per month at medical dispensaries, i.e. 86–87% of patient cannabis dollars through licensed dispensaries. The remainder is spread across friends/family, homegrow, online, delivery, cooperatives and other sources at 1–4% each in the report’s Table 5 (column order in the search text is scrambled — Verify the table before reproducing the small shares).
  • Version conflict to flag: the December 2025 original and the February/March 2026 updated CPPC version give different tourist figures (see §6.6). The medical-monthly figure ($5.34m) is stable across versions; the tourist and year-5 figures are not. Parts 0–5 must cite the version used for every CPPC number.

Method — what “sales” means in each series: DOH GET series: tax collected from dispensaries, not gross sales; GET-inclusive vs GET-exclusive treatment is not stated in the retrieved text — Verify. BioTrack series: seed-to-sale recorded retail sales at licensed dispensaries; whether GET is included in the BioTrack transaction value is not stated in the retrieved text — Verify. CPPC survey series: self-reported patient/adult expenditure, cross-validated to BioTrack for one month only. No series retrieved breaks sales out by product category (flower vs edibles vs vapes vs concentrates vs topicals) at state level. Product-category sales split: Unavailable from official sources.

6.2 Patients — in-state, by county, and out-of-state (329V)

6.2.1 Source and reporting rhythm

Source: DOH Medical Cannabis (329) Registry Program, monthly “Program Data” reports and the Program Statistics index page: health.hawaii.gov — Program Statistics. Monthly PDFs, e.g.: January 2026: January-2026-Report_Valid.pdf; March 2026: March-2026-Report_Valid.pdf; April 2026: April-2026-Report_Valid.pdf; August 2026: August-2026-Report_Valid.pdf; October 2025: October-2025-Report_Valid.pdf. DOH states the programme issues “roughly 1,500 329 Cards each month” and that valid-participant counts change daily as new/renewal applications are processed; month-end counts are posted around the 15th of the following month. Counts below are valid registrations at month-end, not cumulative ever-registered.

Use for Parts 0–5 cross-checks: the defensible “current” figures are 29,315 at 31 August 2026 (with county split, §6.2.3) and 29,142 in September 2026 (index only, no split retrieved). The programme is down roughly 17% from the August 2021 peak (35,444 → 29,315 = −17.3%) and down about 5% year-on-year at June (30,957 in June 2024 → 28,889 in June 2026 is a two-year comparison; the one-year June comparison is 29,399 in June 2025 → 28,889 in June 2026, −1.7% — state the comparison period explicitly, as secondary sources mix them).

Peak conflict — flag, do not resolve by picking silently: HB 302 HD2 (2025) states the peak was 35,444 in August 2021, falling “over fifteen per cent to 30,035 by November 2024”. The Marijuana Herald summary of the DOH July 2025 report calls 34,037 in May 2023 “the program’s high”. Both cannot be the all-time high. The likely explanation is that the Herald summary meant a recent high or mis-stated the date, but Verify against the DOH monthly series before Parts 0–5 state a peak. The secondary month-by-month compilation (Hawaii State Cannabis — statistics) shows 2022 monthly counts around 34,000–34,199, consistent with a 2021 peak above 35,000 having already fallen by 2022, and inconsistent with a May 2023 peak of 34,037 being higher than 2022 — further reason to treat the May 2023 “high” claim as Verify / likely erroneous.

Other registry counts (for Parts 0–5 cross-checks, counts only): caregivers: 2,817 at 30 Sep 2023; 2,797 at 29 Jun 2023; 2,525 at 28 Feb 2025; 2,389 at 31 Oct 2025; 2,330 in March 2026 (DOH March 2026 report snippet). Trend is downward with patients, but the caregiver series is less completely retrieved — Verify the full caregiver series from the monthly PDFs. Note: Part 5.4 gives 2,342 caregivers in August 2026 — the series continues downward then rises slightly into August; do not smooth the series. Certifying providers with ≥1 currently registered patient: 237 at 31 Jan 2026 (report text says “as of January 31, 2025” in one line — an apparent typo in the report, Verify); 239 at 31 Mar 2026; 244 at 30 Apr 2026; 248 at 31 Aug 2026. New vs renewal applications issued (examples): Jan 2026: 456 new / 1,211 renewal; Mar 2026: 439 new / 1,312 renewal; Apr 2026: 500 new / 1,173 renewal; Aug 2026: 484 new / 1,151 renewal; Jul 2025: 386 new / 1,129 renewal; Oct 2025: 434 new / 1,354 renewal. Leading condition, all months retrieved: severe pain, ~82–83% (82.84% Jan 2026; 82.94% Mar 2026; 82.76% Aug 2026; 82.88% Jul 2025). Second/third vary by county/age: PTSD, persistent muscle spasms, severe nausea. Full condition tables are in the monthly PDFs — not reproduced here beyond the headline, as this is Part 6, not Part 1.

6.2.4 Out-of-state (329V) patients — visitors, separate from the county table

Critical distinction for Parts 0–5: the “Out of state” row (99 in Aug 2026) in the in-state county table means in-state registrations using an out-of-state residential address or unknown address (DOH footnote). It is not the visitor (329V) count. The visitor programme is reported separately in the monthly reports’ out-of-state section and in secondary summaries of it. Never add the 99 to the 329V figures, and never present the 99 as “visitors”.

Visitor card terms (for Part 1, stated here only because they define the count): temporary 329V registration valid up to 60 days, renewable once in the calendar year for another 60 days; visitors may apply up to 60 days before arrival. Tourism-related sales from 329V patients: Unavailable as a dollar figure. No DOH source retrieved breaks BioTrack/dispensary sales into in-state vs 329V purchases. The 823 currently registered visitors at 31 Aug 2026 are ~2.8% of the in-state patient count (823 ÷ 29,315), but that ratio must not be converted into a sales share — visitors’ purchase frequency, basket size and length of stay differ from residents’, and no official purchase split exists. See §6.6 for CPPC’s projected (hypothetical adult-use) tourist demand, which is not current visitor sales.

6.3 Retail locations and production centres — counts over time

Licence structure (for context; full licence analysis is Part 4): 8 dispensary licences statewide, unchanged since selection on 29 April 2016 — 3 City and County of Honolulu, 2 Hawai‘i County, 2 Maui County, 1 Kaua‘i County (HRS §329D-2; DOH annual reports). Each licensee may operate up to 3 production centres and up to 2 retail dispensing locations, with DOH able to authorise up to 2 additional retail locations per licensee to serve a rural/underserved area. Plant cap: 7,500 per production centre, 15,000 per licensee. These caps are from the DOH Jan 2026 report text and secondary summaries — Verify the current HAR 11-850 text in Part 4, as interim rules were updated 17 November 2025.

DateProduction centresRetail locationsTotal facilitiesSource

Retail-count conflict — flag for Parts 4–5: DOH news releases state the 23rd retail location (Aloha Green, Kapolei, sales from 3 Jul 2023), the 24th (Pono Life Maui, Kīhei, 10 Oct 2023), and the 25th (Noa Botanicals, 345 Royal Hawaiian Ave., Honolulu, sales from 19 Apr 2025; DOH release 17 Apr 2025: DOH — Noa Botanicals 4th retail (17 Apr 2025)). The Jan 2026 report’s 24 retail locations at 23 Dec 2025 therefore implies a net loss of one retail location and three production centres during 2025, but no DOH source retrieved names which site(s) closed or ceased operation. Do not guess. The April 2025 news release also lists, by operator, locations that sum to 26 if every named address is counted (O‘ahu 11 + Hawai‘i Island 6 + Kaua‘i 2 + Maui 7) against its own stated statewide total of 25 — another arithmetic inconsistency in the release as retrieved (possibly one listed Maui or O‘ahu site had already closed, or one address is a duplicate/relocation). Parts 4–5 must reconcile the site-by-site list against the Jan 2026 report’s Table 1 before publishing any per-island retail table. Part 4.1.6 does that reconciliation as far as the excerpts allow (Maui 5 in Dec 2025 vs 6 in the April 2025 list) and leaves the Pono Kahului/Pā‘ia question as Gap G5.

Retail locations by county — latest defensible statement: because the site-by-site reconciliation is unresolved, the only county-level retail statement that can be made without inventing a split is: O‘ahu had 11 retail locations at the time of the April 2025 Noa release (stated in that release: “O‘ahu’s 11th retail dispensary”), and the 25th statewide. The full per-county split for Dec 2025 (24 locations) is given in Part 4.1.6 from Table 1 as opened in the Parts 4–5 pass (Honolulu 11, Hawai‘i 6, Maui 5, Kaua‘i 2) — use that split, not a proportion or the April 2025 list, for December 2025. Historical anchors that are stated, not derived: first sales August 2017 (Maui Grown Therapies, then Aloha Green); 9 retail locations statewide in early 2019 reporting (secondary, Hemp Gazette, 12 Mar 2019 — dated, do not use as current); 23rd in July 2023; 24th in October 2023; 25th in April 2025; 24 operational in December 2025.

Certified laboratories: 1 certified testing laboratory as of September 2024 (secondary summary of DOH). Current lab count for 2025–2026: Verify — the Jan 2026 report summary retrieved does not state a lab count. Do not carry “1 lab” forward as a 2026 fact without checking (see Part 4.2 / Part 5.3: count Unavailable).

6.4 Home grow vs dispensary purchasing — share

No official registry grow-site count was located. The monthly DOH Registry reports retrieved report patients, caregivers, providers and conditions — they do not, in the retrieved text, publish a count of registered grow sites or a count/share of patients who cultivate. Therefore:

  • Share of patients who grow at home (registry count): Unavailable. Do not state a percentage of patients growing from registry data.
  • What can be stated instead (survey-based, labelled as such): CPPC (state-commissioned, 2025/2026): 86–87% of patient cannabis dollars go through licensed dispensaries; average patient spend ~$210/month all sources, ~$184/month at dispensaries. This is a dollar share, not a patient-count share — a patient can both grow and buy, so 87% dollars ≠ 87% of patients buying exclusively. DOH rapid survey of patients/providers, 2022, as cited in SB 3132 SD1 (2024) findings (SB 3132 SD1 (PDF)): 55% of patients obtained cannabis exclusively from a licensed dispensary; up to 45% obtained it from other sources, including home cultivation. This is a 2022 survey, not a current registry count, and “other sources” includes friends/family, illicit and homegrow combined — it does not mean 45% grow. CPPC homegrow production estimate: “at any given time, residents may be producing between 3,500 and 46,000 pounds of cannabis through homegrow across medical patients and adult consumers.” The 13-fold range signals very high uncertainty; report the range, never a midpoint, and label it a modelled estimate, not a harvest measurement. CPPC access finding: nearly 68% of patients reported “plenty of supply” and fewer than 5% reported serious shortages; patients on Moloka‘i and Lāna‘i reported 70–90 minutes’ travel to the nearest dispensary (no dispensary is located on Moloka‘i or Lāna‘i in the operator lists retrieved — Verify in Part 3, as this is a material access fact for Maui County’s outer islands).

6.5 Official price data

Official DOH price series: Unavailable. No DOH monthly or annual report retrieved publishes an average price per gram, per ounce, or by product category. DOH’s BioTrack data would contain transaction prices, but DOH does not publish a price index from it in the material retrieved. CPPC’s survey collected patient-reported prices to cross-validate BioTrack totals, but a CPPC product-category price table was not extracted in this research — Verify in the full CPPC report before Parts 0–5 quote any CPPC price.

Do not average them.

6.6 Tourism-related sales — current vs projected (do not conflate)

Current visitor (329V) sales dollars: Unavailable (see §6.2.4). No official breakout exists in the material retrieved.

Projected tourist demand under a hypothetical adult-use market (CPPC, state-commissioned — projections, not current sales):

  • December 2025 original: tourists add at least $11.5 million per month (minimum, constant, seasonally variable) to total cannabis demand under adult-use; domestic tourists willing to spend $124.65 per trip on average; international tourists $12.46 per trip; domestic tourists are the main driver; minimal loss from Japanese tourists, gains from Canadian tourists. Sources: CPPC Dec 2025 text via CPPC Dec 2025 mirror (PDF) (mirror of DOH file — Verify against the DOH-hosted file, do not cite the mirror as the official source in the final deliverable); Marijuana Herald summary 2026: Marijuana Herald — CPPC market projection (Feb 2026); MJBizDaily 6 Feb 2026: MJBizDaily — $1 billion opportunity.
  • February/March 2026 updated CPPC version (DOH-hosted): tourists add at least $7.6 million per month; domestic tourists willing to spend $135.73–$178.00 per trip; international $14.63–$73.40 per trip. Source: CPPC updated (DOH-hosted PDF). This is a material version conflict. Parts 0–5 must state which CPPC version every tourist figure comes from and must not mix the $11.5m figure with the $135–$178 range, or the $7.6m figure with the $124.65 figure. The updated version’s ranges suggest revised survey weighting; the reason for the revision is not stated in the retrieved text — Verify.
  • Other CPPC projections (all hypothetical adult-use, 15% total tax scenario unless stated): current total market (medical + hemp + illicit/grey, all sources): $16.5–$32 million per month (Marijuana Herald summary of CPPC) — note Mugglehead’s summary phrases the current figure as “~$25 million at the moment” (Mugglehead — recreational cannabis in Hawaii), inside that range but not an official point estimate. Year-5 total market: $59–$95 million per month across all sources; $46–$90 million per month after adjusting for expected participation under a 15% total tax rate (updated version wording). Annualising either range without the seasonality caution in §6.1.3 would be misleading. Retail need: at least 65 retail outlets in year 1 under adult-use (residents + patients + tourists combined), vs 24–25 medical retail locations today; CPPC distribution across islands uses BRFSS 2020–2021 past-month consumer distribution, NSDUH 2023 lower-bound population, DOH patient distribution by island, and DBEDT 2023 tourism expenditure distribution. Adult-use tax optimum modelled: 15% total tax rate maximises revenue while maintaining participation (CPPC) — a model result, not a proposed law; label as such in Part 2. Non-patient adult spending today (CPPC survey): about $27.6 million per month in the February 2026 briefing summary (about $20m residents + $7.6m tourists — note the $7.6m here matches the updated tourist figure, confirming the briefing used the updated version); hemp products alone $6.17 million per month by non-patient adults, ~31% of that group’s combined hemp+cannabis market. Survey bases (CPPC design): adult consumers Hawai‘i n=233; registered/past medical patients n=805; past/potential tourists US n=1,009, Canada n=1,004, Japan n=489; sensitivity sample n=255 (numbers as extracted from the report’s methodology summary — Verify against the full methodology chapter, as the search text scrambles sample labels). Travel-intention findings: 57.4% of Japanese and 64.5% of Canadian respondents said legalisation would not affect travel plans; ~16% of Japanese respondents said they would be deterred on moral grounds; net effect on visitation described as “modest”.

6.7 Hemp — acreage, licences, production and product market

6.7.1 Regulatory frame for the market figures (summary only; Part 7 owns the licence detail)

Hawai‘i does not run its own USDA-approved state hemp plan in the period covered. Under Act 14 (2020), from 1 November 2020 growers must hold a USDA hemp production licence under the USDA Domestic Hemp Production Program; hemp processing/product rules are DOH. This is why USDA NASS figures and USDA licence counts, not a Hawai‘i Department of Agriculture (HDOA) series, are the market source — and why HDOA’s older pilot-programme figures (2019–2020) are not comparable to later USDA figures. Sources: Hemp CBD Business Plans — Hawaii hemp industry; Vote Hemp — Hawaii hemp law; Cannabis Business Times — Hawaii hemp farmers transition to USDA.

6.7.2 Acreage, production and licence counts by year

What this means: Hawai‘i’s licensed hemp cultivation collapsed from a 2020 licensed area in the hundreds of acres (94 planted) to a NASS-measured 17–18 acres in 2021–2022 and to withheld/too-small-to-report thereafter. No official 2025 or 2026 Hawai‘i hemp acreage or production figure was located. Any claim of a “growing Hawai‘i hemp acreage” in 2025–2026 would be unsupported. The Task Force’s own assessment (Aug 2024) is that there is “currently no existing Industrial Hemp industry in Hawai‘i” in the general-fibre sense, apart from a 4-acre test, while the cannabinoid sector is the only active segment — source: Hemp Task Force — findings by hemp consultant (PDF).

6.7.3 Hemp product market figures

  • $54 million CBD products sold in Hawai‘i in 2020; local farmers could capture 25% of that established market. Source: Hawai‘i Hemp Task Force report (Aug 2024), via Civil Beat summary Dec 2024: KPUA / Civil Beat — hemp growers (Dec 2024) and Hemp CBD Business Plans. This is a 2020 figure, based on Task Force analysis — it is not a 2024–2026 market size, and it predates the DOH manufactured-hemp-product rules and the Act 269 retailer registry. Do not present it as current.
  • CPPC hemp spending (survey estimate, 2025/2026): $6.17 million per month by non-patient adults on hemp products (~31% of that group’s combined market); see §6.6. This is a survey/model estimate for a defined sub-population, not total Hawai‘i hemp retail sales. Total Hawai‘i hemp retail sales (all consumers, all channels) for any recent year: Unavailable from official sources.
  • Hemp retailer/distributor registration (market-structure change, not a sales figure): under Act 269 (signed 2 July 2025, HB 1482), from 1 January 2026 all businesses selling/distributing manufactured hemp products (MHP) in Hawai‘i, including online and out-of-state sellers, must hold a DOH OMCCR Certificate of Registration ($50, five-year period); grace period to 30 June 2026; enforcement from 1 July 2026. DOH must post a current list of valid hemp registrants on the OMCCR website (HRS §328G-9(f)). Sources: DOH Hemp Program: health.hawaii.gov — Hemp Program; DOH news release 25-133, 4 Nov 2025: DOH news release 25-133 (PDF); DOH MHP consumer FAQ (current as of 16 Dec 2025): DOH — Consumers of MHP FAQ. Count of registered hemp retailers/distributors at any 2026 date: Unavailable from the material retrieved — the registrant list itself was not extracted. Parts 5/7 must pull the live list; do not estimate a retailer count from the $50 fee or from any press figure.
  • No official Hawai‘i hemp product tax revenue series was located. Hemp products are not subject to a cannabis-specific excise tax in the material retrieved; GET treatment of hemp products is a Part 2 question — Verify, do not assume the medical-cannabis GET treatment applies identically.
  • Federal hemp-definition impact on Hawai‘i market size: Unavailable / Verify after 11 December 2026. No Hawai‘i official source retrieved quantifies how much current Hawai‘i hemp-product sales would fall under the full total-THC definition and 0.4 mg/container cap. Parts 1/7 must not project a Hawai‘i impact figure from national estimates.

Consistency with Run 1: medical-only; no adult-use law; no new cannabis authority; Act 241 SLH 2015 = dispensary system, Act 241 SLH 2025 (HB 302) = medical amendments (always cite year); 8 licences 3/2/2/1/0, no new since 29 Apr 2016, 10 production + 24 retail = 34 at 23 Dec 2025; patients 29,315 at 31 Aug 2026, Sep 2026 index 29,142; 329V 823 current + 298 pending at 31 Aug 2026; GET retail 4% + 0.5% surcharge = 4.5% all counties incl. Maui from 1 Jan 2024 (DOH "Maui had not adopted surcharge" stale/rejected); GET collected 2017–2025 $17,247,813 is TAX not sales. No municipal governments below county level; City and County of Honolulu covers all of O'ahu; Kalawao state-administered.

Part 7 — Hemp licences

7(a) USDA hemp producer licence — Hawaii uses USDA programme directly

  • No Hawaii state plan. USDA plan table, updated 17 Feb 2026, lists Hawaii as "USDA Hemp Producer License" — USDA issues directly because Hawaii has no USDA-approved state plan. https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-review (1 Nov 2026). Corroboration: Hemp Industry Daily 14 Sep 2020, Hawaii opted to halt in-state regulation of production plans, growers apply directly to USDA: https://hempindustrydaily.com/hawaii-virginia-eliminate-in-state-hemp-regulation-while-usda-approves-utah-and-tribal-production-plans/
  • Application route: USDA Hemp eManagement Platform (HeMP), rolling applications through year; paper/mail route also described in USDA summaries. https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/information-for-hemp-growers — USDA public search tool exists for verifying producer licence (business name, licence number, status, jurisdiction) — USDA AMS Specialty Crops newsletter May 2023: https://content.govdelivery.com/accounts/USDAAMS/bulletins/358fee9?reqfrom=share . Live HeMP public-search count for Hawaii NOT run (no live-browser access in subagent), so no current count asserted.
  • Fees: No USDA application fee and no renewal fee, per secondary USDA summaries — secondary-verified only, not confirmed against USDA fee page (Gap 4).
  • Term: valid until 31 December of year three years after year issued. 7 CFR §990.22 https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/7/990.22 ; see also §990.21 https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/CFR-2022-title7-vol8/pdf/CFR-2022-title7-vol8-sec990-21.pdf . Licences may not be sold, assigned, transferred, pledged, or otherwise disposed of.
  • Felony bar: no licence unless criminal history report(s) confirm all key participants not convicted of felony under state/federal law relating to controlled substance within past 10 years, unless exception in 7 CFR §990.20(b) applies. FBI criminal history report must be dated within 60 days of application, per secondary USDA summaries + §990.21 extract.
  • Reporting (federal, stated exactly): report hemp crop acreage to USDA FSA within 30 days of planting, using FSA-578; lot ID Farm–Tract–Field–Subfield. Annual Production Report (AMS-28) via HeMP on/before 15 Dec each year, due for all licensees regardless of planting. Remediation/Disposal Report (AMS-27) via HeMP within 30 days of remediation/disposal where lab result exceeds federal limit; remediation options incl. removing/destroying flowers + resampling, or drying/grinding whole plant for biomass resample; destruction options incl. deep burial, mowing/shredding, composting, tillage, burning. Lab testing must be by DEA-registered lab for delta-9 THC post-decarboxylation, per USDA producer checklist summaries.
  • Hawaii state overlay: state adds no USDA inspections/sampling/penalties beyond growing without USDA licence, if licensee complies with USDA — see §141-42(g) in 7(d). State does impose cultivation setbacks (300 ft / 100 ft), transport reporting to Department of Agriculture and Biosecurity (DAB), labelling rules in §141-42.
  • Current licence/grower count — Unavailable as live figure; dated series only (do not merge): 101 active USDA licences in 2020; 58 active, 39 expired, 4 surrendered in 2024; only 4 of 2024 USDA farmers held DOH hemp processing licence. Source: Hawai'i Hemp Task Force Findings & Recommendations 14 Aug 2024 https://dab.hawaii.gov/wp-content/uploads/2024/08/8-14-Hawaii-Hemp-Task-Force-SM.pdf . About 91 USDA-licensed farmers circa 2022 (secondary Civil Beat reporting, via Senator Mike Gabbard site): https://www.mikegabbard.com/in-print/hemp-farmers-are-fed-up-with-government-regulations-hampering-the-industrys-potential . 51 licensed growers July 2025 (secondary USDA-search figure from Run 1 — secondary only). No live count as of 1 Nov 2026 obtained. USDA plan table gives plan status only, not count.
  • Info link: https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/information-for-hemp-growers

7(b) DOH hemp processor permit — HRS ch 328G / HAR ch 11-37

  • What it allows (DOH Sep 2025 processor sheet, opened directly https://health.hawaii.gov/hempprogram/files/2025/09/HempProcessorPermit-Sep-2025.pdf ): pathways requiring permit + HAR 11-37 compliance: (A) processing hemp biomass to make crude extract; (B) processing hemp biomass into manufactured hemp product (MHP); (C) using MHP as ingredient to make another MHP; (D) all packaging/labelling activities; (E) repackaging/relabeling from bulk MHP or crude extract. "Crude extract" = initial cannabinoid-containing extraction, not fit for use/consumption, requiring further processing. "MHP" = cannabinoid-containing product used topically or taken orally in form allowed in interim rules.
  • Applicant requirements (same DOH sheet): complete application with permit fee + supporting documentation. Valid USDA licence to grow hemp OR consent to background check establishing proof of no disqualifying felony conviction. Indoor facility + planned processing operations (e.g. extraction methods) comply with local zoning/building/fire codes; OR operations do not include heat, volatile compounds or gases under pressure and occur in enclosed indoor facility exempt from building permit/code under HRS §46-88, or in food hub/agricultural park. Applicants sharing facility such as food hub/commercial kitchen with Food Safety Branch-permitted business should consult permit owner + FSB. Facility design description showing compliance with HAR 11-37 facility provisions (cleanable materials, ventilation, source water, plumbing, hand-wash sinks/toilets, etc.); consult DOH Environmental Management Division / Environmental Health Services Division and, for solvents/hazardous waste, DOH Hazardous Waste Program.
  • Fees — CONFIRMED from rule text (resolves Run 1 "$500 processor — verify" gap). HAR §11-37-44(a): "The fee for a new or renewal permit shall be $500 plus an online application processing fee." HAR §11-37-43(a): to ensure timely renewal, processor "shall submit a complete renewal application … at least sixty days prior to the expiration date of the current permit." HAR §11-37-46: permit "shall have a term of one year" and "shall not be assigned or otherwise transferred to another person"; issuance requires confirmation applicant/representative does not have disqualifying conviction for state/federal felony related to controlled substance during 10 years prior to application. Source: HAR ch 11-37, Amendment and Compilation dated 30 Dec 2025, compiled 28 Jan 2026: https://health.hawaii.gov/hempprogram/files/2026/02/Standard11-37HAR-20260128.pdf
  • HAR 11-37 processor structure (same compilation): Subchapter 5 comprises §§11-37-40 to 11-37-49: §11-37-40 Permit required; §11-37-41 Eligibility; §11-37-42 Application; §11-37-43 Renewal; §11-37-44 Fees; §11-37-45 Denial; §11-37-46 Permit issuance, term, conditions; §11-37-47 Changes in information (30 days' notice; relocation or new facility requires new permit application, no processing at new facility until new permit issues); §11-37-48 Permit revocation; §11-37-49 Surrender (no refund of any portion of fee on voluntary surrender). §11-37-40 in substance: no person shall process hemp biomass or produce MHP without valid permit; processor shall not begin processing until DOH issues permit; copy of current valid permit must be displayed in facility at all times. §11-37-41 eligibility in substance: applicant shall have Hawaii tax ID + registered to do business in Hawaii unless sole proprietorship; application fee nonrefundable, application without fee deemed incomplete. §11-37-50(a): processors shall obtain hemp biomass only from producer holding valid USDA licence under 7 U.S.C. §1639q or state/tribal plan approved under §1639p.
  • 500-foot setback — CONFIRMED in rule text; earlier-session correction. Prior pass reported no "500 feet" match in HAR compilation — that was search failure, corrected here: HAR §11-37-51(a) verbatim in substance: "Hemp biomass shall not be processed into crude extract or a manufactured hemp product within five hundred feet of a preexisting playground, school, state park, state recreation area, residential neighborhood, hospital, or daycare facility." §11-37-51(b): processing facility shall not be located within house/dwelling unit/residential apartment/other residential structure; §11-37-51(c): enclosed indoor facility complying with all applicable zoning/building/fire codes, secured against unauthorised entry + contamination. Same HAR compilation. Scope tension to flag, not resolve by assertion: DOH processor sheet states 500-foot requirement applies to pathways A/B only and "does NOT apply" to C/D/E — but rule text as read says "processed into crude extract or a manufactured hemp product" without express C/D/E carve-out, and making MHP from MHP literally is processing into MHP. DOH guidance reading narrower than rule text on face; treat C/D/E exemption as DOH guidance, Verify vs DOH interpretation or rule amendment. Do NOT confuse with (i) medical-cannabis 750-foot buffer HRS §329D-22 (production centres/dispensaries; schools/playgrounds only), or (ii) hemp cultivation setbacks HRS §141-42 (300 ft / 100 ft, see 7(d)).
  • Testing/labelling duties (general): Subchapter 3 Testing (§§11-37-20 to 11-37-25: lab requirements, reserve samples, content testing, contamination testing/limits, accessibility of results, recordkeeping); Subchapter 4 Packaging and Labeling (§§11-37-30 to 11-37-34); Subchapter 6 General processor requirements (§§11-37-50 to 11-37-56: biomass sourcing, facility requirements, solvents/processing practices, storage, waste disposal, recordkeeping — records retained at facility ≥2 years — SOPs); Subchapter 7 Facility standards (§§11-37-60 to 11-37-66); Subchapter 8 Quality control (§§11-37-70 to 11-37-75: batch records, QC review, returned product, complaints, recalls); Subchapter 9 Enforcement (§§11-37-80 to 11-37-82: inspection, embargo/detention, seizure/disposition). Batch testing for cannabinoid content + contaminants before sale, QR code/website access to batch-matched lab results, no sale of failing product remain Run 1 position, not contradicted. Solvents banned: benzene, carbon tetrachloride, 1,2-dichloroethane, 1,1-dichloroethene, 1,1,1-trichloroethane; no butane in open system (§11-37-52). Waste meeting HRS §712-1240 "marijuana" definition disposed under ch 23-200 (§11-37-54). Same HAR compilation.
  • Current processor count/list — Unavailable. No processor count/names extracted. Processors with valid permit automatically added to distributor/retailer registry (see 7(c)), so processor + registrant lists overlap, must not be added together. Task Force figure (4 DOH processing licences among 2024 USDA farmers) dated 2024, not current count.
  • Info link: https://health.hawaii.gov/hempprogram/ ; processor sheet above.

7(c) DOH manufactured-hemp-product distributor/retailer Certificate of Registration — HRS §328G-9 / Act 269, SLH 2025

  • Official info page (opened directly https://health.hawaii.gov/hempprogram/ ): Retailers = any business selling/offering MHP to consumers in Hawaii; distributors = any business selling/offering MHP to retailers in Hawaii; online sellers = any business retailing/distributing MHP in Hawaii via online services, incl. online sellers operating outside Hawaii. Application available 1 Jan 2026 at 0800 HST; account creation required before applying; application fee $50.00; valid 5 years; OMCCR grace period with enforcement beginning 1 July 2026; grace period does not allow sale of non-compliant products (no flower, vapes, etc.; THC potency limits, testing, labelling still apply). Conflict flagged: secondary summary (LegalClarity) claims DOH enforcement began Feb 2026 — contradicts DOH's own 1 July 2026 enforcement date, rejected in favour of DOH page: https://legalclarity.org/is-cbd-legal-in-hawaii-thc-limits-and-state-laws/
  • Statute (opened directly, read in full) HRS §328G-9 http://data.capitol.hawaii.gov/hrscurrent/Vol06_Ch0321-0344/HRS0328G/HRS_0328G-0009.htm — verbatim operative provisions:
  • (a) "The department shall establish and maintain a registry of all manufactured hemp product distributors and manufactured hemp product retailers doing business in the State."
  • (b) "Beginning January 1, 2026, no manufactured hemp product distributor or manufactured hemp product retailer shall sell or offer for sale manufactured hemp products without a certificate of registration issued by the department pursuant to this section."
  • (c) Application on department-prescribed form + "an application fee of $50."
  • (e) After receipt of complete + accurate application + payment, DOH shall list applicant as registrant + issue certificate; duplicate certificate fee $10 per copy.
  • (f) "The department shall post on its website a current list of registrants on the registry."
  • (g) Certificate "valid for five years" + renewed by completed renewal application + "a renewal fee of $50."
  • (h) Fees non-transferable + non-refundable, must be fully paid before issuance of new/renewed certificate.
  • (i) Certificate "shall be displayed at all times in a conspicuous place at the place of business where the sale of manufactured hemp products is conducted and a digital reproduction of the certificate of registration shall be displayed conspicuously on the website of a registrant conducting sales online."
  • (j) DOH may suspend/revoke for failure to comply with ch 328G + rules, pursuant to §328G-6 procedures.
  • (k) Carriers/entities that ship goods not subject to §328G-9 by reason of receipt/carriage/holding/shipping/delivery in usual course unless they sell/offer for sale MHP.
  • (l) Hemp processors with valid §328G-2 permit "shall be issued a certificate of registration and added to the registry by the department for the sale and distribution of manufactured hemp products at the locations indicated on the permit," with same expiration date as permit; application + renewal fees in (c)+(g) "shall not apply" to such processors.
  • (m) "This section shall not apply to the sale of the following manufactured hemp products: (1) Products that are topically applied; or (2) Tinctures; provided that tinctures shall not be sold to individuals under the age of twenty-one; provided further that the manufactured hemp products otherwise comply with this chapter and any rules adopted pursuant to this chapter." History: [L 2025, c 269, pt of §1]. Act 269 = HB 1482, signed 2 July 2025 (Gov. Msg. No. 1372).
  • Exception-list resolution — important correction to Run 1. Statute exception list is ONLY (1) topically applied products + (2) tinctures (21+ proviso). It does NOT list GRAS products or industrial hemp as §328G-9(m) exceptions. Run 1 broader DOH FAQ formulation — "Unless an exception applies (i.e., sale of tinctures, topicals, GRAS products, or industrial hemp)" — confirmed as current DOH guidance (DOH Consumers of MHP FAQ, current as of 16 Dec 2025: https://health.hawaii.gov/hempprogram/consumers-of-mhp-faq/ ), but broader than statute text. Statute list controls; FAQ list is conflict/Verify item. Possible explanation, noted not asserted: GRAS/industrial products may fall outside "manufactured hemp product" definition or outside ch 328G applicability under §328G-8 (which, per Act 263, provides chapter applies only to processing hemp biomass into crude extract or into MHP, processing crude extract into MHP, using MHP as ingredient, + their sale/distribution) rather than being §328G-9(m) exceptions — but that explanation itself is Verify vs §§328G-1, 328G-8. Do not merge two lists.
  • HAR 11-37 distributor/retailer subchapter (TOC, opened directly): Subchapter 2 comprises §11-37-11 Distributor and retailer registration; §11-37-12 Display of certificate; §11-37-13 Suspension/revocation of certificate; §11-37-14 Buyer due diligence; recordkeeping; §11-37-15 Prohibition on sale of tincture to persons under 21; §11-37-16 Distributor and retailer location restriction. Full text §§11-37-11 to 11-37-16 NOT read (re-open rate-limited; Gap 1) — display, due-diligence, location-restriction, suspension details beyond statute therefore stated from §328G-9 only. Search extract confirms §11-37-12 tracks statute on display (conspicuous place + conspicuous digital reproduction online), but not substitute for full rule read. Secondary DOH retailer application instructions (Sep 2026 file) indicate applicants incl. out-of-state businesses must submit business/legal entity registered with DCCA Business Registration (BREG), + multiple retail/distributor locations require separate registration per location — Verify vs HAR §11-37-11/§11-37-16 before stating location rule as rule: https://health.hawaii.gov/hempprogram/files/2026/09/Instructions-for-retailer-app-v4.pdf
  • Live registrant list count — Unavailable. Hemp Program page states registrants under tab "LIST OF HEMP REGISTRANTS" pursuant to §328G-9(f), but tab contents/count not returned by page text or searches. Searches returned only program page + secondary summaries, no count/names. Do not estimate from July 2026 secondary press figure ("fewer than / about 50 businesses had registered at enforcement opening" from Run 1 — secondary only). DOH outreach release confirms registry covers in-state + out-of-state sellers equally, $50 for 5 years, application link from 1 Jan 2026: https://governor.hawaii.gov/newsroom/doh-news-release-doh-launches-outreach-and-education-initiative-ahead-of-2026-hemp-retail-registration-requirement/
  • Enforcement/penalty context (statute, via search extract of current capitol text) HRS §328G-6(a): "Any person who violates this chapter or any rule adopted by the department pursuant to this chapter shall be fined no more than $10,000 for each separate offense. Any action taken to collect the penalty provided for in this subsection shall be considered a civil action." Director may also by order impose forfeiture of products, revocation of permit or suspension/revocation of certificate, or any combination; order becomes final unless hearing requested in writing within 20 days of service. §328G-10 violations additionally subject to nuisance abatement proceedings under HRS ch 712, part V, + §328G-10 products to forfeiture under ch 712A; ch 328G violation constitutes unfair/deceptive act or practice. Source http://data.capitol.hawaii.gov/hrscurrent/Vol06_Ch0321-0344/HRS0328G/HRS_0328G-0006.htm . §328G-10 (unlawful hemp distribution/retailing), §328G-11 (inspection authority), §328G-12 (forfeiture/confiscation/seizure/disposition) listed in chapter index but full text not re-read. Chapter index http://data.capitol.hawaii.gov/hrscurrent/Vol06_Ch0321-0344/HRS0328G/HRS_0328G-.htm
  • Info link: https://health.hawaii.gov/hempprogram/

7(d) Other hemp authorisations, former pilot licences, Run 1 gap resolutions

  • HRS ch 141, Part III — Commercial Hemp Production — CURRENT TEXT, correcting Run 1. https://data.capitol.hawaii.gov/hrscurrent/Vol03_Ch0121-0200D/HRS0141/HRS_0141-0042.htm . Part heading note: "Part repealed July 1, 2027. L 2023, c 263, §§15, 19." Supersedes Run 1 older Justia note "repealed June 30, 2022. L 2020, c 14, §9" — that 2022 date superseded by later amendments, must not be cited as current.
  • §141-42(a) verbatim in substance: "It shall be legal for an individual or entity to produce hemp, as defined in title 7 United States Code section 1639o, if that individual or entity has a license to produce hemp, issued by the Secretary of the United States Department of Agriculture pursuant to title 7 United States Code section 1639q; provided that: (1) Hemp shall not be grown within three hundred feet of pre-existing real property comprising a playground, childcare facility, or school" (with pilot-programme grandfathering for licensees licensed in those areas before 27 Aug 2020); "(2) Hemp shall not be grown within one hundred feet of any pre-existing house, dwelling unit, residential apartment, or other residential structure that is not owned or controlled by the license holder" (same grandfathering); "(3) Hemp shall not be grown in any house, dwelling unit, residential apartment, or other residential structure, unless that structure is part of a United States Department of Agriculture area."
  • Correction: Run 1 stated 500-foot cultivation setbacks from 2020 Act 14 text. Current §141-42 text as opened gives 300 feet / 100 feet. Present 300/100 as current; amendment history: [L 2020, c 14, pt of §3, §9; am L 2022, c 137, §2; am L 2023, c 263, §3; am L 2025, c 236, §4]. Whether 500-foot figure was ever enacted text or secondary error is Verify vs Act 14 enrolled text.
  • §141-42(b): USDA-licensed producer may transport hemp within State to facility authorised by law to process hemp or to another licensed producer's grow area, provided transportation reported to DAB; DAB may require movement reports incl. USDA test results, may deny authorisation if hemp does not comply.
  • §141-42(c): growing hemp without USDA licence — fine ≤$10,000 per separate offence; notice of violation may be accompanied by cease-and-desist order, violation of which is further violation; collection is civil action.
  • §141-42(e): USDA-licensed producer in Hawaii may sell hemp biomass.
  • §141-42(f): labelling/advertising identity statement must identify % of Hawaii-grown hemp; product containing non-Hawaii hemp must identify origin + % from outside Hawaii; multiple origins must be identified as "United States" or "Foreign" if from outside US.
  • §141-42(g): USDA-licensed producer shall follow all USDA inspection/sampling rules/protocols; "The State shall not require other inspections or sampling. The State shall not issue notices of violations or impose penalties upon any hemp producer licensed by the United States Department of Agriculture; provided that the licensee complies with all of the requirements imposed by the United States Department of Agriculture. The State shall impose no penalty with respect to the production of hemp, except penalties for growing hemp without a license issued by the United States Department of Agriculture."
  • §141-42(h): "hemp biomass" means stalks of hemp plants for purposes of §141-42 — note narrow definition differs from DOH processor-sheet usage (floral + leaf plant material); flag difference, do not merge.
  • Chapter 328G repeal 1 July 2027 — RESOLVED as current statutory note, with remaining extension question. Chapter index note, opened directly: "Chapter repealed July 1, 2027. L 2023, c 263, §15. (This Note supersedes the Note in the main volume.)" Chapter index above. Act 263, SLH 2023 = HB 1359, H.D. 2, S.D. 1, became law without Governor's signature 11 July 2023 (Governor's message, DAB file: https://dab.hawaii.gov/wp-content/uploads/2024/07/Act263-SLH2023-GM1377_HB1359-Hemp.pdf ). Act 263 §1 finding (8): extend State's hemp processor law through 1 July 2027. 2024 House committee report (HB 2444, H.D. 1, HSCR 51-24) confirms Act 263 "extended the repeal date for the hemp processors chapter to July 1, 2027." Same repeal note applies to ch 141, Part III (§§15, 19) and, via HRS §329-1 notes, to "hemp" + "marijuana" definition repeal-and-reenactment 1 July 2027 (L 2023, c 263, §15). Remaining Verify: whether any 2025/2026 act extended 1 July 2027 sunset further or made ch 328G / Part III permanent. No such extension located; SB 1548 (2024) text found in search would have changed repeal date from 2025 to 2027 in earlier draft — not extension beyond 2027: https://capitolwebsite.azurewebsites.net/sessions/session2024/bills/SB1548_.HTM . State sunset as current law 1 Nov 2026, flag extension as follow-up, not assumption.
  • HAR 11-37 interim-rule sunset + interaction with repeal — SUBSTANTIALLY resolved. HRS §328G-4(b), via FindLaw (current as of 1 Jan 2025) + Act 263 AG testimony: "The department may adopt and amend interim rules, which shall be exempt from chapter 91 and chapter 201M, to effectuate the purposes of this chapter; provided that any interim rules shall only remain in effect until July 1, 2027, or until rules are adopted pursuant to subsection (a), whichever occurs sooner." Sources https://codes.findlaw.com/hi/division-1-government/hi-rev-st-sect-328g-4/ and http://data.capitol.hawaii.gov/sessions/Session2023/Testimony/HB1359_SD1_TESTIMONY_JDC-WAM_04-04-23_.PDF . HAR 11-37 compilation itself (28 Jan 2026) still labels chapter "(Interim Rules)" + gives no permanent-rule adoption date. Therefore: interim rules remain interim as of Jan 2026 compilation; statutory interim authority runs to same 1 July 2027 sunset (or earlier permanent-rule adoption); if ch 328G repeals 1 July 2027 without extension, interim-rule authority repeals with it, unless separate permanent-rule or extension act intervenes. Verify: exact current §328G-4(a) wording — FindLaw says "shall adopt rules," while Act 263 draft markup suggested "may" replacing "shall"; confirm vs current capitol §328G-4 text before quoting (a) verbatim (Gap 7). Related: HRS §846-2.7(41) criminal-history-check authority for DOH/designee on hemp processor permit applicants carries note "[Repeal and reenactment on July 1, 2027. L 2024, c 110, §4.]" — consistent with 2027 sunset cluster.
  • Tincture 85 mg/bottle derivation — NOT resolved as rule figure. Text search of HAR 11-37 compilation PDF for "85" returned only CAS numbers + page number — no 85 mg tincture figure in rule text located. Run 1 position stands: tincture rule max 1 oz per container, 3 mg total THC per serving (HAR §11-37-5, per Run 1; full §11-37-5 text not re-read — Gap 2); DOH consumer guidance states up to 85 mg per 1-oz bottle. Arithmetic note, labelled calculation not rule: 3 mg × ~28 servings ≈ 84 mg, consistent with 85 mg if 1-oz bottle yields about 28 dropperful servings — but servings-per-bottle definition not located in rule text, so derivation remains Verify. Do not present 85 mg as HAR cap.
  • Universal 21+ vs tincture-only sale ban — SUBSTANTIALLY resolved in favour of tincture-only as statutory sale ban.
  • Statutory sale ban located: HRS §328G-13 (Tinctures; persons under 21), http://data.capitol.hawaii.gov/hrscurrent/Vol06_Ch0321-0344/HRS0328G/HRS_0328G-0013.htm : (a) "It shall be unlawful to sell or furnish a tincture to a person under twenty-one years of age." (b) Retail sellers shall check ID if purchaser reasonably appears <27. (d) Unlawful for person <21 to purchase/possess any tincture. (e) Seller violation: $500 first offence, $500–$2,000 subsequent; under-21 purchaser/possessor: $10 first offence, $50 subsequent (no part suspended) or 48–72 hours' community service; tincture in possession seized, summarily forfeited + destroyed following administrative/judicial finding, with ch 712A procedures not applying. [L 2025, c 269, pt of §1]. HAR §11-37-15 is matching rule (title only confirmed).
  • §328G-9(m) confirms tinctures excepted from registration only "provided that tinctures shall not be sold to individuals under the age of twenty-one."
  • No universal 21+ sale ban for all MHP located in §328G-9. "Not for sale to persons under the age of 21" wording in Run 1 is labelling statement required for MHP generally (HAR labelling subchapter / DOH FAQ), not, on evidence located, universal statutory sale prohibition.
  • Explanation found for confusion — proposed bills, NOT law: HB 1109, H.D. 1 (2025) would have prohibited sale of any MHP to persons <21, but carried defective effective date 1 July 3000, not enacted: https://capitolwebsite.azurewebsites.net/sessions/session2026/bills/HB1109_HD1_.pdf . HB 2613 (2026) would create 21+ ban for cannabinoid drink products only; status Introduced, referred 2 Feb 2026 — PROPOSED, not law: https://legiscan.com/HI/text/HB2613 . Neither may be presented as current law.
  • Hawaii response (or absence) to federal 12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026 hemp dates as of 1 Nov 2026 — NO response located. No Hawaii statute, HAR amendment, DOH guidance, or AG opinion responding to PL 119-37 §781 / PL 119-103 §2019 located in Run 1 or this session's searches. State absence as of 1 Nov 2026, note mismatch (Hawaii allows 5 mg per edible package, 0.5 mg per beverage container, up to 85 mg guidance per tincture bottle, vs federal baseline 0.4 mg per container after 11 Dec 2026), flag re-check after 11 Dec 2026. Do not present Hawaii as already compliant. Secondary Oct 2026 summary describing federal timeline (synthetically derived cannabinoids losing hemp status 12 Nov 2026; total-THC/0.4 mg definition applying 11 Dec 2026) consistent with given baseline but not Hawaii response, must not be cited as one.
  • Former HDOA pilot licences (historical only). Hawaii industrial hemp pilot programme under HRS ch 141 (2017 pilot; University of Hawaii at Mānoa research in 2016 in secondary summaries; HDOA pilot licences from June 2018, 3 growers in secondary reporting; $500 application non-refundable, $250/year + $2/acre in Run 1 secondary figures). Pilot superseded 1 Nov 2020 by USDA licensing under Act 14, SLH 2020 (HB 1819, signed 27 Aug 2020, Gov. Msg. No. 1118). From 1 Nov 2020, Hawaii growers must hold USDA licence. HDOA pilot licences historical only, confer no current cultivation right. Maui example (secondary, Maui Now tag page): Pacific Biodiesel / Imua Energy 10-acre site off Honoapi‘ilani Highway near Waikapū, Central Maui, part of larger 115-acre biofuel crop farm; described as Maui's first state-licensed industrial hemp farm, HDOA licence for research on growth/cultivation/marketing, extract manufactured by Imua Energy + distributed by Maiden Hawaii Naturals: https://mauinow.com/tag/maui-hemp-farm/feed/ . Historical pilot-era material, not current licence. HAR ch 4-161 (Hemp Production Interim Rules, historical pilot rules) superseded for cultivation by USDA programme; do not cite as current cultivation rule without checking current status (Gap 11).
  • Other hemp authorisations — none located as standalone current licences. No standalone Hawaii hemp seed licence, hemp transport licence, or hemp research licence located as current authorisation separate from: USDA producer licence (cultivation, incl. seed production within licensed lot), DOH processor permit (processing), DOH distributor/retailer registration (sale/distribution). Seed sale restricted: viable hemp seeds may be sold only to USDA-licensed producer or permitted processor (HAR §11-37-3(f), Run 1 position). Transport by USDA licensee to authorised processor or another licensee's grow area under §141-42(b) (reported to DAB), not separate transport licence. Proposed-only items excluded from current law: SB 620 (2025/2026) proposing state-funded hemp manufacturing facility + amendments to §§328G-2/328G-3 — PROPOSED, not law. SB 2178 (2026) / SB 2178, S.D. 1, "Hawaii Industrial Hemp Infrastructure and Innovation Act," proposing DAB industrial hemp programme (education, advisory board, CTAHR research support; interim rules by 1 Oct 2026 in introduced text; licensing from 1 July 2027 in amended text) — PROPOSED, not law; UH testimony 4 Mar 2026 + DAB duplication concern on record, final disposition not located: https://legiscan.com/HI/text/SB2178/id/3322303/Hawaii-2026-SB2178-Introduced.html and https://legiscan.com/HI/text/SB2178/id/3368899/Hawaii-2026-SB2178-Amended.html . Medical-cannabis dispensary licences (8, ch 329D) are NOT hemp licences, confer no hemp-processing right beyond Act 241, SLH 2025 retail sale of hemp products/accessories at retail locations (Run 1 position); do not conflate with processor permits.

Part 8 — Cannabis-zoned properties

Legal frame (Run 1, restated)

  • HRS §329D-22(a): production centres/dispensaries must comply with all county zoning ordinances; production centre permitted in any area where agricultural production permitted (except as provided in ch 329D); no production centre or dispensary within 750 feet of real property comprising playground or school.
  • HRS §46-4(f): no law/county ordinance/rule shall prohibit use of land for ch 329D production centres/dispensaries, provided land otherwise zoned for agriculture, manufacturing, or retail purposes.
  • No county cannabis-specific zoning category, cap, ban, or moratorium located in Run 1; counties control where (district, setbacks, parking, building, SMA), not whether state programme exists.
  • Dispensary licence itself not transferable by statute (§329D-3: "A dispensary license may not be sold or otherwise transferred from one person to another person") — critical limit for any "dispensary for sale" listing: what can be sold is real estate/business assets, not licence itself, unless lawful ownership-change route verified (still open — Gap 14).
  • DOH retail-site addresses already public (Run 1 Part 3 list, Apr 2025, 25 sites; Dec 2025 count 24) are operating facilities, NOT for-sale properties, listed separately; no operating dispensary address found listed for sale.

Strict standard applied

Property counts as verified cannabis-zoned ONLY if (i) current public listing shows it, AND (ii) zoning (county district + State Land Use district) verified from county/state source as permitting claimed cannabis/hemp use, AND (iii) 750-foot school/playground buffer (HRS §329D-22) + §46-4(f) agriculture/manufacturing/retail condition checked for that parcel. Otherwise listed separately as marketed-but-unverified, or result is ZERO.

Result: ZERO properties meet strict verified standard

  • Properties passing (i)+(ii)+(iii): 0
  • Closest candidates (marketed-but-unverified): 2 (table below)
  • Properties searched + excluded as non-cannabis: 1 farm business + general land/commercial listings (below)
