10.1 No federally recognised tribes in Hawai'i — confirmed from official federal sources

  • Finding: no federally recognised tribes in Hawai'i, no tribal reservation in Hawai'i, therefore no tribal cannabis compact, tribal dispensary licence, or tribal hemp plan in Hawai'i.
  • BIA list published under Federally Recognized Indian Tribe List Act of 1994 (Pub. L. 103-454) + 25 CFR §83.6(a). Most recent list before 1 Nov 2026 cut-off located: BIA, "Indian Entities Recognized by and Eligible To Receive Services From the United States Bureau of Indian Affairs," 91 FR 4102 (30 Jan 2026), FR Doc. 2026-01899: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-01-30/html/2026-01899.htm — publishes 575 Tribal entities, updated from 89 FR 99899 (11 Dec 2024); only addition noted is Lumbee Tribe of North Carolina, recognised by National Defense Authorization Act for FY 2026 on 18 Dec 2025 (Pub. L. 119-60, §8803), subject to conditions on eligibility for services. Text search of opened Federal Register notice for "Hawaii" returned no Hawai'i entry. That negative search alone not whole proof — list organised by entity name, not state — corroborated by explicit federal statement below.
  • U.S. EPA, Pacific Southwest (Region 9), "Federally Recognized Tribes": https://www.epa.gov/tribal-pacific-sw/federally-recognized-tribes : "Hawaii has no Indian nations designated at this time." Same page lists Arizona, California, Nevada area tribes, no Hawai'i entry. EPA Region 9 covers Hawai'i, so direct federal statement for Hawai'i, not inference from national total.
  • Consequence: no government-to-government tribal cannabis programme, no tribal-state compact, no USDA-approved tribal hemp plan for Hawai'i. USDA hemp route for Hawai'i is USDA programme directly (see §10.10 + Part 7): growers hold USDA licence, not tribal-plan licence. Do not import another state's tribal-compact model; do not describe Hawaiian Home Lands as reservation or "Indian Country" — state trust under different statute (§10.3).
  • What "Native Hawaiian" is, legally — distinct from "federally recognised tribe": Native Hawaiian status in Hawai'i law turns on ancestry, not tribal enrolment. OHA framework distinguishes "native Hawaiians" (descendants of not less than one-half part of races inhabiting Hawaiian Islands prior to 1778, HRS §10-2) from larger class of "Hawaiians" (descendants of people inhabiting Hawaiian Islands in 1778): see Rice v. Cayetano, 528 U.S. 495 (2000), summarising Haw. Const. art. XII, §5 + HRS §10-2: https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/98-818 No Hawai'i statute located creates state-recognised tribe, no state tribal-recognition list for Hawai'i located — Gap 1. Ancestry alone confers no cannabis licence, exemption, or transport right in current law; cannabis/hemp licences in Parts 1 + 7 apply same terms regardless of ancestry, except proposed (not enacted) DHHL licence scheme in §10.3.
  • Native Hawaiian Organizations (NHOs) — separate federal list, not tribal recognition: U.S. Department of Interior maintains voluntary Native Hawaiian Organization Notification List to help federal agencies locate/consult interested Native Hawaiian organisations. Interior FAQ states expressly: "The placement of an organization on the Notification List shall not be construed as recognition by the Federal Government that the organization is a governmental, tribal, or other similar type of entity" + confers no substantive/procedural right not otherwise available by law: https://edit.doi.gov/hawaiian/nholist/faqs (Interior page, undated FAQ) National Park Service consultation page likewise lists "Native Hawaiian Organization List, Office of Hawaiian Relations, U.S. Department of Interior" separately from BIA Tribal Leaders Directory + official Federal Register list of federally recognised tribes: https://www.nps.gov/subjects/nagpra/consultation-resources.htm NHO status therefore consultation/notification mechanism (notably under NAGPRA, NEPA, NHPA processes), not cannabis licensing authority. No NHO cannabis/hemp programme located — Gap 2.

10.2 Native Hawaiian organisations, cultural and agricultural interests

No Native Hawaiian organisation found operating, licensing, or exempting cannabis/hemp programme in Hawai'i law as of 1 Nov 2026. Organisations below are relevant official/civic framework; none presented as cannabis regulator. - Office of Hawaiian Affairs (OHA) — state agency, not tribe, not cannabis regulator. Established by Haw. Const. art. XII, §5 (1978 constitutional amendments); board of trustees manages/administers proceeds for native Hawaiians + Hawaiians, formulates policy relating to their affairs, controls property set aside for them (art. XII, §6). Constitutional text reproduced in bill history: https://capitolwebsite.azurewebsites.net/sessions/session2018/Bills/SB2136_SD1_.HTM No OHA cannabis/hemp policy, programme, grant, or position statement located — do not attribute cannabis position to OHA. OHA trust role matters only because ceded/public-trust lands + proceeds are separate trust question from cannabis licensing; no cannabis revenue directed to OHA in current law. - Department of Hawaiian Home Lands (DHHL) + Hawaiian Homes Commission — see §10.3. - Island Burial Councils + other NHOs — secondary NAGPRA article names OHA, five Island Burial Councils, DHHL as organisations most commonly contacted for NAGPRA returns in Hawai'i: https://culturalpropertynews.org/nagpra-in-hawaii-museum-and-native-hawaiian-conflicts/ (secondary only) That is cultural-resource consultation fact, not cannabis fact; "five" count + current council composition not verified vs primary source — Gap 2. - Council for Native Hawaiian Advancement (now Hawaiian Council, per secondary reporting) — civic/economic-development organisation for Native Hawaiians. No cannabis/hemp programme located; named only to prevent confusion with OHA/DHHL, not as cannabis actor. - Cultural practice — no traditional-use exemption located. No official source ties cannabis to traditional Native Hawaiian cultural practice, or creates cultural-use exemption from HRS chs 329/329D/328G. Traditional Hawaiian plant practices sometimes discussed in general cultural sources (e.g. 'awa/kava) are different plant + different legal question, not cannabis; do not conflate. Agricultural interest documented for Native Hawaiians is economic/agricultural — homestead farming + proposed DHHL dispensary licences in §10.3 — not cultural-use exemption. - Agricultural interest in hemp, generally: hemp cultivation open to any person holding USDA licence who meets state setbacks + reporting (§10.10). No Native Hawaiian set-aside, quota, or priority in current USDA or DOH hemp rules as opened. Whether Native Hawaiian beneficiary growing hemp on homestead lease faces additional DHHL lease condition is Verify — no DHHL lease cannabis/hemp clause opened (Gap 3).

10.3 Hawaiian Home Lands — trust framework, SB 444 proposal NOT enacted

Current law: no DHHL cannabis dispensary licence, no DHHL cannabis programme, no DHHL-specific cannabis zoning rule in current law. - Trust framework: DHHL states: "The Department of Hawaiian Home Lands is governed by the Hawaiian Homes Commission Act of 1920, enacted by the U.S. Congress to protect and improve the lives of native Hawaiians. The act created a Hawaiian Homes Commission to administer certain public lands, called Hawaiian home lands, for homesteads," trust "consists of over 200,000 acres on the islands of Hawai'i, Maui, Moloka'i, Lāna'i, O'ahu, and Kaua'i": https://dhhl.hawaii.gov/dhhl/ Beneficiaries "may receive 99-year homestead leases at $1 per year for residential, agricultural, or pastoral purposes" (extendable to aggregate 199 years), DHHL also leases trust lands not in homestead use at market value + issues revocable permits, licences, rights-of-entry: same source. Eligibility, per DHHL: at least 18 + "native Hawaiian, defined as 'any descendant of not less than one-half part of the blood of the races inhabiting the Hawaiian Islands previous to 1778'" (50% blood quantum): https://dhhl.hawaii.gov/applications/applying-for-hawaiian-home-lands/ HHCA policy, §101, is to enable native Hawaiians to return to lands for self-sufficiency + self-determination + preserve Native Hawaiian values, traditions, culture, lands held in trust in perpetuity: https://codes.findlaw.com/hi/hawaiian-homes-commission-act-1920/hi-hhca-sect-101/ (FindLaw, current as of 1 Jan 2025 — verify vs HHCA text before quoting verbatim in compiled dossier). - Why county zoning does not simply answer DHHL question: Hawaiian Home Lands are trust lands administered by DHHL/Hawaiian Homes Commission, not ordinary county-zoned parcels, HHCA adopted as provision of Hawai'i Constitution subject to amendment/repeal only with consent of US for certain provisions (Admission Act compact). Trust overlay is reason legislature kept proposing DHHL-specific dispensary section — does not, by itself, authorise dispensary or hemp operation on trust land. Any production centre, dispensary, hemp farm, or processor on Hawaiian Home Lands would still need state licence/permit that applies everywhere (HRS ch 329D licence; USDA hemp licence; DOH processor permit), plus whatever DHHL disposition/lease authorisation applies — DHHL disposition term is Verify (Gap 3). - SB 444 — PROPOSED ONLY, NOT ENACTED. Do not cite as law. SB 444, 2024 (carried over from 2023), "Relating to Cannabis," capitol archive status: https://data.capitol.hawaii.gov/session/archives/measure_indiv_Archives.aspx?billtype=SB&billnumber=444&year=2024 History shown: introduced 20 Jan 2023, passed first reading, referred 25 Jan 2023 to HHS/HWN/AEN, CPN/JDC, carried over to 2024 Regular Session 11 Dec 2023 — no further action, no committee reports, no testimony, no hearings scheduled shown on archive page. LegiScan summary likewise records introduced, 25% progression, died in committee: https://legiscan.com/HI/bill/SB444/2024 (secondary tracking — capitol archive page is controlling status source). - What SB 444 would have done (proposal text, not law): Part V would have required DOH to issue eight dispensary licences to DHHL — 2 City and County of Honolulu; 3 Hawai'i County (one each east, north, west Hawai'i); 2 Maui County (one Maui, one Moloka'i); 1 Kaua'i — with DHHL-administered applications, DOH advice/assistance, native-Hawaiian (HHCA §201) individual-applicant + 51%-ownership element: SB 444 introduced text, Part V, §§13–14, via https://legiscan.com/HI/text/SB444/2024 (secondary reproduction — proposal character established by capitol status page). Same eight-licence-to-DHHL idea appeared earlier in SB 86, S.D. 1 (2021), which would have added new §329D- "Medical cannabis dispensaries on Hawaiian home lands" section on same allocation: https://legiscan.com/HI/text/SB86/2021 (secondary reproduction) SB 86 final disposition not separately verified (Gap 4), but neither bill appears as section in current HRS ch 329D as opened, neither may be presented as current law. - Current DHHL position — Unavailable, stated plainly. No DHHL cannabis/hemp policy, commission minute, or guidance statement located on dhhl.hawaii.gov or capitol testimony searched. Absence itself is finding: as of 1 Nov 2026 no located DHHL position either authorising or prohibiting cannabis/hemp on trust land beyond generally applicable state licences + trust-lease framework above. Recommended follow-up direct DHHL/Hawaiian Homes Commission enquiry + commission-minutes search (Gap 3) — not assumption silence equals permission or prohibition. - Practical consequence: Native Hawaiian beneficiary who is also registered in-state patient, caregiver, USDA hemp licensee, or DOH processor permittee is same state-law position as any other holder of that registration, plus any DHHL lease term applying to parcel (Verify). No separate "Hawaiian Home Lands dispensary" to list, no Moloka'i DHHL dispensary in operation under SB 444, no county-zoning exemption for trust land in current law. Note for compiler: Run 1 Part 4 table row stating SB 444 "would have expressly provided that production centres/dispensaries on Hawaiian Home Lands 'shall not be subject to county zoning'" is Run 1 summary of proposal's effect; SB 444 Part V text opened here framed as licence-issuance section, exact county-zoning sentence not re-verified — flag Run 1 wording as Verify vs full SB 444 text before republication (Gap 4).

10.4 Visitors — 329V out-of-state patient programme (recap, Run 1 figures, no sales share derived)

329V is temporary Hawai'i registration, not recognition of home-state card at dispensary counter. Visitor must register with DOH + receive Hawai'i card before purchasing. - DOH official page (opened directly): "Visitors from a U.S. state or territory who hold a medical cannabis card issued by their home state/territory may be eligible to apply for a temporary Hawaii medical cannabis registration valid for up to 60 days. Out-of-state patients are allowed to renew this registration once per calendar year for an additional 60 days": https://health.hawaii.gov/medicalcannabisregistry/travel/ Requirements: (1) valid state/territory ID or driver's licence from home state/territory; (2) valid medical cannabis registration card issued by same home state/territory; (3) debilitating medical condition as defined under Hawai'i law for which visitor received medical recommendation. Parent/legal-guardian caregiver required for minor patients, up to two maximum. Registration valid maximum 60 days, or until home card expires if earlier; applications may be made up to 60 days before desired start date; renewals allow up to 120 days' validity per calendar year. Only out-of-state minor patients may register caregiver; adult visitor who needs caregiver directed to apply as in-state patient instead: same DOH page. - Run 1 figures (carried forward, not re-derived): 823 currently registered + 298 awaiting activation at 31 Aug 2026 (38 states/territories + DC); 3,792 registered Jan–Aug 2026 (+14.7% vs 2025). In-state county-table "out-of-state address" row (99 at Aug 2026) is NOT 329V count, must never be added to it. Visitor sales dollars: Unavailable — no DOH source breaks dispensary sales into in-state vs 329V purchases, no sales share may be derived from 823 ÷ 29,315. - Terms (Run 1 anchors, with fee conflict flagged above): $47.41 [audit-corrected application fee] per 60-day term in Run 1 (secondary-confirmed) vs $47.41 non-refundable application fee on DOH travel page opened 1 Nov 2026 — see conflict box above. Maximum two 60-day terms per calendar year; no cultivation by 329V patient or caregiver; purchase limits same as in-state: 4 ounces in 15 days, 8 ounces in 30 days, counted across all dispensaries statewide (HRS §329D-13, Run 1 anchor). Adequate supply for out-of-state patient no more than 4 ounces usable cannabis at any given time + no live plants (Run 1 anchor, HRS §329-121). - International visitors — no 329V route: DOH travel page: "While an international visitor cannot apply as an out-of-state patient, we welcome visitors from abroad to apply as in-state patients. To do so, you will need to see a Hawaii medical provider who will certify your medical condition": same DOH page. Foreign medical card, foreign prescription, or foreign government cannabis authorisation therefore NOT Hawai'i dispensary credential. Whether international visitor who obtains in-state registration faces additional immigration or federal consequence for prior foreign cannabis use/admission outside sources opened — Gap 5, do not advise on immigration consequences in compiled dossier without federal immigration source. - What 329V does NOT do: does not authorise visitor to bring cannabis into Hawai'i, carry between islands, or take home (§§10.5–10.7). 329V card is Hawai'i purchase/possession credential for product bought in Hawai'i, on island where bought, subject to same public-place, workplace, school-grounds, park/beach, moving-vehicle prohibitions as in-state patients (HRS §329-122(e), Run 1 anchor; DOH restrictions page, §10.7).

10.5 Bringing cannabis into/out of Hawai'i by air — federal jurisdiction at checkpoint, DOH own warning

Short answer: Hawai'i law gives no right to fly cannabis in or out, DOH itself warns visitors not to try. - DOH travel page (official, opened directly): in answer to travelling with cannabis, DOH states: "We do not recommend traveling with cannabis as you will be passing through federal jurisdiction which may put you at jeopardy of delay or arrest." https://health.hawaii.gov/medicalcannabisregistry/travel/ - TSA (official, opened directly — read text, not just label): TSA "Medical Marijuana" page https://www.tsa.gov/travel/security-screening/whatcanibring/items/medical-marijuana (page crawled 18 July 2026) Header reads "Carry On Bags: Yes (Special Instructions)" / "Checked Bags: Yes (Special Instructions)," but operative text states: "Marijuana and certain cannabis infused products, including some Cannabidiol (CBD) oil, remain illegal under federal law except for products that contain no more than 0.3 percent THC on a dry weight basis or that are approved by FDA. (See the Agriculture Improvement Act of 2018, Pub. L. 115-334.) TSA officers are required to report any suspected violations of law to local, state or federal authorities." Adds: "TSA security officers do not search for marijuana or other illegal drugs, but if any illegal substance is discovered during security screening, TSA will refer the matter to a law enforcement officer," + "The final decision rests with the TSA officer on whether an item is allowed through the checkpoint." - Flag, do not resolve: "Yes (Special Instructions)" label sits uneasily with "remain illegal except ≤0.3% or FDA-approved" text, page as crawled July 2026 contains no Schedule III update for state-licensed medical marijuana. Secondary 2026 guide claiming TSA updated page Apr 2026 to permit state-licensed medical marijuana found in search, not adopted — contradicts TSA text itself as opened, no TSA Schedule III guidance located. For compiled dossier, describe TSA practice exactly as above (not searched for; referred if discovered; officer's final decision; ≤0.3% / FDA-approved exception as written) + flag Schedule III / TSA interaction as Verify (Gap 6). In particular, do not tell patient 329/329V card, or given 28 Apr 2026 Schedule III move, guarantees passage through Hawai'i airport checkpoint. - What ≤0.3% exception does/does not cover: on TSA text as written, hemp product at/below 0.3% THC dry-weight basis, or FDA-approved product, falls inside stated exception; dispensary medical product above threshold does not, on that text. After 11 Dec 2026 federal hemp test itself changes (total THC + 0.4 mg per container cap, given baseline) — whether TSA updates 0.3% dry-weight wording to match is Verify after that date (Gap 6). Hemp product lawful in Hawai'i under HAR ch 11-37 (e.g. edible at 5 mg per package, beverage at 0.5 mg per container) will on face exceed 0.4 mg per container federal cap after 11 Dec 2026 even if at/below 0.3% concentration — see §10.10. Do not advise travellers Hawai'i hemp product automatically flyable. - Airports as federal jurisdiction + agricultural-inspection overlay: airports + aircraft in federal airspace are federal jurisdiction for drug purposes, which is why Hawai'i law itself states protections "do not apply outside of the jurisdictional limits of the State" (HRS §329-122(f), §10.7). Separately, passengers departing Hawai'i by air for another state subject to USDA agricultural inspection of baggage/personal effects (7 CFR §318.13-10, plant-quarantine inspection at checkpoint/gate or check-in inspection station, with seizure of articles in violation of that part) — plant-quarantine rule, not cannabis rule, cited only to explain second federal inspection point. Full §318.13-10 text located via secondary reproduction (CaseMine) in search, not opened from eCFR — Verify before quoting verbatim (Gap 7). - No Hawai'i "amnesty box" or airport safe-harbour rule located: Secondary articles describe cannabis amnesty boxes at some mainland airports; no Hawai'i airport amnesty-box programme located, none claimed for Daniel K. Inouye International Airport (Honolulu, O'ahu), Kahului Airport (Maui), Ellison Onizuka Kona International Airport (Hawai'i Island), or Līhu'e Airport (Kaua'i) — Gap 7.

10.6 Bringing cannabis into/out of Hawai'i by sea — federal waters, flag state, port law

No Hawai'i state-law sea route for cannabis that avoids federal problem, no regular interisland passenger-ferry exception. - Federal waters + territorial sea: within 12 nautical miles of coast coastal nation exercises sovereignty, incl. water column + airspace above it (UNCLOS territorial-sea framework, as summarised in secondary maritime sources). Beyond that, on high seas, vessel generally subject to flag state's jurisdiction, but U.S. federal criminal jurisdiction under 18 U.S.C. §7 ("special maritime and territorial jurisdiction") reaches, among other cases, foreign vessel on voyage with scheduled departure from or arrival in US where offence by/against US national. Secondary summaries of §7: https://legalclarity.org/can-you-get-arrested-on-a-cruise-ship/ + https://legalclarity.org/what-are-international-waters-and-what-laws-apply/ (both secondary only — §7 text itself not opened from U.S. Code, Gap 8). Practical point does not depend on fine §7 subsections: private vessel, cargo vessel, or cruise ship leaving/arriving in Hawai'i does not leave federal drug law behind by crossing island channel or 12-mile line, Hawai'i 329/329V card not defence at sea. - Interisland sea transport by patients barred by same Hawai'i rule as air transport (§10.7: "by any means"). No Hawai'i interisland passenger ferry network comparable to mainland ferry system that could be mistaken for intra-state road journey; limited Maui–Lāna'i passenger service + cargo carriers (e.g. Young Brothers) do not create cannabis-transport exception, no carrier cannabis policy located that would authorise it. Patient who needs cannabis on second island must buy it on that island (if dispensary exists there) or grow on-island — same rule as air travel. - Cargo + mail: bringing cannabis into Hawai'i by cargo, parcel, or mail is interstate/international movement of controlled substance under federal law, not Hawai'i retail delivery. Dispensaries separately barred from off-premises delivery (HRS §329D-6(n)), so Hawai'i dispensary cannot lawfully "ship" medical cannabis to patient on another island or mainland. No Hawai'i law authorising cannabis shipment by sea, air cargo, or mail located. Hemp shipments different, addressed in §10.10 — do not merge two. - Ports + harbours: U.S. ports of entry, harbours, Coast Guard facilities are federal jurisdiction. Coast Guard general maritime law-enforcement role at sea not separately documented with Hawai'i-specific cannabis boarding policy (Gap 8); no statement made about Coast Guard boarding practice beyond general federal-law position above.

10.7 Interisland transport — Hawai'i rule, verbatim, two narrow dispensary/lab routes

Patients + caregivers: prohibited interisland, by any means, for any purpose — with one patient-facing lab-testing transport provision whose interisland scope itself flagged below. - Current statute text (reproduction, to be re-verified vs capitol current §329-122 before publication-grade quotation): HRS §329-122(f), via LawServer reproduction https://www.lawserver.com/law/state/hawaii/hi-statutes/hawaii_statutes_329-122 (secondary reproduction): "For purposes of interisland transportation, 'transport' of cannabis, usable cannabis, or any manufactured cannabis product, by any means is allowable only between dispensaries to the extent authorized by section 329D-6(r) and between a production center or retail dispensing location and a certified laboratory for the sole purpose of laboratory testing pursuant to section 329D-8, as permitted under section 329D-6(m) and subject to section 329D-6(j), and with the understanding that state law and its protections do not apply outside of the jurisdictional limits of the State. Allowable transport pursuant to this section does not include interisland transportation by any means or for any purpose between a qualifying patient, primary caregiver, qualifying out-of-state patient, or caregiver of a qualifying out-of-state patient and any other entity or individual, including an individual who is a qualifying patient, primary caregiver, qualifying out-of-state patient, or caregiver of a qualifying out-of-state patient." Matches Run 1 / task anchor exactly. No conflict found between task anchor + reproduction. - DOH plain-language confirmation (official, opened directly): DOH Medical Cannabis Use & Restrictions page https://health.hawaii.gov/medicalcannabisregistry/patients/restrictions/ : "'…transport does not include the inter-island transportation of cannabis, usable cannabis, or any manufactured cannabis product'. This means that registered patients and caregivers CANNOT transport cannabis inter-island." Same page notes Act 241 SLH 2025 amended §329-122 so cannabis in public place "shall be transported in a sealed container, not be visible to the public, and shall not be removed from its sealed container or consumed or used in any way while it is in the public place" — sealed-container rule governs lawful intra-island transport in public places; does not authorise interisland transport. - 1-gram lab-testing provision — scope flagged, not assumed: HRS §329-122(f)(5) (as reproduced in bill texts + Part 11 working material) allows qualifying patient, primary caregiver, qualifying out-of-state patient, or caregiver of qualifying out-of-state patient to transport up to one gram of cannabis per test to certified laboratory for laboratory testing, only if person secures appointment, obtains confirmation (may be electronic) incl. specific time/date + detailed description of product + amount, has confirmation available during transport. Whether 1-gram route may itself cross islands — i.e. whether patient on island without certified laboratory may fly/ferry 1 gram to laboratory on another island — not clearly answered by extracts opened, DOH restrictions page states interisland prohibition flatly without 1-gram interisland exception. Do not advise patients 1-gram lab route authorises interisland carriage until §329-122(f)(5) + DOH laboratory-transport guidance checked together (Gap 9). Safe statement for compiled dossier: general patient interisland transport prohibited; 1-gram lab-testing transport exists in §329-122(f)(5) with appointment/confirmation conditions, its interisland application is Verify. - Dispensaries: prohibited inter-county/interisland except as authorised by §329D-6(r) + for lab testing: HRS §329D-6(m) (Justia 2025 reproduction, opened directly) https://law.justia.com/codes/hawaii/title-19/chapter-329d/section-329d-6/ : "Except as authorized by subsection (r), a dispensary shall not transport cannabis or manufactured cannabis products to another county or another island; provided that this subsection shall not apply to the transportation of cannabis or any manufactured cannabis product solely for the purposes of laboratory testing pursuant to section 329D-8, and subject to subsection (j), if no certified laboratory is located in the county or on the island where the dispensary is located; provided further that a dispensary shall only transport samples … in an amount and manner prescribed by the department, in rules adopted pursuant to this chapter, and with the understanding that state law and its protections do not apply outside of the jurisdictional limits of the State." HRS §329D-6(r) (same Justia page): department "may authorize a dispensary to purchase cannabis and manufactured cannabis products from another dispensary" if purchasing dispensary establishes continuous-access need or medical/scientific/other legitimate purpose approved by State, with selling-dispensary limit no more than 800 ounces (or other amounts with prior department approval) to purchasing dispensary within 30-day period, transported for approved purpose. - Geographic scope — still Verify (Run 1 gap, not closed). Subsection (r) as opened does not contain words "same island." Earlier bill, HB 477, H.D. 2 (2022), would have expressly limited dispensary-to-dispensary sales to purchasing dispensary "located on the same island as the selling dispensary" + clarified that, while federal law prohibits transport over water, such sales may only occur between same-island dispensaries: https://capitolwebsite.azurewebsites.net/sessions/session2022/bills/HB477_HD2_.HTM (bill text — proposal, not law). Because (m) frames (r) as exception to inter-county/interisland ban, reader could infer (r) can authorise cross-island wholesale; because (p) bans transport to/from/within federal property + federal law governs water/air between islands, reader could equally infer it cannot in practice. Compiled dossier must not state §329D-6(r) authorises interisland wholesale transfers as settled fact — record as Verify vs full §329D-6(r) rulemaking/HAR §11-850-45 history + any DOH dispensary-to-dispensary guidance (Gap 9). Task anchor wording — "dispensaries only for lab testing and dispensary-to-dispensary under §329D-6(r)" — preserved as anchor; geographic scope is open question. - Federal-property ban (dispensaries): HRS §329D-6(p) (same Justia page): "No cannabis or manufactured cannabis products shall be transported to, from, or within any federal fort or arsenal, national park or forest, any other federal enclave, or any other property possessed or occupied by the federal government." Applies to dispensary transport; patient/caregiver position on federal property addressed in §10.8. - Proposed interisland patient transport — NOT law: SB 465, S.D. 1 (2024), would have amended §329-122(f) to allow interisland transport "only by a qualifying patient or qualifying out-of-state patient for the patient's personal medical use," deleting patient-to-patient prohibition, with proviso no person be required to violate federal law, defective effective date 31 Dec 2050 in S.D. 1 text: https://data.capitol.hawaii.gov/sessions/session2024/bills/SB465_SD1_.HTM LegiScan records SB 465 (2024) as engrossed 7 Mar 2023, carried over to 2024, pending House Consumer Protection & Commerce Committee, died in committee: https://legiscan.com/HI/bill/SB465/2024 (secondary tracking) SB 465 therefore proposed only, must not be presented as current §329-122(f). Same applies to older HB 2740 (2018) interisland draft noted in Run 1. - Islands without dispensary: practical consequence, stated in Run 1 + unchanged, patient on Moloka'i, Lāna'i, or Ni'ihau — islands with no licensed retail dispensary (licensing outcome, not ban) — cannot lawfully be supplied by interisland carriage from another island's dispensary; lawful in-state routes are home cultivation on-island by patient or registered caregiver (in-state patients/caregivers only; 329V patients may not cultivate), or travel to island with dispensary + purchase there. No "interisland delivery" service by dispensary lawful: off-premises delivery barred by §329D-6(n) (same Justia page).

10.8 Military bases + other federal land — O'ahu concentration, federal-possession consequence stated plainly

Plain consequence first: Hawai'i 329 card, 329V card, given Schedule III move for state-licensed medical marijuana do not authorise possession, use, or transport of cannabis on military installation or other federal property, do not protect service member from UCMJ action. No post-28 Apr 2026 DoD or Hawai'i-installation cannabis guidance located that changes position — Gap 10. - Major installations (list, not official DoD master list — counts vary by whether support facilities counted): U.S. Army Garrison Hawai'i "Move With Aloha" guide lists Hawai'i military installations incl., on O'ahu: Hickam Air Force Base (Honolulu), Bellows Air Force Station (Waimānalo), Fort Shafter (Honolulu), Schofield Barracks (Wahiawa), Tripler Army Medical Center (Honolulu), Fort DeRussy (Honolulu), Wheeler Army Airfield (Wahiawa), Marine Corps Base Hawai'i (Kāne'ohe, O'ahu), NCTAMS PAC (Wahiawa), Naval Station Pearl Harbor (Pearl Harbor), Coast Guard bases at Honolulu + Barbers Point; on Kaua'i: Barking Sands Missile Range (Kekaha, i.e. Pacific Missile Range Facility); on Hawai'i Island: Pōhakuloa Training Center (central Hawai'i); on Maui: Coast Guard Station Maui (Wailuku): https://home.army.mil/hawaii/1416/5128/0044/MoveWithAloha.APR2022.pdf (U.S. Army publication, Apr 2022 — installation names/locations, not cannabis policy) U.S. House statement on Hawai'i military infrastructure likewise names Joint Base Pearl Harbor-Hickam, Marine Corps Base Hawai'i, Schofield Barracks, Fort Shafter, Pacific Missile Range Facility, Pōhakuloa Training Area, notes Hawai'i hosts U.S. Indo-Pacific Command, U.S. Pacific Fleet, Pacific Air Forces, U.S. Army Pacific, Marine Corps Forces Pacific, Special Operations Command Pacific, Space Force Indo-Pacific, with approximately 60,000 DoD personnel plus families: https://case.house.gov/news/documentsingle.aspx?DocumentID=4882 GSA-attachment installation list adds, for O'ahu: Camp H. M. Smith, Pu'uloa Range Training Facility, Naval Magazine Lualualei; for Hawai'i Island: Bradshaw Army Airfield + Kīlauea Military Camp (joint-services): https://govtribe.com/file/government-file/attachment-7-list-of-possible-locations-dot-pdf (secondary reproduction of federal attachment — installation list only, Verify vs current DoD installation directory before republication as master list, Gap 10). - O'ahu in particular: Joint Base Pearl Harbor-Hickam (merged Naval Station Pearl Harbor + Hickam Air Force Base), Schofield Barracks, Wheeler Army Airfield, Fort Shafter, Marine Corps Base Hawai'i at Kāne'ohe Bay (Mokapu Peninsula), Camp H. M. Smith, Tripler Army Medical Center, Bellows Air Force Station, Coast Guard facilities at Honolulu/Barbers Point together cover large share of O'ahu central + windward land + shoreline. No official % of O'ahu or State under federal ownership located — secondary dispensary blog claim "over 20% of Hawaii is owned by the federal government" found in search, not adopted as official figure (Gap 10). Do not publish federal-land % for Hawai'i or O'ahu without federal land-inventory source. - Federal-possession consequence — service members: Under Uniform Code of Military Justice, Article 112a prohibits wrongful use, possession, manufacture, distribution, introduction of controlled substances, incl. marijuana, on/off duty + on/off installation. Official Joint Base Elmendorf-Richardson (Alaska) article — cited for general federal-land principle, not Hawai'i installation order — states: "all service members are subject to article 112a," + "Dependents, civilians, contracted employees or anybody who possesses said substances on base are subject to the federal crime of possession on a military installation [21 U.S. Code 844]," with first-offence fine up to $1,000 or one year jail or both, progressively larger for multiple offences: https://www.jber.jb.mil/News/Articles/Display/Article/1041039/use-of-marijuana-on-federal-land-still-prohibited/ (official .mil source, Alaska installation) DoD "No Room for THC" page likewise states service members prohibited from using any form of marijuana, CBD, or hemp products, incl. for medical purposes + regardless of claimed/actual THC concentration, with violations punishable under UCMJ Article 92(1) + related administrative/criminal action, subject only to narrow exceptions (authorised medical-duty personnel; honest/reasonable lack of knowledge; durable hemp goods such as rope/clothing; FDA-approved drugs with valid DoD-approved prescription): https://toomuchtolose.org/what-can-i-do/no-room-for-thc/ (DoD-affiliated prevention page — secondary for compiled dossier purposes, pre-dating given Schedule III baseline in its Schedule I framing; post-Schedule III DoD position is Gap 10). Do not state Schedule III lifted service-member ban — no such DoD update located. - Federal-possession consequence — civilians, dependents, visitors, federal employees: civilian, dependent, contractor, or visitor found with cannabis on base subject to federal possession law, not Hawai'i medical law, may face confiscation, federal citation/prosecution, loss of base access, and, for service member sponsor, disciplinary consequences for sponsor. Medical card "offers no protection on a military base or from UCMJ violations" in secondary military-law summaries found in search; official JBER statement above is citable federal-land principle. Entry searches at installation gates are further practical point: visitors may be subject to vehicle/person searches on entry — stated as general installation practice from DoD prevention page above, not Hawai'i-installation-specific rule (Gap 10). - Other federal land in Hawai'i (not only bases): Hawai'i Volcanoes National Park (Hawai'i Island) + Haleakalā National Park (Maui) are National Park Service federal land; national wildlife refuges, national forests/forest reserves under federal management, U.S. Fish and Wildlife areas, federal courthouses, post offices, other federally possessed/occupied property likewise federal jurisdiction for drug purposes. Dispensary-transport ban in §329D-6(p) (quoted in §10.7) expressly includes "national park or forest, any other federal enclave, or any other property possessed or occupied by the federal government." State parks, county parks, beaches are NOT federal land, but medical use there separately barred by Hawai'i law as public place (§329-122(e)) — do not confuse two bars. Secondary dispensary blog claim segment of Saddle Road (Hawai'i Island) crosses about 1,000 feet of federal property by Pōhakuloa found in search, not adopted without land-status map (Gap 10); general point — road segment crossing federal land does not create state-law safe corridor — follows from §329D-6(p) + federal jurisdiction, not anecdote. - Kalawao note: Kalawao County (Kalaupapa, Moloka'i) state-administered + no dispensary licence allocated (0 Kalawao, Run 1 anchor). Not tribal land, not military installation; no cannabis-specific Kalawao rule located. Do not describe Kalawao as federal enclave for cannabis purposes without land-status source (Gap 10).

10.9 Cruise ships — contract bans broader than federal drug law, secondary + dated, never Hawai'i law

Cruise-line cannabis bans are private contract/policy, not Hawai'i law. Reported as secondary, dated summaries of line policies, compiled dossier must check each line's current prohibited-items list before republication. Outcome uniform across major lines: no medical-marijuana exception, several lines no hemp/CBD exception either. - Royal Caribbean (secondary summary of line's Guest Health, Safety, and Conduct Policy / ticket contract): "Marijuana, even in its medicinal form or for medicinal purposes, shall be prohibited," ticket-contract formulation adds marijuana possession/use, incl. medical marijuana, strictly prohibited on vessel at all times, in terminals, during shore excursions, on land tours, regardless of local/state/other laws permitting use/possession: summarised at https://legalclarity.org/can-i-bring-marijuana-on-a-cruise-ship/ + https://highseascruising.com/can-you-bring-weed-on-a-cruise-ship/ (both secondary summaries — line's current policy page itself not opened, Gap 11). - Carnival (secondary summary): ships are "drug free zones"; U.S. cruise terminals described as federal facilities; guests found with illegal drugs face fines, arrest, denial of boarding without refund/compensation; CBD + medical marijuana expressly included in prohibited-items language in summaries: same LegalClarity/High Seas Cruising sources above, plus https://legalclarity.org/can-you-bring-medical-marijuana-on-a-cruise/ (secondary) Widely reported 2023 Carnival case (Texas passenger denied boarding at Miami with CBD gummies, subsequently barred from future Carnival cruises) reported in secondary cruise coverage at https://gowayora.com/11-harmless-cruise-mistakes-that-got/ (secondary, 2023 incident) — illustrates enforcement, not rule, must not be presented as Hawai'i legal outcome. - Norwegian Cruise Line (secondary summary): prohibited items include "Marijuana prescribed for medical purposes" + "all products containing CBD, oils, candies, and gummies or any product containing THC," guest conduct policy extends ban to transfers, terminals, shore excursions, private islands: LegalClarity cruise pages above (secondary). Disney Cruise Line (secondary summary): bars "medically prescribed marijuana, as well as items derived from or enriched by marijuana, including items and products containing THC and/or CBD," incl. on Castaway Cay: same sources (secondary). Virgin Voyages (secondary summary): lists "medically prescribed marijuana or synthetic marijuana and CBD products" as prohibited: same sources (secondary). MSC, Holland America, Celebrity, Princess, Cunard described in secondary summaries as prohibiting marijuana incl. medical marijuana; no line-specific quotation adopted without current policy open (Gap 11). - Why ban survives state card + survives Schedule III as contract matter: (1) passenger contract is private agreement — line may ban lawful-at-home product as condition of carriage, breach can mean confiscation, removal, denial of boarding/reboarding, future-sailing bans without criminal conviction; (2) ship's flag state (most major cruise ships foreign-flagged — Bahamas for Royal Caribbean, Norwegian, Disney + much of Carnival fleet, Panama for part of Carnival fleet, Netherlands for Holland America, per secondary flag-state summary at https://legalclarity.org/can-i-bring-marijuana-on-a-cruise-ship/, secondary) governs on high seas, those flag states treat cannabis as illegal; (3) within 12 nautical miles + in port, coastal/port state's law applies, on U.S.-port voyage 18 U.S.C. §7 special maritime jurisdiction can reach offences by/against U.S. nationals (secondary §7 summaries in §10.6). Hawai'i 329/329V card therefore helps cruise passenger in no layer: not under contract, not under flag-state law, not under port-state/federal law as general matter. - Hawai'i-specific cruise points: (1) cruise that embarks, calls, or disembarks in Hawai'i (Honolulu, Kahului, Hilo, Kailua-Kona, Nāwiliwili/Līhu'e) does not become Hawai'i medical-cannabis journey because it touches Hawai'i — ship's policy + applicable maritime/port law govern on board, Hawai'i dispensary purchase on shore does not authorise bringing product back on board; (2) shore excursion in Hawai'i does not authorise public-place medical use in way that overrides line's ban or Hawai'i public-place bar (§329-122(e)); (3) no Hawai'i cruise-specific cannabis statute, port rule, or DOH cruise guidance located — Gap 11. Do not state Hawai'i port cannabis fine, confiscation rule, or embarkation procedure without port/line source. - Hemp/CBD on cruises — broader than federal hemp line: several lines ban CBD/hemp products outright even where product would be federally lawful hemp on land (see Norwegian/Disney/Virgin summaries above). Traveller must therefore not assume Hawai'i-legal manufactured hemp product (HAR ch 11-37) or federally lawful hemp product may be taken on board. Whether specific line's current list still bans all CBD, or only THC-containing CBD, is Verify line by line (Gap 11) — secondary summaries conflict in granularity + dated.

10.10 Hemp shipments — one shipment category is federally protected in principle, with Hawai'i reporting + destination-law limits

Hemp is exception to "do not ship cannabis" rule — but only for hemp that actually meets applicable federal definition, only subject to Hawai'i own product rules on sale in Hawai'i, only subject to destination jurisdiction's rules on sale there. - Federal interstate-commerce protection (2018 Farm Bill, §10114): Agriculture Improvement Act of 2018, §10114, provides in substance: nothing in that title prohibits interstate commerce of hemp or hemp products, no State or Indian Tribe shall prohibit transportation or shipment of hemp or hemp products produced in accordance with subtitle G of Agricultural Marketing Act of 1946 through State or territory of Indian Tribe, as applicable. Secondary analyses of USDA hemp rule + USDA Office of General Counsel opinion (28 May 2019) confirm USDA reading states/tribes may not prevent movement of lawfully produced hemp through territory even if they prohibit production: https://cannabusiness.law/usda-hemp-regulations-analysis-interstate-transport-of-hemp/ + https://cultivalaw.com/affirm-legality-interstate-transportation-hemp/ (both secondary legal analyses — §10114 statutory text itself not opened from U.S. Code, Gap 12). Two limits, stated plainly: (a) protects transportation/shipment through jurisdiction + interstate commerce in hemp/hemp products — does not create right to sell non-compliant product in destination state, which may impose own sale, testing, labelling, registration rules; (b) protects only hemp produced in accordance with federal programme (USDA licence or approved state/tribal plan) + meeting federal hemp definition in force at time of shipment — which is exactly what changes 11 Dec 2026 (below). - Hawai'i cultivation-to-shipment reporting (official, opened directly): DAB Hemp Production page https://dab.hawaii.gov/hemp/ Hawai'i growers must hold USDA licence (USDA Domestic Hemp Production Program, HeMP platform, rolling applications, FBI criminal-history report, 10-year controlled-substance felony bar, 3-year licence term) + comply with Hawai'i setbacks (no growing within 300 feet of pre-existing playground/childcare/school; within 100 feet of pre-existing house/dwelling/residential structure not owned/controlled by licence holder; not in house/dwelling/residential structure). For transportation, DAB requires form HP-1, Transportation Report, for any planned transportation — incl. importation, inter-island, intra-island, exportation — of: (1) live hemp plants, propagative plant parts, viable seeds; (2) harvested hemp floral material in raw botanical form (incl. dried); (3) harvested hemp leaf material in raw botanical form (incl. dried). Report must be submitted at least three business days prior to transportation (by DAB email/attachment route or by mail ensuring arrival three business days prior). No report required for: hemp products/processed hemp; mature stalk material; retted fiber; non-viable seed. DAB also notes transportation must abide by all applicable laws, incl. state requirements on importation, inter-island transportation, or exportation of live seed, live plants, cut flowers (Plant Quarantine Branch), refers hemp processing/products questions to DOH OMCCR. Note distinction: HP-1 regime governs raw botanical hemp material, not finished manufactured hemp products — processor shipping finished MHP does not file HP-1 for that shipment on DAB page as written, but remains subject to DOH testing/labelling/registration rules + destination law. - Hawai'i shipment volume (dated briefing figure, not Part 7 count): DAB Fiscal Biennium 2025–2027 supplemental budget briefing (capitol testimony, Jan 2026) states hemp cultivation operates under USDA programme as mandated by Act 14 SLH 2020; as of 30 June 2025, 44 USDA-licensed hemp producers active across islands (acreage withheld as confidential USDA data due to small number of growers); DAB enforces buffer zones + oversees hemp transportation, with 14 monitored movements reported in 2025, mostly within islands: https://data.capitol.hawaii.gov/sessions/session2026/Testimony/Info_Testimony_WAM_01-09-26_AGR.pdf (official briefing document) Flag: 44 figure (30 June 2025, DAB briefing) sits alongside Part 7 dated figures (58 active in 2024, Task Force; secondary 51 July 2025) — different dates, sources, possibly universes; do not merge into single "current grower count", do not use 14-movement figure as shipment volume (counts monitored HP-1 movements, not finished-product shipments, which require no HP-1 report). - Hemp products shipped into Hawai'i for sale in Hawai'i: out-of-state seller selling manufactured hemp products to Hawai'i consumers — incl. online — must hold DOH Certificate of Registration under HRS §328G-9 from 1 Jan 2026 ($50, five years; enforcement from 1 July 2026; processors auto-registered under §328G-9(l)) + product must meet Hawai'i HAR ch 11-37 product rules on sale in Hawai'i (total THC ≤0.3%; edible 1 mg/serving, 5 mg/package; beverage 0.5 mg/container; tincture 3 mg/serving, 1-ounce container; smokable hemp banned; artificially derived/synthetic cannabinoids banned) — Run 1 / Part 7 anchors, DOH Hemp Program page https://health.hawaii.gov/hempprogram/ Federal interstate-commerce protection does not override Hawai'i sale rules for products sold in Hawai'i; protects through-shipment + commerce in compliant hemp, line between two for Hawai'i-destined online sale is destination-sale question, not through-shipment question — flag any "federal preemption lets us sell anything in Hawai'i" claim as incorrect on sources opened (Gap 12). - Hemp biomass shipped within Hawai'i to processor: USDA-licensed producer may transport hemp within State to facility authorised by law to process hemp or to another licensed producer's grow area, provided transportation reported to DAB (HRS §141-42(b), Part 7 anchor). DAB may require movement reports incl. USDA test results, may deny authorisation if hemp does not comply. This is intra-state hemp analogue of dispensary transport rules — unlike medical cannabis, hemp biomass movement between islands not barred by §329-122(f), which governs cannabis/medical products, not USDA-licensed hemp reported under HP-1. Do not apply medical interisland ban to hemp, do not apply hemp HP-1 regime to medical cannabis. - HAR §11-37-6 — export-manufacturing allowance, quoted, federal 0.4 mg tension (flagged, not resolved by assertion): HAR §11-37-6, "Manufactured hemp products for sale outside the State" (HAR ch 11-37, Amendment and Compilation dated 30 Dec 2025, compiled 28 Jan 2026): https://health.hawaii.gov/hempprogram/files/2026/02/Standard11-37HAR-20260128.pdf (§11-37-6 text as reproduced in Dec 2024 compilation search extract, consistent with Jan 2026 compilation TOC — Jan 2026 §11-37-6 full text not separately re-read line by line, Gap 12):

"(a) A hemp processor may manufacture and hold manufactured hemp products exceeding the total tetrahydrocannabinol content limits per serving or per package or container specified in section 11-37-5(a) and (b), with a total tetrahydrocannabinol concentration of no more than 0.3 per cent, for sale outside the State. Manufactured hemp products for sale outside the State shall comply with all other applicable requirements in this chapter, except as otherwise specified in this section, and all applicable requirements of the state or jurisdiction in which they are to be sold." "(b) Testing. Manufactured hemp products for sale outside the state shall: (1) Meet the testing requirements in section 11-37-22 and 11-37-23; or (2) If they are to be sold in a state or jurisdiction that has its own manufactured hemp product testing requirements, meet that state or jurisdiction's testing requirements prior to shipment for sale." Labelling under (c) follows §11-37-30 or destination jurisdiction's labelling requirements, with additional export-labelling statements (remainder of (c) truncated in extract — Verify, Gap 12). - What §11-37-6 does: lets Hawai'i DOH-permitted processor make + hold product that exceeds Hawai'i in-state per-serving/package/container mg caps, provided total THC concentration stays at/below 0.3%, for sale outside State — export-manufacturing allowance, not Hawai'i retail allowance, not marijuana-export allowance. Product above 0.3% total THC not hemp under this rule at all. - Tension after 11 Dec 2026, stated as tension: federal baseline after 11 Dec 2026 replaces concentration-only hemp test with full total-THC definition plus 0.4 mg total THC per container cap for finished products. Hawai'i export product lawful under §11-37-6 as written — at/below 0.3% concentration, but in container holding >0.4 mg total THC (true of almost any ordinary edible, beverage, or tincture container at 0.3% concentration, already true of Hawai'i in-state caps: 5 mg per edible package, 0.5 mg per beverage container, DOH tincture guidance up to 85 mg per 1-ounce bottle) — would therefore satisfy Hawai'i concentration test while failing federal per-container test, if federal cap applies to that product category as written. Whether federal cap applies to all export categories, whether any federal enforcement discretion, transition, or product-category carve-out applies, whether Hawai'i amends §11-37-6 or issues guidance, all unknown as of 1 Nov 2026 — no Hawai'i statute, HAR amendment, DOH guidance, or AG opinion responding to PL 119-37 §781 / PL 119-103 §2019 located in Run 1 or this turn. Do not resolve tension by asserting §11-37-6 products remain federally lawful after 11 Dec 2026, do not assert they become unlawful in every destination — destination law is second, independent condition in §11-37-6(a) itself. Required follow-up post-11 Dec 2026 re-check of both federal hemp definition as applied + any Hawai'i DOH/DAB response (Gap 12). Same tension applies, a fortiori, to hemp products shipped into Hawai'i that meet Hawai'i mg caps but exceed 0.4 mg per container. - Related federal timing points (given baseline, not re-researched): USDA enforcement of DEA-registered lab testing for hemp delayed to 31 Dec 2026 — so Hawai'i hemp shipment's lab-testing route (DEA-registered lab vs other qualified lab) around Nov/Dec 2026 transition itself Verify item for any shipment straddling those dates (Gap 12). Federal medical-marijuana appropriations rider runs through 11 Dec 2026; does not authorise interstate medical-cannabis shipment, does not convert medical product into hemp.

10.11 International travel — no Hawai'i route, foreign law governs abroad

  • Out of Hawai'i / into US: U.S. Customs and Border Protection states, in 12 Mar 2026 traveller release (Buffalo, land-border context, general statement): "And remember, marijuana remains illegal in the United States federally. Do not cross the border with any marijuana or marijuana products." https://www.cbp.gov/newsroom/local-media-release/buffalo-cbp-reminds-travelers-tips-improve-their-cross-border (official CBP release) Secondary summaries of CBP Canada position quote CBP to same effect — importation of marijuana or cannabis products, even for medical use or otherwise authorised under local law, remains illegal under federal law, with seizure, fines, arrest, possible admissibility consequences for non-citizens — but CBP Canada-specific statement page itself not opened (Gap 5). Compiled dossier should state international rule at level CBP release supports (do not cross border with marijuana/marijuana products; federal law governs at border + supersedes state law) + flag detailed CBP import/export penalty + immigration-consequence statements as Verify vs CBP statement page.
  • Into foreign country: foreign law governs, frequently stricter than US federal law. No destination-country cannabis table provided — traveller to Canada, Japan, Australia, Singapore, UAE, or any other destination must check destination's drug law, must not assume US state card, Hawai'i card, hemp label, or CBD label recognised abroad. In particular, several Asian + Middle Eastern jurisdictions impose severe penalties for cannabis possession that have no parallel in Hawai'i $130 violation for ≤3 grams by non-patient — contrast stated as general travel warning from secondary travel sources, not destination-law finding for any named country (Gap 5). Product lawful hemp in US may be treated as controlled substance abroad if destination counts total THC, bans CBD, or bans all cannabis derivatives.
  • International visitors in Hawai'i (recap from §10.4): no 329V route; in-state registration via Hawai'i provider only DOH route opened. International visitor who obtains in-state registration then same intra-island position as any in-state patient (buy on island, no interisland carriage, no export home). Taking Hawai'i-bought cannabis home abroad is export/import offence on both sides of border on sources above — do not present "buy it in Hawai'i and take it home" as option for any visitor, domestic or international.

10.12 Practical travel summary (research summary only — not legal advice)

Journey Cannabis (medical, incl. 329/329V) Hemp / manufactured hemp product
Mainland ↔ Hawai'i by air No Hawai'i right to carry; DOH: do not recommend; TSA: not searched for, referred if discovered, officer's final decision; ≤0.3% / FDA-approved exception as written on TSA page; Schedule III / TSA interaction Verify ≤0.3% dry-weight hemp product falls inside TSA stated exception as written; Hawai'i mg-cap products may exceed federal 0.4 mg/container cap after 11 Dec 2026 — Verify before travel after that date
Interisland (air or sea) Prohibited for patients/caregivers by any means (§329-122(f)); buy on destination island or grow on-island (in-state only); 1-gram lab route interisland scope Verify Raw botanical hemp material: HP-1 report ≥3 business days before inter-island movement; processed hemp products: no HP-1 report required on DAB page as written
Dispensary → dispensary Only lab-testing (if no lab on island/county) + §329D-6(r) purchases (800 oz/30 days, department-authorised); interisland wholesale scope Verify; never to/from/within federal property (§329D-6(p)) Processor → processor / out-of-state sale under HAR §11-37-6 (≤0.3% concentration, destination law + testing/labelling) — federal 0.4 mg tension after 11 Dec 2026, flagged not resolved
Military base / federal land (incl. national parks) No card protection; UCMJ Art. 112a for service members; federal possession law (21 U.S.C. §844) for civilians/dependents/visitors; §329D-6(p) bars dispensary transport there Service-member hemp/CBD ban as stated on DoD prevention page (narrow exceptions only); post-Schedule III DoD position Verify; civilian hemp on federal land — no general permission located, Verify
Cruise ship Banned by every major line, including medical; confiscation, removal, denial of boarding, future-sailing ban possible; flag-state + port-state + §7 jurisdiction layers Several major lines ban CBD/hemp products outright as contract policy, even if federally lawful hemp on land — check current line list
International Do not cross border with marijuana/products (CBP); foreign law governs abroad, often severe; no 329V route for foreign visitors Do not assume US hemp/CBD legality travels; destination may ban all cannabis derivatives — check destination law
